Chapter I
Introduction

A subset
P
of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
may be considered a ‘problem’ whose ‘solutions’ are its elements, as in the problems “Find a completion of
\mathsf{PA}
” or “Find a
1
-random infinite binary sequence”, corresponding to the subsets
\mathrm{CPA}
and
\mathsf{MLR}
, respectively. In this context, we call
P
a
mass problem
. To compare the ‘degree of unsolvability’ of two mass problems
P
and
Q
, one approach is to use weak reducibility, where
P\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}Q
if and only if every member of
Q
computes a member of
P
.

Two well-studied hierarchies of mass problems are the complexity and diagonally non-recursive hierarchies. The former consists of the sets

\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)\coloneq\{X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\text{$\pfc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\geq f(n)-O(1)$ for all $n$}\}

where
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to[0,\infty)
is an unbounded, nondecreasing, computable function (an
order function
) and
\pfc
is prefix-free Kolmogorov complexity. In other words,
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)
consists of all infinite binary sequences whose first
n
bits cannot be described with less than
f(n)
bits of information, up to addition of a constant. The latter hierarchy consists of the sets

\mathrm{DNR}(p)\coloneq\{X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\text{$X(n)\nsimeq\varphi_{n}(n)$ and $X(n)<p(n)$ for all $n$}\}

where
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is a nondecreasing, computable function and
\varphi_{n}
is the
n
-th
1
-place partial recursive function. In other words,
\mathrm{DNR}(p)
consists of all
p
-bounded infinite sequences which avoid the diagonal of a fixed enumeration of the
1
-place partial recursive functions.

Although the two hierarchies are quite different in presentation, the connections between them have been widely studied. Among them is a result of Kjos-Hanssen, Merkle, & Stephan
[
17
, Theorem 2.3]
which shows that the complexity and diagonally non-recursive hierarchies are tightly coupled when going downward:

Theorem
.

[
17
, Theorem 2.3]

Suppose
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
. Then the following are equivalent.

1.

X\in\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)
for some order function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
.

2.

There exists an order function
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
and a
Y\in\mathrm{DNR}(p)
such that
Y
is computable from
X
.

Two other connections were proven by Greenberg & Miller
[
9
]
, relating the diagonally non-recursive hierarchy to the upper levels of the complexity hierarchy. The former says that regardless of how slow-growing an order function
p
is, there is an
X\in\mathrm{DNR}(p)
which cannot compute a maximally complex infinite binary sequence (an element of
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\id_{\mathbb{N}})
), while the latter gives an upshot that if
p
is sufficiently slow-growing then any
X\in\mathrm{DNR}(p)
computes highly complex infinite binary sequences.

Theorem
.

[
9
, Theorem 5.11]

If
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is an order function, then there exists
X\in\mathrm{DNR}(p)
such that
X
computes no member of
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.n)
.

Theorem
.

[
9
, Theorem 4.9]

For all sufficiently slow-growing order functions
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
, every
X\in\mathrm{DNR}(p)
computes a member of
\bigcap_{0\leq\delta<1}{\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.\delta n)}
.

In
[
29
]
, Simpson introduced a variation of
\mathrm{DNR}
,
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
(
L
inearly
U
niversal
A
voidance)
1
1

1

The notation used by Simpson in
[
29
]
was
\mathrm{LDNR}
, standing for
L
inearly
D
iagonally
N
on-
R
ecursive.
, to remove its dependence on any specific choice of enumeration of the partial recursive functions as well as to more closely tie the growth rate of
p
to the degree of unsolvability of the class
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
. For any order function
p
there are order functions
p^{+}
and
p^{-}
such that any
X\in\mathrm{DNR}(p^{+})
computes a member of
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
and any
Y\in\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
computes a member of
\mathrm{DNR}(p^{-})
, so all of the aforementioned results linking the complexity and diagonally non-recursive hierarchies translate to the
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchy.

An observation made by Bienvenu & Porter
[
2
]
, Greenberg, Miller
[
19
]
, and Slaman is that the behavior of
\mathrm{DNR}(p)
(for specific types of enumerations of the partial recursive functions) changes significantly depending on whether the series
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
converges (in which case
p
is called
fast-growing
) or diverges (in which case
p
is called
slow-growing
), and this observation applies to
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
as well
[
29
, Theorem 5.4]
. Thus, we may consider the
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchy as being made up of two sub-hierarchies, the
fast-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchy
(consisting of
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
for fast-growing
p
) and the
slow-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchy
(consisting of
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
for slow-growing
p
).

Within
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
(where our degrees of interest lie) there is a subregion in its upper reaches consisting of so-called ‘deep degrees’. The slow-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchy lies in this subregion, while both the fast-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
and complexity hierarchies lies in its complement. The notion of ‘shift complexity’ provides a randomness notion lying in that deep region, providing another way to study the connections between the slow-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchy and randomness/complexity notions.

Our goal is to explore the relationships between the complexity, fast-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
, shift complexity, and slow-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchies, expanding existing relationships and providing explicit bounds on the growth rates of the corresponding order functions.

I.1
Summary of Chapters

Each chapter is summarized below. Additionally, Figure
I.1
summarizes the main general reductions proven, Figure
I.2
summarizes specific examples of reductions, and Figures
I.3
and
I.4
collect references to the results, sections, and questions pertaining to each of the explored relationships between the hierarchies of interest. Figure
I.5
gives a visual representation of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
and how the hierarchies of interest sit within it.

Chapter

I

The remainder of this chapter covers notation, conventions, and terminology.
Section

I.2
covers basic notions, such as notation and terminology for number systems, set theoretic functions & relations, strings over a set, the Cantor & Baire spaces, and various encoding functions.
Section

I.3
gives a brief overview of the relevant notation and terminology from computability theory. Finally,
Section

I.4
briefly reviews the Turing, weak, & strong reducibility notions and the classes of mass problems we will be principally interested in.

Chapter

II

This chapter serves to introduce many of the main notions discussed within the remainder of the document. We start by giving a brief overview of partial randomness, reviewing the notation, terminology, and some basic results. Following that, we discuss
\mathrm{DNR}
and its dependence on a choice of an enumeration of the partial recursive functions, using its definition to motivate the definition of the class
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
for a recursive
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
and a partial recursive
\psi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
. After defining the family of linearly universal partial recursive functions, we define
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
, covering some of the basic reducibility results between those classes. The fast-growing, slow-growing dichotomy is examined, where we state and prove several technical results used later. Finally, we define depth and discuss the weak degrees of deep
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes, the basic structure of the region of deep degrees in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
and its relation to the fast-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
and slow-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchies.

Chapter

III

This chapter is centered around the relationships between the complexity and fast-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchies. One way in which we do this is by strengthening
[
17
, Theorem 2.3]
, addressing the problems “given
f
, find
q
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)
” and “given
p
, find
g
such that
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
” and giving explicit bounds for each. In particular, one of our main theorems is the following.

Theorem
III.0.5
.

To each sub-identical order function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
there is a fast-growing order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)
, and to each fast-growing order function
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
there is a sub-identical order function
g\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
such that
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
.

We also address the ‘upward’ problem “given
p
fast-growing, find sub-identical
g
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)
”, giving a partial answer.

Theorem
III.3.3
.

If
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is a fast-growing order function such that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real, then there exists a convex sub-identical order function
g
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)\neq\mathsf{MLR}
.

Chapter

IV

In this chapter we address the problem “given
f
, find
q
such that
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
”, giving a partial answer and providing explicit bounds for those cases. Our main results are the following.

Theorem
IV.4.10
.

Given an order function
\Delta\colon\mathbb{N}\to[0,\infty)
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}{\Delta(n)/\sqrt{n}}=0
and any rational
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,

\mathrm{COMPLEX}\bigl(\lambda n.n-\sqrt{n}\cdot\Delta(n)\bigr)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}\bigl(\lambda n.\exp_{2}\bigl((1-\varepsilon)\Delta(\log_{2}\log_{2}n)\bigr)\bigr).

More generally,
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.n-\sqrt{n}\cdot\Delta(n))\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
for any order function
q
satisfying

q\left(\exp_{2}((1-\varepsilon)^{-1}\cdot[(n+1)^{2}-(n+1)\cdot\Delta((n+1)^{2})]\cdot\ell(n))\right)\leq\ell(n)

for almost all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\ell(n)=\exp_{2}\left((1-\varepsilon)[(n+1)\cdot\Delta((n+1)^{2})-n\cdot\Delta(n^{2})]\right)
.

Chapter

V

In this chapter we examine classes of ‘shift complex’ sequences – in which the prefix-free complexity of all segments of a sequence
X
are quantified rather than only the initial segments – with respect to (non)negligibility and depth, as well as relationships with the complexity and
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchies. Our main results are the following.

Theorem
V.1.8
.

(Corollary of
V.1.7
)

Fix a rational
\varepsilon>0
. For all rational
\delta\in(0,1)
we have

\mathrm{SC}(\delta)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}\bigl(\lambda n.(\log_{2}n)^{1-\varepsilon}\bigr).

Theorem
V.3.12
.

Suppose
f
is a sub-identical order function such that
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}{f(2^{m})/2^{m}}
converges to a recursive real. Then there is an order function
g
such that
\mathrm{SC}(f)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)
and for which
\lim_{n\to\infty}{g(n)/n}=0
.

Chapter

VI

This chapter focuses on the relationships between the fast and slow-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
subhierarchies and their structures, with applications to the depth properties of the boundaries of the slow-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchy and relationships with the shift complexity hierarchy. Our main results are the following.

Theorem
VI.2.1
.

For all order functions
p_{1}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
and
p_{2}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
, there exists a slow-growing order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p_{1})\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p_{2})
.

In particular, for any order function
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
, there exists a slow-growing order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
and
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
are weakly incomparable.

Theorem
VI.4.1
.

\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}
is not of deep degree.

Theorem
VI.4.2
.

There is no order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
.

Theorem
VI.4.3
.

\mathrm{SC}\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}
.

Chapter

VII

Using results of the previous chapters, we further explore the structure of the region of deep degrees in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
and a larger region consisting of ‘pseudo-deep’ degrees in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
. Our main result is the following.

Theorem
VII.0.1
.

Define

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}\mid\text{$\mathbf{p}$ a deep degree}\}.

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}\mid\text{$\mathbf{p}=\inf\mathcal{C}$ for some $\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}$}\}.

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}\mid\forall P\in\mathbf{p}\forall X\in\mathsf{MLR}\,(\exists Y\in P\,(Y\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}X)\to(0^{\prime}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}X))\}.

Then
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}
is a principal filter while
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}
and
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}
are nonprincipal filters. Consequently,
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\subset$}\hss\cr\kern 0.86108pt\raise-2.15277pt\hbox{\scalebox{0.7}{$\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\hbox{\scalebox{1.4}{$-$}}\hss\cr\kern 0.86108pt\raise-0.43057pt\hbox{\rotatebox{45.0}{$-$}}\crcr}}}$}}\crcr}}}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\subset$}\hss\cr\kern 0.86108pt\raise-2.15277pt\hbox{\scalebox{0.7}{$\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\hbox{\scalebox{1.4}{$-$}}\hss\cr\kern 0.86108pt\raise-0.43057pt\hbox{\rotatebox{45.0}{$-$}}\crcr}}}$}}\crcr}}}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}
.

I.2
Basic Conventions and Notation

\mathbb{N}
is the set of natural numbers (including
0
).

\mathbb{Z}
is the set of integers.

\mathbb{Q}
is the set of rational numbers.

\mathbb{R}
is the set of real numbers.
Open, closed, and half-open intervals in
\mathbb{R}
are written
(a,b)
,
[a,b]
, and
{[a,b)},{(a,b]}
, respectively, for any
-\infty\leq a\leq b\leq\infty
.
If
\mathbb{F}
is any one of
\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{Q}
, or
\mathbb{R}
and
a\in\mathbb{F}
, then we write
\mathbb{F}_{\geq a}\coloneq\{x\in\mathbb{F}\mid a\leq x\}
and
\mathbb{F}_{>a}\coloneq\{x\in\mathbb{F}\mid a<x\}
.

n\bmod m
is the remainder after dividing
n
by
m
.
{}^{m}n
denotes the
m
-th tetration of
n
.
\log_{2}^{k}
denotes the composition of
k
-many base-
2
logarithms.
\lceil{-}\rceil
and
\lfloor{-}\rfloor
denote the ceiling and floor functions, respectively. When it would increase readibility, we write
\exp_{a}(n)=a^{n}
for
a>0
.

Set containment is denoted by
\subseteq
and proper containment is denoted by
\subset
or
\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\subset$}\hss\cr\kern 0.86108pt\raise-2.15277pt\hbox{\scalebox{0.7}{$\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\hbox{\scalebox{1.4}{$-$}}\hss\cr\kern 0.86108pt\raise-0.43057pt\hbox{\rotatebox{45.0}{$-$}}\crcr}}}$}}\crcr}}}
.
\mathcal{P}(S)
denotes the power set of
S
, while
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(S)
denotes the set of finite subsets of
S
.
B^{A}
denotes the set of all functions with domain
A
and codomain
B
.

Ordered
n
-tuples (or just ‘
n
-tuples’) are denoted using angled brackets, as in
\langle x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-1}\rangle
. We assume that if
\langle x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-1}\rangle=\langle y_{0},y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{m-1}\rangle
, then
n=m
. We sometimes identify an
n
-tuple
\langle x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-1}\rangle
with the set
\{\langle 0,x_{0}\rangle,\langle 1,x_{1}\rangle,\langle 2,x_{2}\rangle,\ldots,\langle n-1,x_{n-1}\rangle\}
and with the function
f\colon\{0,1,2,\ldots,n-1\}\to\{x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-1}\}
defined by
f(i)\coloneq x_{i}
for each
i\in\{0,1,2,\ldots,n-1\}
. More generally, we identify a function
f\colon A\to B
with its graph
\{\langle a,b\rangle\in A\times B\mid b=f(a)\}
.

Given a function
f\colon A\to B
, we write
\dom f=A
and
\cod f=B
. Given subsets
A_{0}\subseteq A
and
B_{0}\subseteq B
, we write
f[A_{0}]\coloneq\{f(a)\mid a\in A_{0}\}
and
f^{-1}[B_{0}]\coloneq\{a\in A\mid f(a)\in B_{0}\}
for the preimage of
B_{0}
.
\im f
is defined to be equal to
f[A]
.
f{\upharpoonright}\,A_{0}
denotes the restriction of
f
to
A_{0}
. If
\dom f=\mathbb{N}
, then we write
f{\upharpoonright}\,n
for the restriction
f{\upharpoonright}\,\{0,1,2,\ldots,n-1\}
.

If
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
is nondecreasing, we define
f^{\mathrm{inv}}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{N}
by
f^{\mathrm{inv}}(x)\coloneq\text{least $m$ such that $f(m)\geq x$}
.

Given functions
f,g\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
, we write
f\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}g
, read “
g

dominates

f
” or “
f
is
dominated by

g
” to mean that
f(n)\leq g(n)
for almost all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

A
partial function

f\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0muA\to B
is a function
f\colon A_{0}\to B
for some
A_{0}\subseteq A
. Given
a\in A
, then
f(a)
is said to be
defined
or to
converge
, written
f(a){\downarrow}
, if
a\in\dom f
, otherwise
f(a)
is said to be
undefined
or
diverge
, written
f(a){\uparrow}
. If
f\colon a\mapsto b
, we sometimes write
f(a){\downarrow}=b
. If
f
and
g
are partial functions
{\subseteq}A\to B
and
a\in A
, then we write
f(a)\simeq g(a)
to mean that either
a\in\dom f\cap\dom g
and
f(a)=g(a)
(i.e.,
f(a)
and
g(a)
both converge and are equal) or that
a\notin\dom f\cup\dom g
(i.e.,
f(a)
and
g(a)
both diverge).
f
is
total
if
\dom f=A
.

Given a set
S
, a
string over
S
– or simply a
string
if
S
is understood – is any element of
S^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
S^{\ast}
is the set of all strings over
S
, i.e.,
S^{\ast}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{S^{n}}
.
Given a string
\sigma\in S^{\ast}
, its
length

|\sigma|
is the unique
n\in\mathbb{N}
for which
\sigma\in S^{n}
.
Given
k<|\sigma|
,
\sigma(k)
is the
k
-th coordinate of
\sigma
, so that
\langle\sigma(0),\sigma(1),\ldots,\sigma(|\sigma|-1)\rangle=\sigma
.
If
\sigma=\langle s_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\rangle
and
\tau=\langle t_{0},t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}\rangle
are strings over
S
, their
concatenation

\sigma{}^{\frown}\tau
is given by

\sigma{}^{\frown}\tau=\langle s_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{n},t_{0},t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}\rangle.

Given
\sigma\in S^{\ast}
and
n\leq|\sigma|
,
\sigma{\upharpoonright}\,n
denotes the string
\langle\sigma(0),\sigma(1),\ldots,\sigma(n-1)\rangle
.
Given
\sigma,\tau\in S^{\ast}
, then
\sigma
is an
initial segment
of
\tau
(equivalently,
\tau
is an
extension
of
\sigma
) if
\sigma=\tau{\upharpoonright}\,|\sigma|
, written
\sigma\subseteq\tau
.
\sigma
is a
proper
initial segment of
\tau
(equivalently,
\tau
is a
proper
extension of
\sigma
) if
\sigma\subseteq\tau
and
\sigma\neq\tau
, written
\sigma\subset\tau
.
\sigma
and
\tau
are
compatible
if either
\sigma\subseteq\tau
and
\tau\subseteq\sigma
, otherwise they are
incompatible
.
A set of strings
A\subseteq S^{\ast}
is
prefix-free
if
\sigma\nsubseteq\tau
for all distinct elements
\sigma,\tau
in
A
.
If
\leq
is a partial order on
S
, then the
lexicographical ordering

\leq_{\mathrm{lex}}
on
S^{\ast}
is defined by setting
\sigma\leq_{\mathrm{lex}}\tau
if
\sigma\subseteq\tau
or
\sigma(k)<\tau(k)
for the least index
k
at which
\sigma(k)\neq\tau(k)
. The
shortlex ordering

\leq_{\mathrm{slex}}
on
S^{\ast}
is defined by setting
\sigma\leq_{\mathrm{slex}}\tau
if
|\sigma|<|\tau|
or if
|\sigma|=|\tau|
and
\sigma\leq_{\mathrm{lex}}\tau
, i.e., we order by length first, then lexicographically.

Suppose
\sigma\in S^{\ast}
and
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to S
are given.
\sigma
is an
initial segment
of
f
(equivalently,
f
is an
extension
of
\sigma
) if
f{\upharpoonright}\,|\sigma|=\sigma
.
\sigma
and
f
are
incompatible
if
\sigma\nsubset f
. Finally, we define
\sigma{}^{\frown}f\colon\mathbb{N}\to S
by

(\sigma{}^{\frown}f)(n)\coloneq\begin{cases}\sigma(n)&\text{if $n<|\sigma|$,}\\
f(n-|\sigma|)&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The
Baire space

\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
is endowed with the topology with basic open sets

\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket\coloneq\{f\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\sigma\subset f\}

for
\sigma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
.
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
, with this topology, is a non-empty zero-dimensional perfect polish space whose compact subsets have empty interior (with these properties characterizing
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
up to homeomorphism).

The
Cantor space

\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is endowed with the subspace topology coming from
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
. Alternatively, it has the topology with basic open sets

\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket_{2}\coloneq\{X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\sigma\subset X\}

for
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
.
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, with this topology, is a non-empty zero-dimensional compact perfect Polish space (with these properties characterizing
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
up to homeomorphism). We make the usual identification between elements of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
and subsets of
\mathbb{N}
. The
fair coin measure

\lambda
is the outer measure on
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
induced by the assignments
\lambda(\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket_{2})\coloneq\sum_{i=1}^{n}{2^{-|\sigma_{i}|}}
where
A=\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{n}\}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\ast}
is prefix-free.

Given
f_{0},f_{1},\ldots,f_{n-1}\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
, we define
f_{0}\oplus f_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus f_{n-1}\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
by

(f_{0}\oplus f_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus f_{n-1})(x)\coloneq f_{x\bmod n}(\lfloor x/n\rfloor).

E.g.,
(f_{0}\oplus f_{1})(2x)=f_{0}(x)
and
(f_{0}\oplus f_{1})(2x+1)=f_{1}(x)
. The assignment
\langle f_{0},f_{1},\ldots,f_{n-1}\rangle\mapsto f_{0}\oplus f_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus f_{n-1}
defines a homeomorphism
(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})^{n}\to\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
, and restricting to
(\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}})^{n}
also yields a homeomorphism
(\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}})^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

For
k\geq 2
, the functions
\pi^{(k)}\colon\mathbb{N}^{k}\to\mathbb{N}
denote the bijections defined recursively by

\displaystyle\pi^{(2)}(x,y)

\displaystyle\coloneq 2^{x}(2y+1)-1,

\displaystyle\pi^{(k+1)}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k},x_{k+1})

\displaystyle\coloneq\pi^{(2)}(\pi^{(k)}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}),x_{k+1}).

We additionally define
\pi^{(1)}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
and
\pi^{(0)}\colon\{\langle\rangle\}\to\mathbb{N}
by setting
\pi^{(1)}\coloneq\id_{\mathbb{N}}
and
\pi^{(0)}(\langle\rangle)\coloneq 0
.

We define a bijection
\str\colon\mathbb{N}\to\{0,1\}^{\ast}
by

\str(n)=\sigma\iff n+1=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}{\sigma(i)\cdot 2^{i}}+2^{k}.

Note that
n\leq m
if and only if
\str(n)\leq_{\mathrm{slex}}\str(m)
.

We define a bijection
\#_{\infty}\colon\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{N}
by setting

\#_{\infty}(\sigma)\coloneq\sum_{i=0}^{|\sigma|-1}{\exp_{2}(\sigma(0)+\sigma(1)+\cdots+\sigma(i)+i)}.

for each
\sigma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
.
Note that if
\sigma\subseteq\tau
, then
\#_{\infty}(\sigma)\leq\#_{\infty}(\tau)
.

If
\mathbb{D}
is an understood domain of discourse and
S\subseteq\mathbb{D}
, then the
characteristic function for
S
is the function
\chi_{S}\colon\mathbb{D}\to\{0,1\}
defined by

\chi_{S}(x)\coloneq\begin{cases}1&\text{if $x\in S$,}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

I.3
Computability - Definitions, Notation, and Conventions

Here we briefly review the definitions of recursiveness/computability for various objects. With the possible exception of notation given in
Section

I.2
and through the remaining chapters, we have attempted to adhere to standard notation and terminology whenever possible, so the reader is encouraged to consult any of the standard references (e.g.,
[
21
]
,
[
31
]
,
[
27
]
, etc.) for additional background.

I.3.1
(Partial) Recursive Functions and Sets

We define the collections of elementary, primitive, or partial recursive functions as the smallest collections of partial functions
{\subseteq}\mathbb{N}^{k}\to\mathbb{N}
closed under particular operations.

Definition I.3.1
.

•

The
initial functions
consist of the zero function
Z\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
(
\forall x\,(Z(x)\coloneq 0)
), the successor function
S\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
(
\forall x\,(S(x)\coloneq x+1)
), and for each
k\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
and
j\in\{0,1,\ldots,k-1\}
the projection
\pi_{j}^{k}\colon\mathbb{N}^{k}\to\mathbb{N}
(
\forall x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{k-1}\,(\pi_{j}^{k}(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{k-1})\coloneq x_{j})
).

•

Given
f

k
-ary and
g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{k}
each
n
-ary, their
generalized composition
is the
n
-ary function
h
where for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
we have

h(\mathbf{x})\coloneq f(g_{1}(\mathbf{x}),g_{2}(\mathbf{x}),\ldots,g_{k}(\mathbf{x})).

•

Given
f

(k+1)
-ary, its
bounded sum
and
bounded product
are the
k
-ary functions
g
and
h
, respectively, where for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
we have

g(n,\mathbf{x})\coloneq\sum_{i=0}^{n}{f(i,\mathbf{x})}\quad\text{and}\quad h(n,\mathbf{x})\coloneq\prod_{i=0}^{n}{f(i,\mathbf{x})}.

•

Given
f

(k+2)
-ary and
g

k
-ary, the result of
primitive recursion
applied to
f
and
g
is the
(k+1)
-ary function
h
defined recursively for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
by

\displaystyle h(0,\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\coloneq g(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle h(n+1,\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\coloneq f(n,h(n,\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{x}).

•

Given
f

(k+1)
-ary, its
minimization
is the
k
-ary function
g
where for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
we have

g(\mathbf{x})\coloneq\text{least $y$ such that $f(y,\mathbf{x})=1$}.

Definition I.3.2
.

The collection of …

\ldots

elementary recursive functions
is the smallest collection
\mathcal{C}
of total functions of the form
\mathbb{N}^{k}\to\mathbb{N}
containing the initial functions and the function
\dotminus\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
defined by
x\dotminus y\coloneq\max\{x-y,0\}
and closed under generalized composition and taking bounded sums and products.

\ldots

primitive recursive functions
is the smallest collection
\mathcal{C}
of total functions of the form
\mathbb{N}^{k}\to\mathbb{N}
containing the initial functions and closed under generalized composition and primitive recursion.

\ldots

partial recursive functions
is the smallest collection
\mathcal{C}
of partial functions of the form
{\subseteq}\mathbb{N}^{k}\to\mathbb{N}
containing the initial functions and closed under generalized composition, primitive recursion, and minimization of its total members.

\ldots

total recursive functions
is the collection of total partial recursive functions.

Remark I.3.3
.

There are many other characterizations of the above classes. Regarding the partial recursive functions (which are exactly the partial functions computed by Turing machine programs or by register machine programs) Church’s Thesis claims that any reasonable characterization of the ‘effectively computable’ partial functions is equivalent to being partial recursive.

The notion of recursiveness is extended to subsets of
\mathbb{N}^{k}
:

Definition I.3.4

(recursive predicate)
.

A predicate
S\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{k}
is
recursive
if its characteristic function
\chi_{S}\colon\mathbb{N}^{k}\to\{0,1\}
is recursive.

To extend the notion of partial recursiveness to partial functions whose domains or codomains are not
\mathbb{N}^{k}
for some
k
, we make use of Gödel numbers.

Definition I.3.5
.

Suppose
S
and
T
are countable sets
S
and
T
and then fix injections
\#_{S}\colon S\to\mathbb{N}
and
\#_{T}\colon T\to\mathbb{N}
for which
\im\#_{S}
and
\im\#_{T}
are both recursive subsets of
\mathbb{N}
, which we informally call
Gödel numberings
of
S
and
T
, respectively. Then a partial function
f\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0muS\to T
is
partial recursive
(with respect to
\#_{S}
and
\#_{T}
) if the partial function
\#_{T}\circ f\circ\#_{S}^{-1}\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is partial recursive.

Convention I.3.6
.

Unless stated otherwise, we assume the following Gödel numberings:

•

\mathbb{N}^{k}
is Gödel numbered by
\pi^{(k)}
.

•

\{0,1\}^{\ast}
is Gödel numbered by
\#_{2}\coloneq\str^{-1}
.

•

\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
is Gödel numbered by
\#_{\infty}
.

•

If
\#\colon S\to\mathbb{N}
is a Gödel numbering of
S
, then
S^{\ast}
is Gödel numbered by setting
\#(\langle s_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{k-1}\rangle)\coloneq\#_{\infty}(\langle\#(s_{0}),\#(s_{1}),\ldots,\#(s_{k-1})\rangle)
.

•

If
\#\colon S\to\mathbb{N}
is a Gödel numbering of
S
, then
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(S)
is Gödel numbered by setting
\#(T)\coloneq\\
\#(\langle s_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{k-1}\rangle)
, where
T=\{s_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{k-1}\}
and
\#(s_{0})<\#(s_{1})<\cdots<\#(s_{k-1})
.

•

\mathbb{Z}
is Gödel numbered by setting
\#_{\mathbb{Z}}(n)\coloneq 2n
if
n\geq 0
and
\#_{\mathbb{Z}}(n)\coloneq 2n+1
if
n<0
.

•

\mathbb{Q}
is Gödel numbered by setting
\#_{\mathbb{Q}}(r)\coloneq\pi^{(2)}(n,m)
, where if
r=p/q
with
\gcd(p,q)=1
and
q\geq 1
then
n=\#_{\mathbb{Z}}(p)
and
q
is the
m
-th positive integer coprime with
p
.

Remark I.3.7
.

Under any reasonable Gödel numberings, such as in
I.3.6
, relevant operations and relations on the Gödel numbered objects yield recursive operations and relations on their Gödel numbers. E.g., for strings (either in
\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
or
\{0,1\}^{\ast}
), this includes for operations the length and concatenation functions and for relations the the lexicographical ordering, the shortlex ordering, and
\subseteq
; for integers and rationals, this includes virtually all number theoretic operations, the standard total orderings, and the divisibility relation.

In some cases our choices of Gödel numberings are purposeful, as in the cases of
\pi^{(k)}
and
\#_{2}
. Unless otherwise stated, we have chosen particular Gödel numberings for the remaining cases solely for exactness, and in principle the reader may substitute them with their preferred encodings.

I.3.2
Enumerations of the Partial Recursive Functions

Thanks to their connection to effective algorithms, a partial recursive function
\theta\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{k}\to\mathbb{N}
can be described by a single natural number
e
which we may think of as encoding an algorithm computing
\theta
.

Definition I.3.8

(effective enumeration)
.

An
enumeration

\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\ldots
of the
k
-place partial recursive functions is any surjection from
\mathbb{N}
onto the set of all
k
-place partial recursive functions
\theta\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{k}\to\mathbb{N}
. Such an enumeration is
effective
if the partial function
\Phi(e,x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})\simeq\varphi_{e}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})
is partial recursive.

Convention I.3.9
.

Unless otherwise specified,
\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\ldots
will be an enumeration of the
1
-place partial recursive functions.
For concision,
\varphi_{\bullet}
will denote an enumeration
\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\ldots
.

Given an enumeration
\varphi_{\bullet}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
, we denote by
\varphi_{\bullet}^{(k)}
the enumeration of the
k
-place partial recursive functions defined by
\varphi_{e}^{(k)}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\simeq\varphi_{e}(\pi^{(k)}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}))
for all
e,x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
.

For many purposes, an enumeration
\varphi_{\bullet}
satisfying stronger properties than simply being effective is required. An example of such a property is that the
S^{m}_{n}
Theorem holds:

Property I.3.10

(
S^{m}_{n}
Theorem)
.

For all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a primitive
2
2

2

It is essential that
\pi^{(k)}\colon\mathbb{N}^{k}\to\mathbb{N}
be primitive recursive for each
k
so that the fulfillment of the
S^{m}_{n}
Theorem (particularly, the primitive recursiveness of
S^{m}_{n}
) does not depend on the choice of
\pi^{(k)}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

recursive function
S^{m}_{n}\colon\mathbb{N}^{m+1}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\varphi_{S^{m}_{n}(e,x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})}^{(n)}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\simeq\varphi_{e}^{(m+n)}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
for all
e,x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
.

A weaker version that often suffices for application (and does for our uses) is the following.

Property I.3.11

(Parametrization Theorem)
.

For any partial recursive function
\theta\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
, there exists a total recursive function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\varphi_{f(e)}(x)\simeq\theta(e,x)
for all
e,x\in\mathbb{N}
.

The Parametrization Theorem has further implications.

Proposition I.3.12
.

(well-known)

Suppose
\varphi_{\bullet}
is an effective enumeration for which the Parametrization Theorem holds.

1.

For all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a total recursive function
S^{m}_{n}\colon\mathbb{N}^{m+1}\to\mathbb{N}
such that

\varphi_{S^{m}_{n}(e,x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})}^{(n)}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\simeq\varphi_{e}^{(m+n)}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})

for all
e,x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
.

2.

Recursion Theorem:
For any partial recursive function
\theta\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{N}
there exists an
e\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\varphi_{e}^{(n)}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\simeq\theta(e,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
for all
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
.

The Parametrization
Theorem
is stated as a property of a enumeration rather than a feature of all effective enumerations due to the following observation.

Definition I.3.13

(admissible enumeration)
.

An enumeration
\varphi_{\bullet}
is
admissible
if it is effective and for which the Parametrization Theorem holds.

Proposition I.3.14
.

[
20
, following Definition 3]

There exists an effective enumeration
\varphi_{\bullet}
which is not admissible.

In
[
20
]
, Rogers examines the relationships between different enumerations of the partial recursive functions
3
3

3

Within
[
20
]
, Rogers uses the term ‘numbering’ where we use ‘enumeration’ and ‘semi-effective’ where we use ‘effective’.
. There, an enumeration is defined as a surjection
\rho
from a recursive subset
D_{\rho}
of
\mathbb{N}
onto the set of all
1
-place partial recursive functions. ‘Effectiveness’ is defined as above, and the only modification necessary for ‘admissibility’ is that the total recursive function
f
in the statement of the Parametrization Theorem take values in
D_{\rho}
.

Definition I.3.15
.

Suppose
\rho
and
\tau
are enumerations. We write
\rho\preceq\tau
if there is a recursive function
g\colon D_{\rho}\to D_{\tau}
such that
\rho=\tau\circ g
.

Lemma I.3.16
.

\preceq
is a preorder on the set of all effective enumerations.

Proposition I.3.17
.

[
30
, Exercise 5.10]

Suppose
\tau
is an admissible enumeration. Then
\tau
is admissible if and only if
\rho\preceq\tau
for any effective enumeration
\rho
.

Proof.

Suppose
\rho
is an effective enumeration. It suffices to show that if
\tau
is admissible, then

1.

\rho\preceq\tau
, and

2.

if
\tau\preceq\rho
, then
\rho
is admissible.

Suppose
\tau
is admissible.
\rho
is an effective enumeration, so the partial function
\Phi(e,x)\simeq\rho(e)(x)
is recursive. Because
\tau
is admissible, there exists a total recursive function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to D_{\tau}
such that
\tau(f(e))(x)\simeq\Phi(e,x)
for all
e,x\in\mathbb{N}
, or equivalently that
\tau\circ f=\rho
. Thus,
\rho\preceq\tau
.

Now suppose
\tau\preceq\rho
, so that there is a total recursive function
g\colon D_{\tau}\to D_{\rho}
such that
\tau=\rho\circ g
. Suppose
\theta\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
is any partial recursive function. Because
\tau
is admissible, there exists a total recursive function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\tau(f(e))(x)\simeq\theta(e,x)
for all
e,x\in\mathbb{N}
. But then
\rho((g\circ f)(e))(x)\simeq\theta(e,x)
for all
e,x\in\mathbb{N}
, showing
\rho
is admissible.
∎

Corollary I.3.18
.

Let
E
denote the set of effective enumerations, let
\cong
be the equivalence relation induced by
\preceq
(i.e.,
\rho\cong\tau
if and only if
\rho\preceq\tau
and
\tau\preceq\rho
), and let
\leq
be the partial order on
E/{\cong}
induced by
\preceq
(i.e.,
\rho/{\cong}\leq\tau/{\cong}
if and only if
\rho\preceq\tau
). Then the poset
(E/{\cong},\leq)
has a maximum, and that maximum is equal to the set of all admissible enumerations.

Remark I.3.19
.

All standard enumerations of the
1
-place partial recursive functions are admissible.

Convention I.3.20
.

Unless otherwise specified, any effective enumeration
\varphi_{\bullet}
used is assumed to be admissible. Given a partial recursive
\theta
, an
index for
\theta
is any
e\in\mathbb{N}
for which
\theta=\varphi_{e}
.

Many admissible enumerations have a natural way to interpret the statement, “
\varphi_{e}(x)
converges to
y
within
s
steps.”

Notation I.3.21
.

Given an admissible enumeration
\varphi_{\bullet}
of the
k
-place partial recursive functions, the notation
\varphi_{e,s}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})
is used to denote the output of a partial recursive function
\langle e,x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k},s\rangle\mapsto\varphi_{e,s}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})
satisfying the following properties:

1.

\varphi_{e}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}){\downarrow}
if and only if
\varphi_{e,s}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}){\downarrow}
for some
s\in\mathbb{N}
, in which case
\varphi_{e}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})=\varphi_{e,s}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})
.

2.

If
s<t
and
\varphi_{e,s}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}){\downarrow}
, then
\varphi_{e,t}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}){\downarrow}=\varphi_{e,s}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})
.

3.

The set
\{\langle e,x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k},s\rangle\mid\varphi_{e,s}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}){\downarrow}\}
is recursive.
4
4

4

In contrast,
\{\langle e,x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}\rangle\mid\varphi_{e}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}){\downarrow}\}
is nonrecursive and in fact
\{\pi^{(k+1)}(e,x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})\mid\varphi_{e}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}){\downarrow}\}
is many-one equivalent to the Halting Problem.

I.3.3
Partial Recursive Functionals

Although
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
cannot be Gödel numbered, reasoning about initial segments of sequences in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
allows us to talk about partial recursiveness for partial functions involving these spaces.

Proposition I.3.22
.

(well-known)

Suppose
\Psi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
is given. The following are equivalent.

1.

There exists a partial recursive function
\Gamma_{1}\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that

\forall\sigma\forall\sigma^{\prime}\forall x\forall y\,\bigl((\langle\sigma,x,y\rangle\in\Gamma_{1}\wedge\sigma\subseteq\sigma^{\prime})\implies\langle\sigma^{\prime},x,y\rangle\in\Gamma_{1}\bigr)

for which
\Psi(X)\simeq Y
if and only if
\forall x\exists n\,\langle X{\upharpoonright}\,n,x,Y(x)\rangle\in\Gamma_{1}
.

2.

There exists a partial recursive function
\Gamma_{2}\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that

\forall\sigma\forall\sigma^{\prime}\forall x\,\bigl((\langle\sigma,x\rangle\in\dom\Gamma_{2}\wedge\langle\sigma^{\prime},x\rangle\in\dom\Gamma_{2}\wedge\sigma\subseteq\sigma^{\prime})\implies\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}\bigr)

for which
\Psi(X)\simeq Y
if and only if
\forall x\exists x\,\langle X{\upharpoonright}\,n,x,Y(x)\rangle\in\Gamma_{2}
.

3.

There exists a partial recursive function
\Gamma_{3}\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
such that

\forall\sigma\forall\sigma^{\prime}\forall\tau\forall\tau^{\prime}\,\bigl((\langle\sigma,\tau\rangle\in\Gamma_{3}\wedge\langle\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}\rangle\in\Gamma_{3}\wedge\sigma\subseteq\sigma^{\prime})\implies(\tau\subseteq\tau^{\prime}\vee\tau^{\prime}\subseteq\tau)\bigr)

for which
\Psi(X)\simeq Y
if and only if
Y\simeq\bigcup\{\tau\mid\exists\sigma\subset X\,(\langle\sigma,\tau\rangle\in\Gamma_{3})\}
.

Definition I.3.23

(partial recursive functional)
.

A partial functional
\Psi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
is
partial recursive
if any (equivalently, all) of the conditions in
I.3.22
hold.

Notation I.3.24
.

We will implicitly identify a partial recursive functional
\Psi
with a partial recursive function
\Gamma_{\Psi}\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
as in
I.3.22
(iii). We define:

\displaystyle\Psi^{X}

\displaystyle\coloneq\bigcup\{\tau\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\mid\exists\sigma\subset X\,(\langle\sigma,\tau\rangle\in\Gamma_{\Psi})\}

for
X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
,

\displaystyle\Psi^{-1}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\coloneq\{X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\Psi^{X}\supseteq\sigma\}=\left\llbracket\{\tau\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\mid\exists\sigma^{\prime}\supseteq\sigma\,(\langle\tau,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle\in\Gamma_{\Psi})\}\right\rrbracket

for
\sigma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
, and

\displaystyle\Psi^{-1}(S)

\displaystyle\coloneq\bigcup_{\sigma\in S}{\Psi^{-1}(\sigma)}

for
S\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
.

Note that
\Psi^{X}
is a member of
\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\cup\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
, and that
\Psi^{X}\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
if and only if
X\in\dom\Psi
, in which case
\Psi(X)=\Psi^{X}
.

For partial functions of the form
\Psi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})^{k}\times\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\to(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})^{m}\times\mathbb{N}^{n}
(
k,m\geq 1
) to be partial recursive, we may either make appropriate adjustments to the above definitions, or reduce to the case
{\subseteq}\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
by associating a tuple
\langle X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{k-1},x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1}\rangle
with
\langle x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1}\rangle{}^{\frown}(X_{0}\oplus X_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus X_{k-1})
.

I.3.4
The Arithmetical Hierarchy

The arithmetical hierarchy provides one stratification of the complexity of subsets of
(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})^{k}\times\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
, and although we will only be interested in very low levels of that hierarchy, it is more convenient to define the general notion than those individual levels separately.

Definition I.3.25

(arithmetical hierarchy)
.

A subset
S\subseteq(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})^{k}\times\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
is
\Sigma^{0}_{0}
and
\Pi^{0}_{0}
if
S
is recursive. Given
\Sigma^{0}_{n}
and
\Pi^{0}_{n}
have been defined, we say that
S
is
\Sigma^{0}_{n+1}
if there exists a
\Pi^{0}_{n}
subset
R\subseteq(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})^{k}\times\mathbb{N}^{\ell+1}
such that

S(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k},y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell})\equiv\exists m\,R(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k},y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell},m).

for all
X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}
. Likewise,
S
is
\Pi^{0}_{n+1}
if there exists a
\Sigma^{0}_{n}
subset
R\subseteq(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})^{k}\times\mathbb{N}^{\ell+1}
such that

S(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k},y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell})\equiv\forall m\,R(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k},y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell},m)

for all
X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}
.

Other names are given to the lowest nontrivial level of the arithmetical hierarchy for subsets of
\mathbb{N}^{k}
.

Definition I.3.26

((co-)recursively enumerable)
.

A subset
X\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{k}
is
recursively enumerable
, or
r.e.
, if
X
is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
, and
co-recursively enumerable
, or
co-r.e.
, if
X
is
\Pi^{0}_{1}
.

Remark I.3.27
.

More generally, we can extend the use of ‘r.e.’ and ‘co-r.e.’ to sets of objects which are Gödel numbered. In particular, it makes sense to speak of a set of strings
S\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
being r.e. or co-r.e.

Often we are interested in sequences of sets each of which is at the same level of the arithmetical hierarchy in a uniform way.

Definition I.3.28

(uniformly
\Sigma^{0}_{n}
/
\Pi^{0}_{n}
)
.

A sequence
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}
of subsets
S_{i}\subseteq(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})^{k}\times\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
is
uniformly
\Sigma^{0}_{n}
(respectively,
uniformly
\Pi^{0}_{n}
) if it is
\Sigma^{0}_{n}
(resp.,
\Pi^{0}_{n}
) when considered as a subset of
(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})^{k}\times\mathbb{N}^{\ell+1}
.

I.3.5
Recursive Reals and Real-Valued Functions

Like
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
, although
\mathbb{R}
cannot be Gödel numbered, we can reason about real-valued functions by approximating real outputs by recursive sequences of rational numbers.

Definition I.3.29

(recursive/computable real)
.

\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
is
left recursively enumerable
, or
left r.e.
, if there is a recursive sequence
\langle p_{n}\rangle_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of rational numbers converging monotonically to
\alpha
from below.

\alpha
is
right recursively enumerable
, or
right r.e.
, if there is a recursive sequence
\langle q_{n}\rangle_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of rational numbers converging monotonically to
\alpha
from above.

If
\alpha
is both left r.e. and right r.e., then
\alpha
is
recursive
or
computable
.

Lemma I.3.30
.

(well-known)

Suppose
\alpha
is a real number.

1.

\alpha
is recursive if and only if there exists a monotone recursive sequence
\langle\alpha_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of rational numbers such that
|\alpha-\alpha_{k}|\leq 2^{-k}
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

2.

\alpha
is left r.e. if and only if there exists a sequence
\langle\alpha_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of uniformly recursive reals
\alpha_{k}\leq\alpha
converging to
r
.

3.

\alpha
is right r.e. if and only if there exists a sequence
\langle\alpha_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of uniformly recursive reals
\alpha_{k}\geq\alpha
converging to
\alpha
.

Proof.

Straight-forward.
∎

Definition I.3.31

(recursive/computable real-valued function of a discrete variable)
.

f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
is
left recursively enumerable
, or
left r.e.
, if there is a recursive function
p_{-,-}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{Q}
such that for each
x\in\mathbb{N}
, the sequence
\langle p_{n,x}\rangle_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges monotonically to
f(x)
from below.

f
is
right recursively enumerable
, or
right r.e.
, if there is a recursive funciton
q_{-,-}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{Q}
such that for each
x\in\mathbb{N}
, the sequence
\langle q_{n,x}\rangle_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges monotonically to
f(x)
from above.

If
f
is both left r.e. and right r.e., then
f
is
recursive
or
computable
.

Remark I.3.32
.

In the definitions of left r.e., right r.e., and recursive functions
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
, we may replace
\mathbb{N}
with any set
S
which can be Gödel numbered, associating a function
g\colon S\to\mathbb{R}
with the function
g\circ\#^{-1}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
, where
\#\colon S\to\mathbb{N}
is a Gödel numbering.

One of the principal ways in which recursive real-valued functions appear is to quantify growth rate.

Definition I.3.33

(order function)
.

An
order function
is a function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
which is nondecreasing, unbounded, and computable.

Now we describe what it means for a real-valued function of a real variable to be computable.

Definition I.3.34

(recursive/computable real-valued function of a real variable)
.

A function
\chi\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{Q}

represents

x\in\mathbb{R}
if
|\chi(n)-x|\leq 2^{-n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Suppose
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
is an interval. A function
f\colon I\to\mathbb{R}
is
recursive
or
computable
if there exists a partial recursive functional
\Psi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{Q}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{Q}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that whenever
\chi\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{Q}
represents
x\in I
and
\im\chi\subseteq I
,
\Psi(\chi){\downarrow}
and
\Psi(\chi)
represents
f(x)
.

Remark I.3.35
.

By ‘interval’ we include both the bounded intervals
{(a,b)}
,
{[a,b)}
,
{(a,b]}
,
[a,b]
, and the unbounded intervals
(-\infty,\infty)
,
{[a,\infty)}
,
{(-\infty,b]}
,
(a,\infty)
,
(-\infty,b)
.

We will assume the following basic facts concerning computable real-valued functions of a real variable.

Proposition I.3.36
.

(well-known)

1.

The functions
x\mapsto\alpha
,
x\mapsto x^{\beta}
,
x\mapsto\log_{2}x
,
x\mapsto 2^{x}
, and
x\mapsto\lfloor x\rfloor
are computable, where each function has its natural domain and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
are computable.

2.

If
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
is computable, then the piecewise-linear extension
\overline{f}\colon{[0,\infty)}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
\overline{f}(x)\coloneq(f(\lfloor x\rfloor+1)-f(\lfloor x\rfloor))(x-\lfloor x\rfloor)+f(\lfloor x\rfloor)
for each
x\in{[0,\infty)}
is computable.

3.

If
f\colon{[0,\infty)}\to\mathbb{R}
is computable, then
f{\upharpoonright}\,\mathbb{N}
is computable.

4.

If
f\colon I\to\mathbb{R}
is computable and
J
is an interval whose endpoints are either infinite or finite, computable reals, then
f{\upharpoonright}\,(I\cap J)
is computable.

5.

If
f,g\colon I\to\mathbb{R}
are computable, then
f+g
and
f\cdot g
are computable.

6.

If
f\colon I\to\mathbb{R}
and
g\colon J\to\mathbb{R}
are computable and
\im f\subseteq J
, then their composition
g\circ f
is computable.

Proof.

Cumbersome but routine.
∎

I.4
Reducibility Notions

I.4.1
Turing Reducibility

The principal way to measure the ‘degree of unsolvability’ of an infinite sequence
X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
is Turing reducibility.

Theorem I.4.1
.

(well-known)

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
. The following are equivalent.

1.

X
is a member of the smallest collection of partial functions
{\subseteq}\mathbb{N}^{k}\to\mathbb{N}
containing the initial functions, containing
Y
, and closed under generalized composition, primitive recursion, and minimization.

2.

There exists an oracle Turing machine which computes
X
given an oracle for
Y
.

3.

There exists an oracle register machine program which computes
X
given an oracle for
Y
.

4.

There exists a recursive functional
\Psi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\Psi(Y){\downarrow}=X
.

Definition I.4.2

(Turing reducibility)
.

Given
X,Y\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
, we say that
X
is
Turing reducible
to
Y
, written
X\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}Y
, if any of the equivalent conditions in
I.4.1
hold. We may also say that
X
is
Y
-computable
,
Y
-recursive
, or that
Y
computes
X
.

If
X\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}Y
and
Y\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}X
, then we say that
X
and
Y
are
Turing equivalent
and write
X\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{T}}}Y
.

\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}
is a preorder, and hence
\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{T}}}
is an equivalence relation.

Definition I.4.3

(Turing degree)
.

The
Turing degree
of an infinite sequence
X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
is the
\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{T}}}
-equivalence class containing
X
, written
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{T}}}(X)
.

The collection of all Turing degrees is written
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{T}}
.
\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}
induces a partial order
\leq
on
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{T}}
defined by setting
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{T}}}(X)\leq\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{T}}}(Y)
if and only if
X\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}Y
.

Example I.4.4
.

\mathbf{0}
is the Turing degree of any recursive
X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
. It is the minimum of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{T}}
.

Example I.4.5
.

Consider the following subsets of
\mathbb{N}
, which we tacitly identify with their characteristic functions:

\displaystyle H_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{e\in\mathbb{N}\mid\varphi_{e}(0){\downarrow}\},

\displaystyle H_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{e\in\mathbb{N}\mid\varphi_{e}(e){\downarrow}\},

\displaystyle H_{3}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{e\in\mathbb{N}\mid\text{$\varphi_{i}(n){\downarrow}$, where $\pi^{(2)}(i,n)=e$}\}.

All three Turing equivalent to one another. Firstly,
H_{1}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}H_{3}
because
e\in H_{1}
if and only if
\pi^{(2)}(e,0)\in H_{3}
; likewise,
H_{2}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}H_{3}
because
e\in H_{1}
if and only if
\pi^{(2)}(e,e)\in H_{3}
. For the opposite direction, consider the partial recursive function
\theta(\pi^{(2)}(i,n),m)\simeq\varphi_{i}(n)
; the Parametrization Theorem yields a total recursive function
f\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\varphi_{f(e)}(m)\simeq\theta(e,m)
for all
e,m\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

e\in H_{3}\iff f(e)\in H_{1}\iff f(e)\in H_{2},

showing
H_{3}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}H_{1}
and
H_{3}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}H_{2}
.
H_{3}
is the Halting problem, though thanks to the above equivalences we may also refer to
H_{1}
or
H_{2}
as the Halting problem. The Turing degree of
H_{1}\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{T}}}H_{2}\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{T}}}H_{3}
is called
\mathbf{0}^{\prime}
and
X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
is said to be
complete
if
\mathbf{0}^{\prime}\leq\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{T}}}(X)
.

Some simple facts about Turing degrees include the following:

Lemma I.4.6
.

Suppose
X,Y\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
are given.

1.

\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{T}}}(X\oplus Y)=\sup\{\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{T}}}(X),\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{T}}}(Y)\}
. Consequently,
(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{T}},\leq)
is a join semi-lattice.

2.

There exists
Z\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
X\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{T}}}Z
.

Proof.

Straight-forward.
∎

In analogy with admissible enumerations of the
k
-place partial recursive functions we may also consider admissible enumerations of the
k
-place partial recursive functionals
{\subseteq}\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\times\mathbb{N}^{k}\to\mathbb{N}
.

Definition I.4.7

(admissible enumeration of partial recursive functionals)
.

An
admissible enumeration
of the
k
-place partial recursive functionals
{\subseteq}\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\times\mathbb{N}^{k}\to\mathbb{N}
is a sequence
\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2},\ldots
of such functionals such that:

1.

For every partial recursive functional
\Psi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\times\mathbb{N}^{k}\to\mathbb{N}
there is
e\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\Psi=\Phi_{e}
.

2.

The partial functional
\Phi(e,X,x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})\simeq\Phi_{e}(X,x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})
is partial recursive.

3.

For any partial recursive functional
\Theta\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\times\mathbb{N}^{k+1}\to\mathbb{N}
there exists a total recursive
g\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that, for all
f\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
e,x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
,

\Phi_{g(e)}(f,x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})\simeq\Theta(f,e,x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}).

Notation I.4.8
.

The notation
\langle X,e,x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}\rangle\mapsto\varphi_{e}^{X}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})
will often be used to denote an (admissible) enumeration of the
k
-place partial recursive functionals
{\subseteq}\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\times\mathbb{N}^{k}\to\mathbb{N}
.

The notation
\varphi_{e,s}^{\tau}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})
is used to denote the output of a partial recursive function

\langle e,x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k},s,\tau\rangle\mapsto\varphi_{e,s}^{\tau}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})
satisfying the following properties:

1.

For every
X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
,
\varphi_{e}^{X}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}){\downarrow}
if and only if
\varphi_{e,s}^{X{\upharpoonright}\,s}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}){\downarrow}
for some
s\in\mathbb{N}
, in which case
\varphi_{e}^{X}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})=\varphi_{e,s}^{X{\upharpoonright}\,s}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})
.

2.

If
\sigma\subseteq\tau
,
s\leq t
, and
\varphi_{e,s}^{\sigma}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}){\downarrow}
, then
\varphi_{e,t}^{\tau}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}){\downarrow}=\varphi_{e,s}^{\sigma}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})
.

3.

The set
\{\langle e,x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k},s,\tau\rangle\mid\varphi_{e,s}^{\tau}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}){\downarrow}\}
is recursive.

I.4.2
Mass Problems and Weak, Strong Reducibility

When measuring the ‘degree of unsolvability’ of a
subset
of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
there are two notions of reducibility we consider.

Definition I.4.9

(weak reducibility)
.

Given
P,Q\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
,
P
is
weakly reducible

to
Q
, written
P\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}Q
, if for every
Y\in Q
there exists
X\in Q
such that
X\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}Y
.

If
P\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}Q
and
Q\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}P
, then
P
and
Q
are
weakly equivalent

, written
P\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}Q
.

Definition I.4.10

(strong reducibility)
.

Given
P,Q\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
,
P
is
strongly reducible

to
Q
, written
P\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}Q
, if there exists a recursive functional
\Psi\colon Q\to P
.

If
P\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}Q
and
Q\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}P
, then
P
and
Q
are
strongly equivalent

, written
P\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{s}}}Q
.

Remark I.4.11
.

Weak reducibility is also called
Muchnik reducibility
, and strong reducibility is also called
Medvedev reducibility
.

An interpretation of a subset
P\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
is as a
problem
(and within this context subsets of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
are sometimes called
mass problems
) whose elements are its
solutions
. If
P\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}Q
, then any solution to
Q
computes a solution to
P
, though this is not necessarily a uniform procedure. If
P\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}Q
, then there is a uniform procedure to turn a solution to
Q
into a solution to
P
. See
[
25
]
and
[
24
]
for additional information and motivation.

As with Turing reducibility, both weak and strong reducibility are preorders on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
and
\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}
and
\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{s}}}
define equivalence relations on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}})
.

Definition I.4.12

(weak and strong degrees)
.

Suppose
P\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
. The
weak degree
of
P
,
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P)
, is the
\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}
-equivalence class containing
P
, and the
strong degree
of
P
,
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{s}}}(P)
, is the
\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{s}}}
-equivalence class containing
P
.

The set of all weak degrees is denoted by
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{w}}
, while the set of all strong degrees is denoted by
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{s}}
.

Here we collect some simple facts about
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{w}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{s}}
that we use repeatedly and often implicitly.

Proposition I.4.13
.

(well-known)

Let
P
and
Q
be mass problems and let
\mathcal{Q}
a family of mass problems.

1.

P\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}Q
implies
P\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}Q
.

2.

Q\subseteq P
implies
P\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}Q
.

3.

P\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}P^{\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}}
, where
P^{\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}}\coloneq\{Y\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\exists X\in P\,(X\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}Y)\}
is the
Turing upward closure
of
P
.

4.

\inf\{\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P)\mid P\in\mathcal{Q}\}=\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}\left(\bigcup\mathcal{Q}\right)
.

5.

\sup\{\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P)\mid P\in\mathcal{Q}\}=\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}\left(\bigcap\{P^{\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}}\mid P\in\mathcal{Q}\}\right)
.

6.

\langle\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{w}},\leq\rangle
is a completely distributive lattice.

7.

\inf\{\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{s}}}(P),\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{s}}}(Q)\}=\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{s}}}(P\times Q)
, where
P\times Q\coloneq\{X\oplus Y\mid X\in P\wedge Y\in Q\}
.

8.

\sup\{\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{s}}}(P),\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{s}}}(Q)\}=\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{s}}}(\{\langle 0\rangle{}^{\frown}X\mid X\in P\}\cup\{\langle 1\rangle{}^{\frown}Y\mid Y\in Q\})
.

9.

\mathrm{REC}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}P\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\emptyset
, where
\mathrm{REC}=\{X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\text{$X$ is recursive}\}
.

10.

\langle\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{s}},\leq\rangle
is a bounded distributive lattice.

11.

For all
X,Y\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
,
X\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}Y
if and only if
\{X\}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\{Y\}
, and if and only if
\{X\}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\{Y\}
. Consequently,
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{T}}}(X)\mapsto\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\{X\})
and
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{T}}}(X)\mapsto\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{s}}}(\{X\})
define embeddings of
\langle\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{T}},\leq\rangle
into
\langle\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{w}},\leq\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{s}},\leq\rangle
, respectively.

12.

P\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{REC}
if and only if
P\cap\mathrm{REC}\neq\emptyset
.

Examples of mass problems, weak reductions, and strong reductions will be encountered throughout the remainder of this thesis, though we give one example presently which is neither empty nor weakly or strongly equivalent to
\{f\}
for some
f\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Example I.4.14
.

Suppose
P\cap\mathrm{REC}=\emptyset
. Then
P\subseteq\mathrm{REC}^{\mathsf{c}}
, so
\mathrm{REC}^{\mathsf{c}}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}P
. It follows that
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{REC}^{\mathsf{c}})
and
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{s}}}(\mathrm{REC}^{\mathsf{c}})
are immediate successors of the minimum of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{w}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{s}}
, respectively.

That
\mathrm{REC}^{\mathsf{c}}\mathrel{\nequiv_{\mathrm{w}}}\{X\}
(and consequently
\mathrm{REC}^{\mathsf{c}}\mathrel{\nequiv_{\mathrm{s}}}\{X\}
) for any
X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
follows from the fact that there exist incomparable minimal nonrecursive Turing degrees.

I.4.3
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
and the Embedding Lemma

Particular attention is given to the weak degrees of nonempty
\Pi^{0}_{1}
subsets of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, which we call
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes
, serving a similar role in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{w}}
as the collection
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{T}}
of r.e. Turing degrees (i.e., Turing degrees of r.e. sequences) in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{T}}
.

Definition I.4.15
.

\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}\coloneq\{\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P)\mid\text{$P\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}$ is $\Pi^{0}_{1}$}\}
.

One motivation for
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
in comparison to
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{T}}
is that specific, natural examples of weak degrees in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
can be given while there are no known specific, natural r.e. degrees aside from
\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{0}^{\prime}
. See
[
25
]
and
[
24
]
for additional details.

Proposition I.4.16
.

Suppose
P\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is given. The following are equivalent.

1.

P
is
\Pi^{0}_{1}
.

2.

There exists a recursive tree
T\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\ast}
such that
P
is the set of paths through
T
.

3.

There exists
e\in\mathbb{N}
such that
P=\{X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\varphi_{e}^{X}(0){\uparrow}\}
.

Although our interests lie chiefly within
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
, the weak degrees we consider are often most naturally represented by mass problems which are not
\Pi^{0}_{1}
subsets of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
. The following result shows that this issue can be side-stepped as long as the mass problem is sufficiently low in the arithmetical hierarchy.

Proposition I.4.17

(Embedding Lemma)
.

[
27
, Lemma 17.1]

Suppose
P
is a nonempty
\Pi^{0}_{1}
subset of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
S
is a
\Sigma^{0}_{3}
subset of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
. Then there exists a nonempty
\Pi^{0}_{1}
subset
Q
of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
Q\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}P\cup S
.

One particular case which is especially well-behaved is with
recursively bounded
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes
.

Definition I.4.18
.

Suppose
h\colon\mathbb{N}\to(0,\infty)
is a computable function. We write

\displaystyle h^{n}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{\sigma\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\mid\forall i<n\,(\sigma(i)<h(i))\},

\displaystyle h^{\ast}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{\sigma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\mid\forall i<|\sigma|\,(\sigma(i)<h(i))\}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{h^{n}},

\displaystyle h^{\mathbb{N}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\forall i\,(X(i)<h(i))\}.

In other words,
h^{n}
is the set of
h
-bounded strings of length
n
,
h^{\ast}
is the set of all
h
-bounded strings, and
h^{\mathbb{N}}
is the set of
h
-bounded infinite sequences.

Lemma I.4.19
.

The subspace topology on
h^{\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
has a basis
\{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket_{h}\mid\sigma\in h^{\ast}\}
, where for
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
we define

\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket_{h}\coloneq\{X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\sigma\subset X\}.

Proof.

For all
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
,
h^{\mathbb{N}}\cap\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket_{h}
. If
\sigma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\setminus h^{\ast}
, then
h^{\mathbb{N}}\cap\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket=\emptyset
.
∎

Proposition I.4.20
.

(well-known)

h^{\mathbb{N}}
is recursively homeomorphic to
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

Define
\psi\colon h^{\ast}\to\{0,1\}^{\ast}
recursively as follows:
\psi(\langle\rangle)=\langle\rangle
and given
\psi(\sigma)
has been defined, let
\psi(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)\coloneq\psi(\sigma){}^{\frown}\langle 1\rangle^{i}{}^{\frown}\langle 0\rangle
for each
i<h(|\sigma|)-1
and
\psi(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle h(|\sigma|)-1\rangle)\coloneq\psi(\sigma){}^{\frown}\langle 1\rangle^{h(|\sigma|)-1}
. We make the following observations:

1.

for all
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in h^{\ast}
,
\sigma\subseteq\sigma^{\prime}
if and only if
\psi(\sigma)\subseteq\psi(\sigma^{\prime})
, and

2.

for all
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
,
\left\llbracket\psi(\sigma)\right\rrbracket_{2}=\bigcup_{i<h(|\sigma|)}{\left\llbracket\psi(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)\right\rrbracket_{2}}
.

Now define
\Psi\colon h^{\mathbb{N}}\to\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
by
\Psi(X)\coloneq\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\psi(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)}
. Observation (i) above implies
\Psi
is well-defined and injective, while observation (ii) implies
\Psi
is surjective. Given
\tau\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
,
\Psi^{-1}[\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket_{2}]=\bigcup\{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket_{h}\mid\psi(\sigma)\supseteq\tau\}
, showing
\Psi
is continuous. Conversely, given
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
,
\Psi[\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket_{h}]=\left\llbracket\psi(\sigma)\right\rrbracket_{2}
– that
\Psi[\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket_{h}]\subseteq\left\llbracket\psi(\sigma)\right\rrbracket_{2}
is immediate, while the reverse inclusion follows from observation (ii) above – and hence
\Psi
is an open map. Since
\Psi
is clearly recursive, it is a recursive homeomorphism.
∎

Definition I.4.21

(recursively bounded)
.

P\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
is
recursively bounded
, or
r.b.
, if there exists an recursive function
h\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
P\subseteq h^{\mathbb{N}}
.

In particular, a
recursively bounded
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class
, or a
r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class
, is a recursively bounded and
\Pi^{0}_{1}
subset of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proposition I.4.22
.

[
24
, Theorem4.7]

Suppose
P
is a r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class and that
\Psi\colon P\to\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
is a recursive functional.

1.

The image
\Psi[P]
is recursively bounded and
\Pi^{0}_{1}
.

2.

\Psi
extends to a total recursive functional
\tilde{\Psi}\colon\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Corollary I.4.23
.

Suppose
P
is a r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class. Then there exists a
\Pi^{0}_{1}
subset
Q
of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
which is recursively homeomorphic to
P
.

Proof.

Let
\Psi
be the recursive homeomorphism defined in the proof of
I.4.20
. Then
I.4.22
(a) shows that the image
Q
of
P
under
\Psi
is another
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class.
∎

Chapter II
Complexity, Avoidance, and Depth

In
Chapter

I
we gave brief definitions of
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)
and
\mathrm{DNR}(p)
for
f
an order function and
p
a nondecreasing computable function, as well as alluded to a variation on
\mathrm{DNR}
which we termed
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
.

In
Section

II.1
, we define Martin-Löf randomness through three paradigms as a precursor to their generalizations which give rise to
f
-randomness and strong
f
-randomness for any computable function
f\colon\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
, with Martin-Löf randomness corresponding to
(\lambda\sigma.|\sigma|)
-randomness. The classes
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)
are defined and shown to lie in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
. Finally, we list some of the properties of prefix-free and conditional prefix-free complexity that we will use later.

In
Section

II.2
, we show that the effect of the growth rate of
p
on
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{DNR}(p)
depends explicitly on the choice of admissible enumeration used, and motivate the definition of the class
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
for any computable
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
and partial recursive
\psi
. Linearly universal partial recursive functions are defined, followed by defining
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
as the union of the classes
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
as
\psi
ranges over those linearly universal partial recursive functions. Basic and technical results are covered for the linearly universal partial recursive functions,
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
, and
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
more generally.

In
Section

II.3
, we formally define the notion of being fast-growing and slow-growing for an order function and address the problem “Given a recursive sequence of fast-growing (resp., slow-growing) order functions
\langle p_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, find fast-growing (resp., slow-growing) order functions
q^{+}
and
q^{-}
such that
p_{k}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}q^{+}
and
q^{-}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}p_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.” Towards that end, for the slow-growing case we prove that such a
q^{-}
always exists and that a
q^{+}
exists with additional hypotheses on
\langle p_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
(
II.3.2
), but that a
q^{+}
need not exist in general (
Example

II.3.3
). On the other hand, for the fast-growing case we can prove that
q^{+}
always exists and that
q^{-}
exists with additional hypotheses on the
p_{k}
’s and the sequence
\langle p_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
(
II.3.5
).

To better understand the extra hypothesis of requiring
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
not only be finite but also recursive, we prove the following equivalence:

Proposition
II.3.10
.

Suppose
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(0,\infty)
is a fast-growing order function and let
\overline{p}\colon[0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
be any continuous nonincreasing extension of
p
. Then
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real if and only if
\int_{0}^{\infty}{\overline{p}(x)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x}
is a recursive real.

Section

II.4
introduces the notion of depth for r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes, of which our interest is based on strong general properties of deep r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes and the fact that the classes
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{p}
are deep exactly when
p
is slow-growing. Depth is shown to be well-behaved with respect to
\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}
while slightly less well-behaved for
\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}
. We end the section with a discussion of the applicability of ‘depth’ to subsets of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
which are not r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes.

II.1
Algorithmic Randomness and Complexity

Downey & Hirschfeldt identify three paradigms through which one can attempt to make precise the idea of ‘algorithmic randomness’ or ‘algorithmic complexity’.
[
6
, Chapter 6]

The measure-theoretic paradigm:

If
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is ‘random’, then it should pass all ‘statistical tests’ (e.g.,
X
should obey the Law of Large Numbers, the Law of the Iterated Logarithm, etc.). Any ‘statistical test’ should be such that the set of sequences failing that statistical test should be ‘effectively null’ (so
X
should not fall into any effectively null subset of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
).

The computational paradigm:

If
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is ‘random’, then the initial segments of
X
should be ‘maximally difficult’ to describe, in the sense that we should need to know roughly
n
bits of information in order to describe
X{\upharpoonright}\,n
.

The unpredictability paradigm:

If
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is ‘random’ and we imagine that each bit of
X
represents the result of a coin flip whose outcome we are betting on, then there shouldn’t be any strategy by which we make arbitrarily high earnings.

There are several ways to make precise the notion of randomness from any of these three paradigms (some of them inequivalent), and our interest will not solely be on ‘randomness’ but on notions of ‘partial randomness’. Quantifying ‘how random’ a partially random sequence is yields the complexity hierarchy.

II.1.1
Martin-Löf Randomness

Martin-Löf randomness is among the most standard ways to capture the notion of a sequence being algorithmically random, and our definitions of partial randomness will be generalizations of those for Martin-Löf randomness.

The first definition we give comes from the within the measure-theoretic paradigm.

Definition II.1.1

(Martin-Löf randomness)
.

A
Martin-Löf test
, or
ML test
, is a sequence
\langle U_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of uniformly
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
subsets of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\lambda(U_{i})\leq 2^{-i}
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Such an ML test
covers

X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
if
X\in\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{U_{i}}
.
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is
Martin-Löf random
(or
1
-random
) if no ML test covers
X
. The set of all Martin-Löf random sequences is denoted by
\mathsf{MLR}
.

The computational paradigm involves measuring the ‘complexity’ of initial segments of an
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
. There are two relevant notions of complexity, that of
prefix-free complexity
and
a priori complexity
.

First, prefix-free complexity:

Definition II.1.2

(prefix-free machine)
.

A
machine
is a partial recursive function
M\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to\{0,1\}^{\ast}
.
A machine
M
is
prefix-free
if
\dom M
is prefix-free.
A prefix-free machine
U
is said to be
universal
if whenever
M
is another prefix-free machine there is a
\rho\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
such that
U(\rho{}^{\frown}\tau)\simeq M(\tau)
for all
\tau\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
.

Lemma II.1.3
.

[
6
, Proposition 3.5.1.(ii)]

There exists a universal prefix-free machine.

Definition II.1.4

(prefix-free complexity)
.

Fix a universal prefix-free machine
U
. Given
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
, its
prefix-free complexity
(with respect to
U
) is defined by

\pfc(\sigma)=\pfc_{U}(\sigma)\coloneq\min\{|\tau|\mid U(\tau){\downarrow}=\sigma\}.

The second notion of complexity we use is that of a priori complexity, which has a more pronounced measure-theoretic leaning than that of prefix-free complexity:

Definition II.1.5

(continuous semimeasure)
.

A
continuous semimeasure
is a function
\nu\colon\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to[0,1]
such that
\nu(\langle\rangle)=1
and
\nu(\sigma)\geq\nu(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle 0\rangle)+\nu(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle 1\rangle)
for all
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
.
A continuous semimeasure
\nu
is
left recursively enumerable
, or
left r.e.
, if it is left r.e. in the usual sense.
A left r.e. continuous semimeasure
\mathbf{M}
is
universal
if whenever
\nu
is another left r.e. continuous semimeasure there is a
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\nu(\sigma)\leq c\cdot\mathbf{M}(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
.

Lemma II.1.6
.

[
6
, Theorem 3.16.2]

There exists a universal left r.e. continuous semimeasure.

Definition II.1.7

(a priori complexity)
.

Fix a universal left r.e. continuous semimeasure
\mathbf{M}
. Given
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
, its
a priori complexity
(with respect to
\mathbf{M}
) is defined by

\apc(\sigma)=\apc_{\mathbf{M}}(\sigma)\coloneq-\log_{2}\mathbf{M}(\sigma).

The complexity of an
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
can be quantified by the growth rate of the complexities of its initial segments.

Definition II.1.8

((strong)
1
-complexity)
.

X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is
1
-complex
if there exists
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\pfc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\geq n-c
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and is
strongly
1
-complex
if there exists
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\apc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\geq n-c
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

The predictability paradigm includes supermartingales as one way to capture a notion of betting.

Definition II.1.9

(supermartingale and success)
.

A
supermartingale
is a function
d\colon\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to[0,\infty)
such that
2d(\sigma)\geq d(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle 0\rangle)+d(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle 1\rangle)
for all
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
.
A supermartingale
d
is
left recursively enumerable
, or
left r.e.
, if it left r.e. in the usual sense.
A left r.e. supermartingale
d

succeeds on
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
if
\limsup_{n}{d(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)}=\infty
.

Each of these approaches (among several others) ultimately give the same notion of an
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
being ‘algorithmically random’.

Proposition II.1.10
.

[
6
, Chapter 6]

Suppose
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
. The following are equivalent.

1.

X
is Martin-Löf random.

2.

X
is
1
-complex.

3.

X
is strongly
1
-complex.

4.

No left r.e. supermartingale succeeds on
X
.

II.1.2
Partial Randomness

Even if
X\notin\mathsf{MLR}
,
X
may still exhibit some degree of randomness.
Partial
randomness can be approached from the measure-theoretic, computational, and predictability paradigms just as in the case of Martin-Löf randomness, though the resulting definitions need not be equivalent in general. Notions of ‘partial
f
-randomness’ can be motivated by interpreting Martin-Löf randomness as corresponding to the choice
f(\sigma)\coloneq|\sigma|
for
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
.

Notation II.1.11
.

Unless otherwise specified,
f
denotes a computable function
f\colon\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
.

Concerning the measure-theoretic paradigm, the map
\sigma\mapsto 2^{-f(\sigma)}
no longer induces a pre-measure on the algebra of basic open subsets of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
; given a
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
subset
U\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, it matters how
U
is expressed as a union of basic open sets. For this reason, our emphasis is on r.e. subsets of
\{0,1\}^{\ast}
instead of
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
subsets of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
. Moreover, we consider two distinct ways to capture the idea of the ‘
f
-weight’ of a subset of
\{0,1\}^{\ast}
.

Definition II.1.12

(direct and prefix-free
f
-weight)
.

The
direct
f
-weight
of a set of strings
S\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\ast}
is defined by

\dwt_{f}(S)\coloneq\sum_{\sigma\in S}{2^{-f(\sigma)}}.

Its
prefix-free
f
-weight
is defined by
