\pwt_{f}(S)\coloneq\sup\{\dwt_{f}(A)\mid\text{prefix-free $A\subseteq S$}\}.

Definition II.1.13

((strong)
f
-randomness)
.

Suppose
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of uniformly r.e. subsets of
\{0,1\}^{\ast}
.
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a
f
-ML test
if
\dwt_{f}(S_{i})\leq 2^{-i}
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and a
weak
f
-ML test
if
\pwt_{f}(S_{i})\leq 2^{-i}
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

covers

X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
if
X\in\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\left\llbracket S_{i}\right\rrbracket}
.

X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is
f
-random
if no
f
-ML test covers
X
and
strongly
f
-random
if no weak
f
-ML test covers
X
.

Replacing the map
\sigma\mapsto|\sigma|
with
f
quickly generalizes the notion of
1
-complexity and strong
1
-complexity:

Definition II.1.14

((strong)
f
-complexity)
.

X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is
f
-complex
if there exists
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\pfc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\geq f(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)-c
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and is
strongly
f
-complex
if there exists
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\apc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\geq f(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)-c
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

For supermartingales and success, we generalize the notion of success.

Definition II.1.15

(
f
-success)
.

A left r.e. supermartingale
d

f
-succeeds on
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
if

\limsup_{n}{\left(d(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\cdot 2^{n-f(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)}\right)}=\infty.

Unlike when
f(\sigma)\coloneq|\sigma|
, these notions are no longer all necessarily equivalent, instead forming two groups. In summary:

Proposition II.1.16
.

[
10
, Theorem 2.6, Theorem 2.8]

[
13
, Theorem 4.1.6, Theorem 4.1.8, Theorem 4.2.3]

Suppose
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
f\colon\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
is computable.

1.

X
is
f
-random if and only if it is
f
-complex.

2.

X
is strongly
f
-random if and only if it is strongly
f
-complex, and if and only if no left r.e. supermartingale
f
-succeeds on
X
.

Remark II.1.17
.

In
[
13
]
, Hudelson generalizes the supermartingale approach to partial randomness by modifying the definition of a supermartingale, defining a
left r.e.
f
-supermartingale
to be a left r.e. function
d\colon\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to[0,\infty)
such that

2^{-f(\sigma)}\cdot d(\sigma)\geq 2^{-f(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle 0\rangle)}\cdot d(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle 0\rangle)+2^{-f(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle 1\rangle)}\cdot d(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle 1\rangle)

for all
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
, with
d

succeeding on
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
if
\limsup_{n}{d(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)}=\infty
. For every
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists an
f
-supermartingale
d
succeeding on
X
if and only if there exists a supermartingale
\tilde{d}

f
-succeeding on
X
.

Our choice to use ordinary supermartingales and
f
-success follows the approaches used for
f
of the form
f(\sigma)\coloneq\delta\cdot|\sigma|
for
\delta\in(0,1]
in
[
5
]
and
[
9
]
.

Although
f
-randomness does not in general imply strong
f
-randomness (see, e.g.,
[
13
, Theorem 4.3.2]
), if
g
grows sufficiently faster than
f
, then
g
-randomness will imply strong
f
-randomness:

Proposition II.1.18
.

[
10
, Theorem 3.5]

Suppose
f,g\colon\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
are computable functions and
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is
g
-random. If there exists a nondecreasing
h\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{2^{-h(n)}}<\infty
and for which
g(\sigma)\geq f(\sigma)+h(f(\sigma))
for all
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
, then
X
is strongly
f
-random.

Corollary II.1.19
.

[
10
, Theorem 3.6]

Suppose
k>0
and
\varepsilon>0
, and let
g=f+\log_{2}f+\log_{2}\log_{2}f+\cdots+\log_{2}^{k-1}f+(1+\varepsilon)\log_{2}^{k}f
. Then any
g
-random
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is strongly
f
-random.

II.1.3
Randomness and Complexity as Mass Problems

For each computable
f\colon\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
there is an associated mass problem consisting of all
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
which are
f
-complex (equivalently,
f
-random). We use the following notation:

Definition II.1.20
.

Suppose
f\colon\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
is computable and
c\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f,c)

\displaystyle\coloneq\{X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\forall n\,(\pfc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\geq f(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)-c)\},

\displaystyle\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)

\displaystyle\coloneq\{X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\text{$X$ is $f$-complex}\}=\bigcup_{c=0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f,c)}.

Notation II.1.21
.

Given
\delta\in(0,1]
, define
f\colon\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
by
f(\sigma)\coloneq\delta|\sigma|
. We write
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\delta,c)
for
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f,c)
and
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\delta)
for
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)
.

Proposition II.1.22
.

The sets
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f,c)
are
\Pi^{0}_{1}
, uniform in
c
. Thus,
\mathrm{COMPLEX}_{f}
is
\Sigma^{0}_{2}
and consequently
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f))\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
whenever
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

Suppose
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
c\in\mathbb{N}
. Fix a universal prefix-free machine
U
and let
e
be such that
U(\tau)\simeq\sigma
if and only if
\varphi_{e}(\str^{-1}(\tau))\simeq\str^{-1}(\sigma)
. Then

X\in\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f,c)\iff\forall n\forall s\forall\tau\,(\varphi_{e,s}(\str^{-1}(\tau)){\downarrow}=\str^{-1}(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\to|\tau|\geq f(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)-c).

The uniformity of the above predicate shows that
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)
is
\Sigma^{0}_{2}
.

If
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)\neq\emptyset
, then
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f,c)\neq\emptyset
for some
c\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular,
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)
contains a nonempty
\Pi^{0}_{1}
subset of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
. Thus, the
Proposition I.4.17

(Embedding Lemma)
.
implies
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f))\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.
∎

Often
f(\sigma)
depends only on
|\sigma|
; such
f
are said to be
length-invariant
and correspond exactly with computable functions of the form
\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
. We extend our notation for
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)
to such functions.

Notation II.1.23
.

If
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
is a computable function and
c\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f,c)
stands for
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\tilde{f},c)
, where
\tilde{f}\colon\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by
\tilde{f}(\sigma)\coloneq f(|\sigma|)
for
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
.

Because we are often interested in
partial
randomness and in light of
II.4.12
, we often only consider
f
satisfying the following condition:

Definition II.1.24

(sub-identical)
.

A function
f\colon\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to[0,\infty)
is
sub-identical
if
\lim_{n\to\infty}{(n-f(X{\upharpoonright}\,n))}=\infty
for every
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Likewise, a function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to[0,\infty)
is
sub-identical
if
\lim_{n\to\infty}{(n-f(n))}=\infty
.

Thanks to the presence of
c
in the definition
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)=\bigcup_{c\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f,c)}
, if
f(n)=g(n)
for almost all
n
, then
\mathrm{COMPLEX}_{f}=\mathrm{COMPLEX}_{g}
.

Convention II.1.25
.

Given
f\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
, suppose
f{\upharpoonright}\,\mathbb{N}_{\geq a}
is a total, computable, nondecreasing, unbounded function for some
a\in\mathbb{N}
. Define
\tilde{f}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
by
\tilde{f}(n)\coloneq f(n)
for
n\geq a
and
\tilde{f}(n)\coloneq f(a)
otherwise. Then
\tilde{f}
is an order function, and we let
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)
denote the class
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\tilde{f})
. This allows us to make sense of something like, e.g.,
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\log_{2})
.

II.1.4
Properties of Prefix-Free Complexity

Here we collect some of the results concerning prefix-free complexity we make use of later. Several of these results involve
conditional
prefix-free complexity.

Definition II.1.26

(oracle prefix-free machine)
.

An
oracle prefix-free machine
is a partial recursive function
M\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\{0,1\}^{\ast}\times\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to\{0,1\}^{\ast}
such that

1.

if
M^{\tau}(\sigma){\downarrow}
and
\tau\subseteq\tau^{\prime}
, then
M^{\tau^{\prime}}(\sigma){\downarrow}=M^{\tau}(\sigma)
and

2.

\{\sigma\mid\langle\sigma,\tau\rangle\in\dom M\}
is prefix-free for every
\tau\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
.

An oracle prefix-free machine
U
is
universal
if for every oracle prefix-free machine
M
there exists
\rho\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
such that
U^{\tau}(\rho{}^{\frown}\sigma)\simeq M^{\tau}(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
.

Lemma II.1.27
.

[
6
, Section 3.2]

There exists a universal oracle prefix-free machine
U
.

Definition II.1.28

(conditional prefix-free complexity)
.

[
6
, Section 3.2]

Fix a universal oracle prefix-free machine
U
. Given
\sigma,\tau\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
, the
conditional prefix-free complexity of
\sigma
given
\tau
(with respect to
U
) is defined by

\pfc(\sigma\mid\tau)=\pfc_{U}(\sigma\mid\tau)\coloneq\min\{|\rho|\mid U^{\overline{\tau}}(\rho){\downarrow}=\sigma\},

where
\overline{\tau}=\langle\tau(0),\tau(0),\tau(1),\tau(1),\ldots,\tau(|\tau|-1),\tau(|\tau|-1),0,1\rangle
.

Notation II.1.29
.

Temporarily write
\vec{\sigma}\coloneq\str\bigl(\pi^{(k)}(\str^{-1}(\sigma_{1}),\str^{-1}(\sigma_{2}),\ldots,\str^{-1}(\sigma_{k}))\bigr)
for
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{k}\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
. Then given
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{k},\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\ldots,\tau_{m}\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
, we define

\displaystyle\pfc(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{k})

\displaystyle\coloneq\pfc(\vec{\sigma}),

\displaystyle\pfc(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{k}\mid\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\ldots,\tau_{m})

\displaystyle\coloneq\pfc(\vec{\sigma}\mid\vec{\tau}).

Notation II.1.30
.

Given functions
f,g\colon S\to\mathbb{R}
, we write
f\leq^{+}g
to mean that there exists
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
f(x)\leq g(x)+c
for all
x\in S
. We write
f=^{+}g
if
f\leq^{+}g
and
g\leq^{+}f
. By an abuse of notation, we may write
f(x)\leq^{+}g(x)
or
f(x)=^{+}g(x)
where
x
is an indeterminate to indicate that
f\leq^{+}g
or
f=^{+}g
, respectively.

Proposition II.1.31
.

1.

[
6
, Theorem 3.6.1 & Corollary 3.6.2]

Kraft-Chaitin: Suppose
(d_{i},\tau_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a recursive sequence of pairs
(d_{i},\tau_{i})\in\mathbb{N}\times\{0,1\}^{\ast}
such that
\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}{2^{-d_{i}}}\leq 1
. Then there exists a prefix-free machine
M
and strings
\sigma_{i}
such that
|\sigma_{i}|=d_{i}
and
M(\sigma_{i})=\tau_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
\dom M=\{\sigma_{i}\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}
. Consequently,
\pfc(\tau_{i})\leq^{+}d_{i}
.

2.

[
6
, Proposition 3.5.4]

If
f\colon\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to\{0,1\}^{\ast}
is computable, then
\pfc(f(\sigma))\leq^{+}\pfc(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
.

3.

[
6
, Corollary 3.7.5]

For any
k
and
\varepsilon>0
,
\pfc(\sigma)\leq^{+}|\sigma|+\log|\sigma|+\log\log|\sigma|+\cdots+(1+\varepsilon)\log^{k}|\sigma|
.

4.

[
6
, Proposition 3.7.13]

For all
\sigma,\tau\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
,
\pfc(\sigma{}^{\frown}\tau)\leq\pfc(\sigma,\tau)\leq^{+}\pfc(\sigma)+\pfc(\tau)
.

5.

[
6
, Theorem 3.10.2]

\pfc(\sigma,\tau)=^{+}\pfc(\sigma)+\pfc(\tau\mid\sigma,\pfc(\sigma))=^{+}\pfc(\sigma)+\pfc(\tau\mid\sigma^{\ast})
, where
\sigma^{\ast}
is the lexicographically least
\tau
such that
U(\tau){\downarrow}=\sigma
in
s
stages for the least possible
s
.

Corollary II.1.32
.

Suppose
f\colon(\{0,1\}^{\ast})^{k}\to\{0,1\}^{\ast}
is computable. Then for all
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{k}
,

\pfc(f(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{k}))\leq^{+}\pfc(\sigma_{1})+\pfc(\sigma_{2})+\cdots+\pfc(\sigma_{k}).

II.2
\mathrm{DNR}
and Avoidance

The diagonally non-recursive (or
\mathrm{DNR}
) hierarchy consists of the sets
\mathrm{DNR}(p)
for
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
computable, where ‘diagonally non-recursive’ is with respect to a fixed admissible enumeration
\varphi_{\bullet}
.

Definition II.2.1

(diagonally non-recursive)
.

The set of
diagonally non-recursive
sequences,
\mathrm{DNR}\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
, is defined by
\mathrm{DNR}\coloneq\{f\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\forall n\,(f(n)\nsimeq\varphi_{n}(n))\}
. Additionally, given a recursive
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
, we define:

\mathrm{DNR}(p)\coloneq\{X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid X\in\mathrm{DNR}\wedge\forall n\,(X(n)<p(n))\}.

The weak degree of
\mathrm{DNR}(p)
has a dependence on the growth rate of
p
. At one extreme,
\mathbf{1}\coloneq\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{DNR}(2))
is the maximum weak degree in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}

[
27
, Theorem 14.6]
. As the growth rate of
p
rises the weak degree of
\mathrm{DNR}(p)
falls;
[
17
, Theorem 2.3]
shows that the class
\mathrm{DNR}_{\mathrm{rec}}\coloneq\bigcup\{\mathrm{DNR}(p)\mid\text{$p$ recursive}\}
is weakly equivalent to the class
\mathrm{COMPLEX}\coloneq\bigcup\{\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)\mid\text{$f$ recursive}\}
. Several results (e.g.,
[
9
]
,
[
15
]
) show that for certain properties of interest, every element of
\mathrm{DNR}(p)
computes an infinite sequence with that desired property if
p
is sufficiently slow-growing.

If we wish to replace ‘sufficiently slow-growing’ with explicit bounds, we quickly run into problems – the weak degree of
\mathrm{DNR}(p)
depends not only on
p
, but also on the particular admissible enumeration
\varphi_{\bullet}
used in the definition of
\mathrm{DNR}(p)
, a fact that has been observed in
[
24
, Remark 10.6]
,
[
2
, §7.3]
, and
[
19
]
. In particular, we can show:

Proposition II.2.2
.

There exist admissible enumerations
\varphi_{\bullet}
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{\bullet}
such that the weak degree of
\mathrm{DNR}(\lambda n.n)
differs when defined with respect to
\varphi_{\bullet}
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{\bullet}
.

Proof.

[
2
]
shows that there exist admissible enumerations
\varphi_{\bullet}
for which
\mathrm{DNR}(p)\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathsf{MLR}
(where
\mathrm{DNR}(p)
is defined with respect to that particular choice of enumeration
\varphi_{\bullet}
) if and only if
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}=\infty
.
1
1

1

This fact has also been independently observed by Greenberg, Miller
[
19
]
, and Slaman.
Let
\varphi_{\bullet}
be such an admissible enumeration.

We define a new admissible enumeration
\tilde{\varphi}_{\bullet}
. Given
x\in\mathbb{N}
, define

\tilde{\varphi}_{e}(x)\simeq\begin{cases}\varphi_{n}(x)&\text{if $e=2^{n}$,}\\
{\uparrow}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

It is straight-forward to check that
\tilde{\varphi}_{\bullet}
is an admissible enumeration.

Write
\mathrm{DNR}^{(1)}
to mean
\mathrm{DNR}
with respect to the enumeration
\varphi_{\bullet}
and
\mathrm{DNR}^{(2)}
to mean
\mathrm{DNR}
with respect to the enumeration
\tilde{\varphi}_{\bullet}
. Our assumption about
\varphi_{\bullet}
implies
\mathrm{DNR}^{(1)}(\lambda n.n)\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathsf{MLR}
while
\mathrm{DNR}^{(1)}(\lambda n.2^{n})\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathsf{MLR}
. However,
\mathrm{DNR}^{(2)}(\lambda n.n)\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{DNR}^{(1)}(\lambda n.2^{n})
and hence
\mathrm{DNR}^{(2)}(\lambda n.n)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathsf{MLR}
and so
\mathrm{DNR}^{(1)}(\lambda n.n)
and
\mathrm{DNR}^{(2)}(\lambda n.n)
are not weakly equivalent.
∎

With this dependence in mind, if we wish to discuss the weak degrees of the classes
\mathrm{DNR}_{p}
, then the notation ‘
\mathrm{DNR}
’ should be replaced with one explicitly acknowledging the choice of admissible enumeration implicit in ‘
\mathrm{DNR}
’. By definition
X\in\mathrm{DNR}
if and only if
X
avoids the diagonal
\psi(e)\simeq\varphi_{e}(e)
, i.e.,
X\cap\psi=\emptyset
. In particular, a more precise observation is that
\mathrm{DNR}
depends only on the choice of
\psi
, where
\psi
is chosen among the diagonals of admissible enumerations. The constraint that
\psi
be the diagonal of an admissible enumeration can be lifted to give a more general notion.

Definition II.2.3

(avoidance)
.

Suppose
\psi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is a partial recursive function. The class
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}
is defined by
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}\coloneq\{X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid X\cap\psi=\emptyset\}
. Additionally, given any recursive
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
, we define:

\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)\coloneq\{X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid X\cap\psi=\emptyset\wedge\forall n\,(X(n)<p(n))\}.

In this more general framework, the analogs of the diagonals of admissible enumerations are the universal partial recursive funcitons.

Definition II.2.4

(universal partial recursive function)
.

A partial recursive function
\psi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is
universal
if for every partial recursive function
\theta\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
there is a total recursive function
f
such that
\psi\circ f=\theta
.

This is based on the fact that the diagonal of any admissible enumeration
\varphi_{\bullet}
is universal.

Proposition II.2.5
.

The diagonal of any admissible enumeration is universal.

Proof.

Let
\varphi_{\bullet}
be an admissible enumeration and
\psi
its diagonal, and let
\theta\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
be any partial recursive function. Let
\chi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}
be defined by
\chi(e,x,y)\simeq\varphi_{e}(x)
for
e,x,y\in\mathbb{N}
. By the Parametrization Theorem, there exists a total recursive function
g\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\varphi_{g(e,x)}(y)\simeq\chi(e,x,y)\simeq\varphi_{e}(x)
for all
e,x,y\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
e\in\mathbb{N}
satisfy
\varphi_{e}(x)\simeq\theta(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
be defined by
f(x)\coloneq g(e,x)
. Then

(\psi\circ f)(x)\simeq\varphi_{g(e,x)}(g(e,x))\simeq\varphi_{e}(x)\simeq\theta(x).

As
\theta
was arbitrary, it follows that
\psi
is universal.
∎

II.2.1
Linearly Universal Avoidance

As with
\mathrm{DNR}(p)
,
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
still depends on the choice of universal partial recursive function
\psi
. To remove the dependence on a
single
choice of universal partial recursive function we instead choose a
collection
of universal partial recursive functions.

Definition II.2.6
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a collection of partial recursive functions and
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is recursive. We define

\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\mathcal{C}}(p)\coloneq\bigcup_{\psi\in\mathcal{C}}{\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)}.

The choice of
\mathcal{C}
we make follows the convention introduced by
[
29
]
, motivated by
[
2
]
and
[
19
]
.

Definition II.2.7

(linearly universal partial recursive function)
.

A
linearly universal partial recursive function
is a partial recursive function
\psi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for any partial recursive
\theta\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
there exist
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\psi(ax+b)\simeq\theta(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{N}
.

Definition II.2.8
.

Given a recursive
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
we define
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\coloneq\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\mathcal{LU}}(p)
, where
\mathcal{LU}
is the family of linearly universal partial recursive functions.

Remark II.2.9
.

The approach used in
[
2
]
and
[
19
]
is to use
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
, where
\psi
is the diagonal of a ‘linear enumeration’, i.e., an admissible enumeration
\varphi_{\bullet}
for which there is a total recursive
\ell\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
satisfying the following conditions:

1.

For all
e,x\in\mathbb{N}
,
\psi(\ell(e,x))\simeq\varphi_{e}(x)
and

2.

for each
e\in\mathbb{N}
there exist
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\ell(e,x)\leq ax+b
for all
x\in\mathbb{N}
.

II.2.2
Properties of Linearly Universal Partial Recursive Functions

Every linearly universal partial recursive function
\psi
produces an effective enumeration
\varphi_{\bullet}
of the partial recursive functions by setting

\varphi_{e}(x)\simeq\psi((e)_{0}x+(e)_{1})

for
e,x\in\mathbb{N}
, where
(-)_{i}\coloneq\pi_{i}\circ(\pi^{(2)})^{-1}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
(so
\pi^{(2)}((n)_{0},(n)_{1})=n
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
). This enumeration
\varphi_{\bullet}
is admissible.

Proposition II.2.10

(Parametrization Theorem for Linearly Universal Partial Recursive Functions)
.

Suppose
\psi
is a linearly universal partial recursive function and
\theta\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{k+1}\to\mathbb{N}
is a partial recursive function. There exist elementary recursive functions
A,B\colon\mathbb{N}^{k}\to\mathbb{N}
such that

\psi(A(\mathbf{x})y+B(\mathbf{x}))\simeq\theta(\mathbf{x},y)

for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
and
y\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

Because
\psi
is linearly universal, there exists
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\psi(a\pi^{(k+1)}(\mathbf{x},y)+b)\simeq\theta(\mathbf{x},y)
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
and
y\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, defining
A,B\colon\mathbb{N}^{k}\to\mathbb{N}
by

A(\mathbf{x})\coloneq a2^{\pi^{(k)}(\mathbf{x})+1}\quad\text{and}\quad B(\mathbf{x})\coloneq a(2^{\pi^{(k)}(\mathbf{x})}-1)+b

gives

\displaystyle\psi(A(\mathbf{x})y+B(\mathbf{x}))

\displaystyle\simeq\psi(a2^{\pi^{(k)}(\mathbf{x})+1}y+a(2^{\pi^{(k)}(\mathbf{x})}-1)+b)

\displaystyle\simeq\psi(a(2^{\pi^{(k)}(\mathbf{x})}(2y+1)-1)+b)

\displaystyle\simeq\psi(a\pi^{(k+1)}(\mathbf{x},y)+b)

\displaystyle\simeq\theta(\mathbf{x},y)

for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
and
y\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

Corollary II.2.11
.

Suppose
\psi
is a linearly universal partial recursive function. Then the effective enumeration
\varphi_{\bullet}
defined by
\varphi_{e}(n)\coloneq\psi((e)_{0}n+(e)_{1})
is admissible.

Corollary II.2.12

(Recursion Theorem for Linearly Universal Partial Recursive Functions)
.

Suppose
\psi
is a linearly universal partial recursive function and
\theta\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{k+2}\to\mathbb{N}
is a partial recursive function. Then there exist
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\psi(a\pi^{(k)}(\mathbf{x})+b)\simeq\theta(a,b,\mathbf{x})

for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\theta}\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{k+1}\to\mathbb{N}
be defined by

\tilde{\theta}(c,\mathbf{x})\simeq\theta((c)_{0},(c)_{1},\mathbf{x})

for all
c\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
.
II.2.11
and
I.3.12
show that that there exists an
e\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\psi((e)_{0}\pi^{(k)}(\mathbf{x})+(e)_{1})\simeq\varphi_{e}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x})\simeq\tilde{\theta}(e,\mathbf{x})\simeq\theta((e)_{0},(e)_{1},\mathbf{x})

for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, we may let
a=(e)_{0}
and
b=(e)_{1}
.
∎

Moreover, the diagonal of
\varphi_{\bullet}
is linearly universal.

Proposition II.2.13
.

If
\psi_{0}
is a linearly universal partial recursive function, then the partial function
\psi
defined by
\psi(e)\simeq\psi_{0}((e)_{0}e+(e)_{1})
for
e\in\mathbb{N}
is also linearly universal partial recursive.

Proof.

Suppose
\theta\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is a partial recursive function. There are
a
and
b
such that
\forall x\,(\psi_{0}(ax+b)\simeq\theta(x))
. For any
x\in\mathbb{N}
,
\pi^{(2)}(0,x)=2^{0}(2x+1)-1=2x
, so

\psi(2x)\simeq\psi(\pi^{(2)}(0,x))\simeq\psi_{0}(0\cdot x+x)=\psi_{0}(x).

It follows that
\psi
is linearly universal.
∎

Together,
II.2.11
and
II.2.13
allow us to enjoy the benefits of linearly universal partial recursive functions and of admissible enumerations simultaneously, e.g., having access to the particularly nice versions of the Parametrization and Recursion Theorems in the forms of
II.2.10
and
II.2.12
, respectively.

Another convenient property of linearly universal partial recursive functions is that we may we edit any finite number of values without affecting the linear universality.

Proposition II.2.14
.

Suppose
\psi,\chi
are partial recursive functions such that
\psi(n)\simeq\chi(n)
for almost all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
\psi
is linearly universal if and only if
\chi
is linearly universal.

Proof.

Suppose
\psi
is linearly universal. Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
\psi(x)\simeq\chi(x)
for all
x\geq N
. Consider the partial recursive function
\theta\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
defined by

\theta(x)\simeq\begin{cases}\psi(x-N)&\text{if $x\geq N$,}\\
{\uparrow}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Because
\psi
is linearly universal, there are
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\psi(ax+b)\simeq\theta(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{N}
.
\theta
is nonconstant, so
a\neq 0
and hence
ax+b\geq x
for all
x\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, for all
x\in\mathbb{N}
,

\chi(ax+(aN+b))\simeq\psi(ax+(aN+b))\simeq\psi(a(x+N)+b)\simeq\theta(x+N)\simeq\psi(x),

showing
\chi
is linearly universal.
∎

Convention II.2.15
.

Given
p\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
, suppose
p{\upharpoonright}\,\mathbb{N}_{\geq a}
is a total, computable, nondecreasing, unbounded function with image in
(1,\infty)
for some
a\in\mathbb{N}
. Define
\tilde{p}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
by
\tilde{p}(x)\coloneq p(x)
for
x\geq a
and
\tilde{p}(x)\coloneq p(a)
otherwise. Then
\tilde{p}
is an order function, and we let
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
denote the class
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(\tilde{p})
. This allows us to make sense of something like, e.g.,
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(\log_{2})
.

II.2.2.1
Basic Properties of the
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
Hierarchy

The regularity of the manner in which linearly universal partial recursive functions express their universality allows us to prove some simple but useful strong and weak reductions.

Proposition II.2.16
.

Let
p,q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
be recursive functions.

1.

If
q(x)=p(ax+b)
for some
a\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
and
b\in\mathbb{N}
for all
x\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
.

2.

If
X\in\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
,
Y\in p^{\mathbb{N}}
, and
X(x)=Y(x)
for almost all
x\in\mathbb{N}
, then
Y\in\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
.

3.

If
p\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}q
, then
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
.

4.

If for all
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
we have
q(ax+b)\leq p(x)
for almost all
x\in\mathbb{N}
and
\psi_{0}
is a partial recursive function, then
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{0}}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
.

Proof.

1.

Let
\psi
be a linearly universal partial recursive function. If
X\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
, then
X\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q)\subseteq\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
since
p(x)\leq q(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, the recursive functional
X\mapsto X
shows
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
.

Conversely, if
X\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q)
, define
\tilde{\psi}
by

\tilde{\psi}(y)\simeq\begin{cases}\psi(x)&\text{if $y=ax+b$,}\\
{\uparrow}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Because
\tilde{\psi}(ax+b)\simeq\psi(x)
, it follows that
\tilde{\psi}
is linearly universal partial recursive. Similarly define
\tilde{X}\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
by

\tilde{X}(y)\simeq\begin{cases}X(x)&\text{if $y=ax+b$,}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

If
X\cap\psi=\emptyset
and
X
is
q
-bounded, then
\tilde{X}\cap\tilde{\psi}=\emptyset
and
\tilde{X}
is
p
-bounded. Thus, the recursive functional
X\mapsto\tilde{X}
shows
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
.

2.

Let
\psi
be a linearly universal partial recursive function and suppose
X\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}_{p}^{\psi}
. Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
X(x)=Y(x)
for all
x\geq N
and define
\tilde{\psi}
by

\tilde{\psi}(x)\simeq\begin{cases}\psi(x)&\text{if $x\geq N$,}\\
{\uparrow}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

so
Y\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\tilde{\psi}}(p)
. By
II.2.14
,
\tilde{\psi}
is linearly universal, so
Y\in\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
.

3.

Suppose
p(x)\leq q(x)
for all
x\geq N
. Given
X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
, let
\tilde{X}
be defined by

\tilde{X}(x)\coloneq\begin{cases}X(x)&\text{if $x\geq N$}\\
c&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

where
c
is a rational number such that
1<c<q(0)
. If
X
is
p
-bounded, then
\tilde{X}
is
q
-bounded. (b) above then shows that
\tilde{X}\in\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
. This process defines a total recursive functional
\Psi
, so
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
.

4.

Let
\psi
be a linearly universal partial recursive function and suppose
X\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q)
. Let
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
\psi(ax+b)\simeq\psi_{0}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\tilde{X}
be defined by
\tilde{X}(x)\coloneq X(ax+b)
. Then
\tilde{X}\cap\psi_{0}=\emptyset
and
\tilde{X}(x)=X(ax+b)<q(ax+b)\leq p(x)
shows that
\tilde{X}\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{0}}(p)
.

∎

Some other general basic reductions we make use of are given below.

Proposition II.2.17
.

Let
\psi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
and
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
be given.

1.

Suppose
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is an order function dominating
p
. Then
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
.

2.

Suppose
u\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is recursive. Then
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi\circ u}(p\circ u)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
.

Proof.

1.

Suppose
p(n)\leq q(n)
for all
n\geq N
. Let
\tau\in\{0,1\}^{N}
be any string such that
\tau(n)\nsimeq\psi(n)
for all
n<N
. Then the recursive functional
X\mapsto\tau{}^{\frown}\left(X{\upharpoonright}\,[N,\infty)\right)
gives a strong reduction from
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q)
to
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
.

2.

The recursive functional
X\mapsto X\circ u
gives a strong reduction from
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
to
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi\circ u}(p\circ u)
.

∎

As our interest lies in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
, we must show that
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p))\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
for any recursive
p
.

Lemma II.2.18
.

There is an effective enumeration of the linearly universal partial recursive functions.

Proof.

Let
\varphi_{\bullet}
be an admissible enumeration of the partial recursive functions, let
\psi_{0}
be a fixed linearly universal partial recursive function, and let
e_{0}
be such that
\psi_{0}=\varphi_{e_{0}}
.

The central observation we make is that for any partial recursive
\psi
,
\psi
is linearly universal if and only if there are
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\psi(ax+b)\simeq\psi_{0}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{N}
. With this in mind, we modify
\varphi_{\bullet}
to produce an enumeration of the linearly universal partial recursive functions as follows: given
a,b,e\in\mathbb{N}
, define
\psi_{\pi^{(3)}(a,b,e)}\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
by

\psi_{\pi^{(3)}(a,b,e)}(x)\simeq\begin{cases}\psi_{0}(y)&\text{if $ay+b=x$,}\\
\varphi_{e}(x)&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Then
\psi
is linearly universal if and only if there exist
a,b,e\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\psi=\psi_{\pi^{(3)}(a,b,e)}
, so
\psi_{\bullet}
gives an effective enumeration of the linearly universal partial recursive functions.
∎

Proposition II.2.19
.

Suppose
p
is a recursive function. Then
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
is
\Sigma^{0}_{2}
. Consequently,
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p))\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

Proof.

By
II.2.18
, there exists an effective enumeration
\psi_{\bullet}
of the linearly universal partial recursive functions. Let
\varphi_{\bullet}
be any admissible enumeration; the Parametrization Theorem implies there is a total recursive function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\varphi_{f(e)}=\psi_{e}
for all
e\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

X\in\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\equiv\exists e\forall n\forall s\forall m\,(\varphi_{f(e),s}(n){\downarrow}=m\to m\neq X(n))\wedge\forall n(X(n)<p(n))

shows that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
is
\Sigma^{0}_{2}
. The
Proposition I.4.17

(Embedding Lemma)
.
then implies
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p))\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.
∎

II.3
Fast & Slow-Growing Order Functions

An important dividing line concerning how the growth rate of
p
determines where in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
the weak degree of
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
falls is whether the series
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
converges or not.

Definition II.3.1

(fast & slow-growing order functions)
.

Suppose
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(0,\infty)
is nondecreasing and computable.
p
is
fast-growing
if
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}<\infty
and
slow-growing
otherwise.

II.3.1
Bounding Sequences of Fast & Slow-Growing Order Functions

Given a recursive sequence of slow-growing order functions, we can effectively find a slow-growing lower bound (in the sense of
\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}
). Under some additional conditions on the sequence, we can also effectively find a slow-growing upper bound.

Proposition II.3.2
.

Suppose
\langle p_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a recursive sequence of slow-growing order functions.

1.

There is a slow-growing order function
q^{-}
such that
q^{-}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}p_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

2.

Suppose, additionally, that
p_{k}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}p_{k+1}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there is a slow-growing order funciton
q^{+}
such that
p_{k}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}q^{+}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

1.

We simultaneously define the values
q^{-}(n)
and natural numbers
M_{n}
by recursion. We start by setting
q^{-}(0)\coloneq p_{0}(0)
and
M_{0}\coloneq 0
.

Given
q^{-}(0),q^{-}(1),\ldots,q^{-}(n)
and
M_{0},M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}
have been defined, let
M_{n+1}
equal
M_{n}+1
if
M_{n}+1\leq\min_{0\leq k\leq M_{n}+1}{p_{k}(n+1)}
and otherwise equal to
M_{n}
. Then define

q^{-}(n+1)\coloneq\min\{p_{0}(n+1),p_{1}(n+1),\ldots,p_{M_{n+1}}(n+1),M_{n+1}+1\}.

We now claim that
q^{-}
is a slow-growing order function dominated by each
p_{k}
.

Nondecreasing.

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle q^{-}(n)

\displaystyle=\min\{p_{0}(n),p_{1}(n),\ldots,p_{M_{n}}(n),M_{n}+1\}

\displaystyle\leq\min\{p_{0}(n+1),p_{1}(n+1),\ldots,p_{M_{n+1}}(n+1),M_{n}+1\}

\displaystyle\leq\min\{p_{0}(n+1),p_{1}(n+1),\ldots,p_{M_{n+1}}(n+1),M_{n+1}+1\}

\displaystyle=q^{-}(n+1)

as
p_{k}
is nondecreasing for each
k
.

Unbounded.

It suffices to show that
\lim_{n\to\infty}{M_{n}}=\infty
. Suppose for the sake of a contradiction that
\lim_{n\to\infty}{M_{n}}<\infty
, so that
M_{n}
is eventually constant, say to
M
. For
M_{n}
to be eventually constant, it must be the case that
M>\min_{0\leq k\leq M}{p_{k}(n+1)}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. But
p_{0}
is unbounded, so
\min_{0\leq k\leq M}{p_{k}(n+1)}
is unbounded as a function of
n
, yielding a contradiction.

Dominated by
p_{k}
.

Given
k
, there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
M_{n}\geq k
. Then
q^{-}(n)\leq p_{k}(n)
for all
n\geq M_{n}
.

Slow-Growing.

As
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p_{0}(n)^{-1}}=\infty
and
q^{-}
is dominated by
p_{0}
, it follows by Direct Comparison that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{q^{-}(n)^{-1}}=\infty
.

Recursive.

The uniform recursiveness of the
p_{k}
’s implies that the simultaneous construction of
q^{-}
and the sequence
\langle M_{n}\rangle_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is recursive.

2.

We start by recursively defining natural numbers
N_{m}\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
N_{0}=0
, and given
N_{m}
has been defined, define
N_{m+1}
to be the least natural number greater than
N_{m}
such that

\sum_{n=N_{m}}^{N_{m+1}-1}{p_{m}(n)^{-1}}\geq 1

and for which
p_{m}(N_{m+1}-1)\leq p_{m+1}(N_{m+1})
. This is possible because
p_{m}
is slow-growing and
p_{m}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}p_{m+1}
.
We define
q^{+}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(0,\infty)
as follows: given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
m
be the unique natural number for which
N_{m}\leq n<N_{m+1}
, and define

q^{+}(n)\coloneq p_{m}(n).

We claim that
q^{+}
is a slow-growing order function dominating each
p_{k}
.

Nondecreasing.

By definition,
q^{+}
is nondecreasing on the interval
N_{m}\leq n<N_{m+1}
since it agrees with the nondecreasing function
p_{m}
on that interval. Thus, to show that
q^{+}
is nondecreasing, it suffices to show that
q^{+}(N_{m+1}-1)\leq q^{+}(N_{m+1})
for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
. But by the definition of
N_{m+1}
and
q^{+}
, we have
q^{+}(N_{m+1}-1)=p_{m}(N_{m+1}-1)\leq p_{m+1}(N_{m+1})=q^{+}(N_{m+1})
.

Unbounded.

By definition,
p_{0}(n)\leq q^{+}(n)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
p_{0}
is unbounded, it follows that
q^{+}
is unbounded.

Slow-Growing.

For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, by the definition of
\langle N_{m}\rangle_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
and
q^{+}
we have

\sum_{n=0}^{N_{m}-1}{q^{+}(n)^{-1}}=\sum_{n=0}^{N_{1}-1}{p_{0}(n)^{-1}}+\sum_{n=N_{1}}^{N_{2}-1}{p_{1}(n)^{-1}}+\cdots+\sum_{n=N_{m-1}}^{N_{m}-1}{p_{m}(n)^{-1}}\geq m.

Thus,
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{q^{+}(n)^{-1}}=\lim_{m\to\infty}{\sum_{n=0}^{N_{m}-1}{q^{+}(N)^{-1}}}=\lim_{m\to\infty}{m}=\infty
, so
q^{+}
is slow-growing.

Dominates
p_{k}
.

By the definition of
\langle N_{m}\rangle_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
and
q^{+}
, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have
q^{+}(n)\geq p_{k}(n)
for all
n\geq N_{k}
, so
q^{+}\mathrel{\geq_{\mathrm{dom}}}p_{k}
.

Recursive.

The uniform recursiveness of the
p_{k}
’s implies that the sequence
\langle N_{m}\rangle_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is recursive, and subsequently that the function
q^{+}
is recursive.

∎

Without the additional hypotheses in
II.3.2
(b), an upper bound may not exist, however:

Example II.3.3
.

We simultaneously define two slow-growing order functions
p_{1},p_{2}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(0,\infty)
and a strictly increasing sequence
\langle N_{m}\rangle_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
. The role of
\langle N_{m}\rangle_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
will be that the behaviors of
p_{1}
or
p_{2}
will be consistent between
N_{m}
and
N_{m+1}-1
, with those behaviors switching upon incrementing
m
. We start by defining
p_{1}(0)=p_{2}(0)\coloneq 1
,
N_{0}\coloneq 0
, and
N_{1}\coloneq 1
. Suppose
N_{m}
has been defined and that
p_{1}(n)
and
p_{2}(n)
have been defined for all
n<N_{m}
. We split into two cases, depending on whether
m
is even or not.

Case 1:
m
even.

Let
N_{m+1}
be the least natural number greater than
N_{m}
such that
\sum_{n=0}^{N_{m}-1}{p_{1}(n)^{-1}}+(N_{m+1}-N_{m})\cdot p_{1}(N_{m}-1)^{-1}\geq m+1
, then define

\displaystyle p_{1}(n)

\displaystyle\coloneq p_{1}(N_{m}-1),

\displaystyle p_{2}(n)

\displaystyle\coloneq n^{2},

for
N_{m}\leq n<N_{m+1}
.

Case 2:
m
odd.

Identical to the case where
m
is even, but with
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
switched.

In other words, we continually switch between being constant and being equal to the square function, with
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
having the opposite behavior of the other.
By construction, both
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are slow-growing order functions, but
\max\{p_{1}(n),p_{2}(n)\}=n^{2}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, so there is no slow-growing order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(0,\infty)
which dominates both
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
since
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\frac{1}{n^{2}}}<\infty
.

For the fast-growing case, an upper bound always exists, but the existence of a lower bound requires an additional hypothesis. Unlike in the slow-growing case, this additional hypothesis is not only on the form of the sequence
\langle p_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, but on the constituent
p_{k}
’s themselves.

Lemma II.3.4
.

Suppose
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(0,\infty)
is a fast-growing order function. Then
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is a left r.e. real.

Proof.

\left\langle\sum_{n=0}^{k}{p(n)^{-1}}\right\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of uniformly recursive reals converging monotonically to
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
from below.
∎

Proposition II.3.5
.

Suppose
\langle p_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a recursive sequence of fast-growing order functions
p_{k}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(0,\infty)
.

1.

There is a fast-growing order funciton
q^{+}
such that
p_{k}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}q^{+}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

2.

Suppose, additionally, that
\langle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p_{k}(n)^{-1}}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of uniformly recursive reals. Then there is a fast-growing order function
q^{-}
such that
q^{-}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}p_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and for which
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{q^{-}(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real.

Proof.

1.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
we define

q^{+}(n)\coloneq\max_{k\leq n}{p_{k}(n)}.

We claim that
q^{+}
is a fast-growing order function dominating each
p_{k}
.

Nondecreasing.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, that each
p_{k}
is nondecreasing implies

q^{+}(n)=\max_{k\leq n}{p_{k}(n)}\leq\max_{k\leq n}{p_{k}(n+1)}\leq\max_{k\leq n+1}{p_{k}(n+1)}=q^{+}(n+1).

Unbounded.

By construction,
q^{+}(n)\geq p_{0}(n)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Because
p_{0}
is unbounded,
q^{+}
is as well.

Fast-Growing.

By construction,
q^{+}(n)\geq p_{0}(n)
for all
n
, so Direct Comparison shows
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{q^{+}(n)^{-1}}<\infty
since
p_{0}
is fast-growing.

Dominates
p_{k}
.

Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
, for all
n\geq k
we have
q^{+}(n)=\max_{m\leq n}{p_{m}(n)}\geq p_{k}(n)
. Thus,
p_{k}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}q^{+}
.

Recursive.

The uniform recursiveness of the
p_{k}
’s immediately shows that
q^{+}
is recursive.

2.

We start by recursively defining natural numbesr
N_{m}\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
N_{0}\coloneq 0
, and given
N_{m}
has been defined, define
N_{m+1}
to be the least natural number greater than
N_{m}
such that

\sum_{k=0}^{m+1}{\sum_{n=N_{m+1}}^{\infty}{\frac{1}{p_{k}(n)}}}\leq\frac{1}{2^{m+1}}

which exists since
p_{k}
is fast-growing for each
k
.

Now define
q^{-}
as follows. Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
m
be the unique natural number for which
N_{m}\leq n<N_{m+1}
. Then define

q^{-}(n)\coloneq\min_{k\leq m}{p_{k}(n)}.

We claim that
q^{-}
is a nondecreasing, unbounded, fast-growing function which is dominated by each
p_{k}
.

Nondecreasing.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
m
be such that
N_{m}\leq n<N_{m+1}
. Then

q^{-}(n+1)=\begin{cases}\min_{k\leq m}{p_{k}(n+1)}&\text{if $N_{m}\leq n+1<N_{m+1}$,}\\
\min_{k\leq m+1}{p_{k}(n+1)}&\text{if $N_{m+1}\leq n+1$,}\end{cases}\geq\min_{k\leq m}{p_{k}(n+1)}\geq\min_{k\leq m}{p_{k}(n)}=q^{-}(n).

Unbounded.

Observe that if
N_{m}\leq n
, then
p_{k}(n)\geq m
for all
k\leq m+1
. Thus, if
N_{m}\leq n<N_{m+1}
, we have

q^{-}(n)=\min_{k\leq m}{p_{k}(n)}\geq m.

It follows that
q^{-}
is unbounded.

Fast-Growing.

By the definition of
N_{m}
for
m\geq 1
,

\sum_{k=0}^{m}{\sum_{n=N_{m}}^{N_{m+1}-1}{p_{k}(n)^{-1}}}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{m}{\sum_{n=N_{m}}^{\infty}{p_{k}(n)^{-1}}}\leq 2^{-m}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{q^{-}(n)^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{N_{1}-1}{q^{-}(n)^{-1}}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}{\sum_{n=N_{m}}^{N_{m+1}-1}{\max_{k\leq m}{p_{k}(n)^{-1}}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=0}^{N_{1}-1}{q^{-}(n)^{-1}}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}{\sum_{k=0}^{m}{\sum_{n=N_{m}}^{N_{m+1}-1}{p_{k}(n)^{-1}}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=0}^{N_{1}-1}{q^{-}(n)^{-1}}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}{2^{-m}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{N_{1}-1}{q^{-}(n)^{-1}}+1

\displaystyle<\infty.

Dominated by
p_{k}
.

By construction, for each
k
,
q^{-}(n)\leq p_{k}(n)
for all
n>N_{k}
.

If the both the functions
p_{k}
and the reals
\alpha_{k}\coloneq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p_{k}(n)^{-1}}
are uniformly recursive, then the function
m\mapsto N_{m}
is recursive since
N_{m+1}
is the least natural number greater than
N_{m}
such that

\sum_{k=0}^{m+1}{\left(\alpha_{k}-\sum_{n=N_{m}}^{N_{m+1}-1}{p_{k}(n)^{-1}}\right)}\leq 2^{-m}.

The recursiveness of the map
m\mapsto N_{m}
then implies that
q^{-}
is recursive.

Finally, we show that
\beta\coloneq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{q^{-}(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real. Define, for
i\geq 1
,

\beta_{i}\coloneq\sum_{n=0}^{N_{i}-1}{q^{-}(n)^{-1}}+2^{-(i-1)}.

We claim that
\langle\beta_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}}
is a recursive sequence of uniformly recursive reals converging monotonically to
\beta
from above. Since
\lim_{i\to\infty}{\sum_{n=0}^{N_{i}-1}{q^{-}(n)^{-1}}}=\beta
and
\lim_{i\to\infty}{2^{-(i-1)}}=0
, an argument analogous to the proof that
q^{-}
was fast-growing shows
\lim_{i\to\infty}{\beta_{i}}=\beta
. Additionally,
\langle\beta_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}}
is nonincreasing:

\beta_{i+1}=\sum_{n=0}^{N_{i}-1}{q^{-}(n)^{-1}}+\sum_{n=N_{i}}^{N_{i+1}-1}{q^{-}(n)^{-1}}+2^{-(i+1)}\leq\sum_{n=0}^{N_{i}-1}{q^{-}(n)^{-1}}+2^{-(i+1)}+2^{-(i+1)}=\sum_{n=0}^{N_{i}-1}{q^{-}(n)^{-1}}+2^{-i}=\beta_{i}.

Thus,
\beta
is right r.e. and hence recursive.

∎

Corollary II.3.6
.

Suppose
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(0,\infty)
is a fast-growing order function such that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is recursive. Then there exists a fast-growing order function
q
such that
p(n)/q(n)\nearrow\infty
as
n\to\infty
and for which
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{q(n)^{-1}}
is recursive.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\coloneq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
and let
p_{m}
denote the function defined by
p_{k}(n)\coloneq p(n)/2^{k}
for
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
. Note that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p_{k}(n)^{-1}}=2^{k}\alpha
is recursive, and the sequences
\langle p_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\langle 2^{k}\alpha\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are uniformly recursive.

By
II.3.5
(b), there exists a fast-growing order function
q
such that
q\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}p_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and where
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{q(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real. Moreover, the proof of
II.3.5
(b) shows that there is such a
q
for which
p(n)/q(n)\nearrow\infty
as
n\to\infty
.
∎

II.3.2
More about Recursive Sums

The extra hypothesis that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
be not only finite but additionally recursive does not hold for all fast-growing order functions
p
, so it is a strictly stronger hypothesis than just being fast-growing:

Example II.3.7
.

Let
\alpha
be any real in
(0,1)
which is left r.e. but not recursive (e.g.,
\alpha=\sum_{\varphi_{e}(0){\downarrow}}{2^{-e}}
) and let
\langle\alpha_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a recursive sequence of rational numbers converging monotonically to
\alpha
from below. Without loss of generality, we may assume
\langle\alpha_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is strictly increasing. We simultaneously define a strictly increasing recursive function
k\mapsto N_{k}
and an order function
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
with the following additional properties:

1.

N_{0}=0
.

2.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
p
is constant on
\{N_{k},N_{k}+1,\ldots,N_{k+1}-1\}
.

3.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha_{k}\leq\sum_{n<N_{k+1}}{p(n)^{-1}}<\alpha_{k+1}
.

Define
N_{0}\coloneq 0
, let
m_{0}
be the least positive integer such that
\frac{1}{m_{0}}<\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{0}
, and let
N_{1}
be the least natural number for which
\alpha_{0}\leq\frac{N_{1}}{m_{0}}
. The minimality of
N_{1}
and
m
imply
\frac{N_{1}}{m_{0}}<\alpha_{1}
.

Now suppose
N_{k+1}
and
p(n)
for
n<N_{k+1}
have been defined such that
\alpha_{k}\leq\sum_{n<N_{k+1}}{p(n)^{-1}}<\alpha_{k+1}
. Let
m_{k}
be the least natural number greater than
p(N_{k+1}-1)
such that
\frac{1}{m_{k}}<\alpha_{k+2}-\alpha_{k+1}
, define
N_{k+2}
to be the least natural number for which
\alpha_{k+1}\leq\sum_{n<N_{k+1}}{p(n)^{-1}}+(N_{k+2}-N_{k+1})\cdot\frac{1}{m_{k}}
, and finally let
p(n)=m_{k}
for all
n\in\{N_{k+1},N_{k+1}+1,\ldots,N_{k+2}-1\}
. By definition,
\alpha_{k+1}\leq\sum_{n<N_{k+2}}{p(n)^{-1}}
. To see that
\sum_{n<N_{k+2}}{p(n)^{-1}}<\alpha_{k+2}
, observe that the minimality of
N_{k+2}
would implies
\sum_{n<N_{k+2}-1}{p(n)^{-1}}<\alpha_{k+1}
, so if
\sum_{n<N_{k+2}}{p(n)^{-1}}\geq\alpha_{k+2}
we would have
m^{-1}\geq\alpha_{k+2}-\alpha_{k+1}
, contradicting the choice of
m_{k}
.

By construction,
p
is an order function for which
\alpha_{k}\leq\sum_{n=0}^{N_{k+1}-1}{p(n)^{-1}}<\alpha
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, so
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}=\alpha
is left r.e. but not recursive.

We cover two results that assist in finding examples of fast-growing order functions
p
for which
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real. The first shows that if a series converges at least as quickly as a series converging to a recursive real, then the first series converges to a recursive real.

Proposition II.3.8
.

Suppose
f,g\colon\mathbb{N}\to(0,\infty)
are recursive functions such that
f\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}g
and
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{g(n)}
is a recursive real. Then
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{f(n)}
is a recursive real.

Proof.

We assume without loss of generality that
f(n)\leq g(n)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

By the Direct Comparison Test,
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{f(n)}
converges. Let
\alpha_{i}=\sum_{n=0}^{i}{f(n)}
,
\alpha=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{f(n)}
,
\beta_{i}=\sum_{n=0}^{i}{g(n)}
, and
\beta=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{g(n)}
. Because
f(n)\leq g(n)
for all
n
,

\alpha-\alpha_{i}=\sum_{n=i+1}^{\infty}{f(n)}\leq\sum_{n=i+1}^{\infty}{g(n)}=\beta-\beta_{i}

and hence
\alpha\leq\alpha_{i}+(\beta-\beta_{i})
. Then the sequence
\langle\alpha_{i}+(\beta-\beta_{i})\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
converges monotonically to
\alpha
from above, showing that
\alpha
is right r.e. and hence recursive by
II.3.4
.
∎

Corollary II.3.9
.

If
p
and
q
are order functions,
p\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}q
, and
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real, then
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{q(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real.

Our second tool is a reduction of the recursiveness of
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
to that of an improper integral
\int_{0}^{\infty}{\overline{p}(x)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x}
for a continuous recursive nondecreasing extension
\overline{p}
of
p
.

Proposition II.3.10
.

Suppose
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(0,\infty)
is a fast-growing order function and let
\overline{p}\colon[0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
be any continuous recursive nondecreasing extension of
p
. Then
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real if and only if
\int_{0}^{\infty}{\overline{p}(x)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x}
is a recursive real.

Proof.

We make two initial observations:

1.

Just as
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is always left r.e., so is
\int_{0}^{\infty}{\overline{p}(x)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x}
. Thus, for either quantity to be recursive it suffices to show that that quantity is right r.e.

2.

The reals
\int_{k}^{k+1}{\overline{p}(x)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x}
are uniformly recursive in
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Suppose
\int_{0}^{\infty}{\overline{p}(x)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x}
is a recursive real. By the Integral Test, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\sum_{n=k+1}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}\leq\int_{k}^{\infty}{\overline{p}(x)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x}\leq\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
and hence

0\leq\left(\sum_{n=0}^{k}{p(n)^{-1}}+\int_{k}^{\infty}{\overline{p}(x)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x}\right)-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}\leq p(k)^{-1}.

Thus,

\left\langle\sum_{n=0}^{k}{p(n)^{-1}}+\int_{0}^{\infty}{\overline{p}(x)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x}-\int_{0}^{k}{\overline{p}(x)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x}\right\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

is a sequence of uniformly recursive reals converging to
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
from above, hence right r.e. by
I.3.30
.

Now suppose
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real. By the Integral Test, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\int_{k+1}^{\infty}{\overline{p}(x)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x}\leq\sum_{n=k+1}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}\leq\int_{k}^{\infty}{\overline{p}(x)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x}
and hence

0\leq\left(\sum_{n=k+1}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}+\int_{0}^{k+1}{\overline{p}(x)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x}\right)-\int_{0}^{\infty}{\overline{p}(x)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x}\leq\int_{k}^{k+1}{\overline{p}(n)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x}\leq\overline{p}(k)^{-1}.

Thus,

\left\langle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}+\int_{0}^{k+1}{\overline{p}(x)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x}-\sum_{n=0}^{k}{p(n)^{-1}}\right\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

is a sequence of uniformly recursive reals converging to
\int_{0}^{\infty}{\overline{p}(x)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x}
from above, hence right r.e. by
I.3.30
.
∎

Using
II.3.10
allows us to show that many of the usual convergent series give recursive sums.

Corollary II.3.11
.

Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
and a recursive real
\alpha\in(1,\infty)
, then

\sum_{n={}^{k}2}^{\infty}{\left(n\cdot\log_{2}n\cdot\log_{2}^{2}n\mathbin{\cdots}\log_{2}^{k-1}n\cdot(\log_{2}^{k}n)^{\alpha}\right)^{-1}}

is a recursive real.

Proof.

Define
p\colon\mathbb{N}_{\geq{}^{k}2}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\overline{p}\colon{[{}^{k}2,\infty)}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle p(n)

\displaystyle\coloneq n\cdot\log_{2}n\cdot\log_{2}^{2}n\mathbin{\cdots}\log_{2}^{k-1}n\cdot(\log_{2}^{k}n)^{\alpha},

\displaystyle\overline{p}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneq x\cdot\log_{2}x\cdot\log_{2}^{2}x\mathbin{\cdots}\log_{2}^{k-1}x\cdot(\log_{2}^{k}x)^{\alpha},

for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in{[{}^{k}2,\infty)}
.

For each
k\geq 1
,

\int_{{}^{k}2}^{\infty}{\frac{1}{x\cdot\log_{2}x\cdot\log_{2}^{2}x\mathbin{\cdots}\log_{2}^{k-1}x\cdot(\log_{2}^{k}x)^{\alpha}}\mathrm{d}x}=(\ln 2)\int_{{}^{k-1}2}^{\infty}{\frac{1}{u\cdot\log_{2}u\mathbin{\cdots}\log_{2}^{k-2}u\cdot(\log_{2}^{k-1}u)^{\alpha}}\mathrm{d}u}.

so by induction on
k
we may show

\int_{{}^{k}2}^{\infty}{\frac{1}{x\cdot\log_{2}x\cdot\log_{2}^{2}x\mathbin{\cdots}\log_{2}^{k-1}x\cdot(\log_{2}^{k}x)^{\alpha}}\mathrm{d}x}=\frac{(\ln 2)^{k}}{\alpha-1},

so
\int_{{}^{k}2}^{\infty}{\overline{p}(x)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}x}
is a recursive real. Then
II.3.10
shows
\sum_{n={}^{k}2}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real.
∎

Corollary II.3.12
.

If
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(0,\infty)
is an order function such that there is a
k\in\mathbb{N}
and a recursive real
\alpha\in(1,\infty)
for which
n\cdot\log_{2}n\cdot\log_{2}^{2}n\mathbin{\cdots}\log_{2}^{k-1}n\cdot(\log_{2}^{k}n)^{\alpha}\leq p(n)
for almost all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq{}^{k}2}
, then
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real.

Proof.

This follows from
II.3.11
and
II.3.9
.
∎

II.3.3
The Fast and Slow-Growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
Hierarchies

The dichotomy between fast-growing and slow-growing allows us to split the
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchy into two sub-hierarchies.

Definition II.3.13

(fast-growing and slow-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchies)
.

The
fast-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchy
is the collection of the classes
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
where
p
is a fast-growing order function, and the
slow-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchy
is the collection of the classes
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
where
q
is a slow-growing order function.

The fast-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchy is downwards closed, while the slow-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchy is upwards closed, so the two hierarchies are separated:

Proposition II.3.14
.

Suppose
p
and
q
are order functions such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
.

1.

If
p
is slow-growing, then
q
is slow-growing.

2.

If
q
is fast-growing, then
p
is fast-growing.

Proof.

[
29
, Theorem 5.4]
(see also
II.4.12
) shows that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathsf{MLR}
if and only if
q
is fast-growing. If
q
is fast-growing, then
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathsf{MLR}
, showing
p
is fast-growing.
∎

The infimum of the slow-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchy lies in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

Proposition II.3.15
.

Define

\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}\coloneq\bigcup\{\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\mid\text{$q$ a slow-growing order function}\}.

\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}
is
\Sigma^{0}_{3}
and hence
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}})\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

Proof.

Let
f
be as in the proof of
II.2.19
, so that
f
is a total recursive function and
\varphi_{f(\bullet)}
is an enumeration of the linearly universal partial recursive functions. Then

\displaystyle X\in\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}

\displaystyle\equiv\exists i\,\Bigl(\exists e\forall n\forall s\forall m\forall k\,\bigl(\bigl(\varphi_{f(e),s}(n){\downarrow}=m\wedge\varphi_{i,s}(n){\downarrow}=k\bigr)\to\bigl(m\neq X(n)\wedge X(n)<k\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\wedge\forall n\exists s\,\bigl(\varphi_{i,s}(n){\downarrow}\bigr)\Bigr)

shows that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}
is
\Sigma^{0}_{3}
. The
Proposition I.4.17

(Embedding Lemma)
.
then implies
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}})\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.
∎

In
Chapter

VI
we will prove the following result:

Theorem
VI.2.1
.

For all order functions
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
, there exists a slow-growing order function
q
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p_{1})\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p_{2})
. In particular, for any order function
p
, there exists a slow-growing order function
q
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
and
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
are weakly incomparable.

In particular, it is not the case that for every fast-growing
p
and slow-growing
q
that
q\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}p
. It is interesting to note, however, that there exist slow-growing order functions
q
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
for all fast-growing order functions
p
– in fact, we may take
q=\id_{\mathbb{N}}
.

Lemma II.3.16
.

Suppose
p
is a fast-growing order function. Then
\id_{\mathbb{N}}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}p
.

Proof.

Suppose
n>0
satisfies
p(n)\leq n
, and let
m\in\mathbb{N}
be the largest natural number such that
2^{m}\leq n
. Then
p(2^{m})\leq p(n)\leq n\leq 2^{m+1}
, so

\frac{1}{2}=\frac{2^{m}}{2^{m+1}}\leq\frac{2^{m}}{p(2^{m})}.

Thus, if
p(n)\leq n
for infinitely many
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then infinitely many of the terms of the series
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}{2^{m}p(2^{m})^{-1}}
are bounded below by
1/2
, implying the series diverges. But an application of the Cauchy Condensation Test shows that the convergence of the series
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
(since
p
is fast-growing) implies the convergence of
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}{2^{m}p(2^{m})^{-1}}
, a contradiction.
∎

Proposition II.3.17
.

\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(\id_{\mathbb{N}})
for every fast-growing order function
p
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from
II.3.16
.
∎

Although there is no fast-growing order function
p
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
for all slow-growing order functions
q
, to every slow-growing
q
there is a fast-growing
p
for which
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
.

Proposition II.3.18
.

For every slow-growing order function
q
there exists a fast-growing order function
p
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
.

Proof.

Given
q
, define
p
by setting
p(n)\coloneq q(n)+2^{n}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

II.4
Depth

The notion of depth is a strengthening of the condition of being negligible.

Definition II.4.1

(negligibility)
.

P\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is
negligible
if
\lambda(P^{\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}})=0
. Equivalently,
\lambda(\Psi^{-1}[P])=0
for every partial recursive functional
\Psi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Depth strengthens this by requiring additional uniformity and was first defined in
[
2
]
for subsets of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, as with negligibility. However, because certain weak degrees in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
are best represented with subsets of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
, it is more convenient to generalize the definition given by Bienvenu & Porter.

Definition II.4.2

(continuous semimeasure on
\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
)
.

A
continuous semimeasure on
\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
is a map
\nu\colon\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\to[0,1]
such that
\nu(\langle\rangle)=1
and
\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}{\nu(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)}\leq\nu(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in T
.

\nu
is
left recursively enumerable
, or
left r.e.
, if it is left r.e. in the usual sense. A left r.e. continuous semimeasure
\mathbf{M}
on
\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
is
universal
if for every left r.e. continuous semimeasure
\nu
on
\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
there exists a
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\nu(\sigma)\leq c\cdot\mathbf{M}(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
.

The left r.e. continuous semimeasures on
\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
can be characterized in terms of partial recursive functionals
\Psi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proposition II.4.3
.

[
32
, Theorem 3.1, essentially]

1.

If
\Psi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
is a partial recursive functional, then the map
\nu\colon\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\to[0,1]
defined by
\nu(\sigma)\coloneq\lambda(\Psi^{-1}(\sigma))=\lambda(\{X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\Psi^{X}\supseteq\sigma\})
is a left r.e. continuous semimeasure on
\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
.

2.

If
\nu
is a left r.e. continuous semimeasure on
\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
, then there is a partial recursive functional
\Psi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\nu(\sigma)=\lambda(\Psi^{-1}(\sigma))=\lambda(\{X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\Psi^{X}\supseteq\sigma\})
for all
\sigma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
.

Remark II.4.4
.

The existence of a universal left r.e. continuous semimeasure on
\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
can be shown by appropriately modifying proofs for the case of
\{0,1\}^{\ast}
(e.g., in
[
6
, Theorem 3.16.2]
consider monotone machines of the form
M\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
).

Notation II.4.5
.

Given
P\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
P{\upharpoonright}\,n
denotes the set
\{X{\upharpoonright}\,n\mid X\in P\}
.

Definition II.4.6

(depth)
.

Let
\mathbf{M}
be a universal left r.e. continuous semimeasure on
\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
. A mass problem
P\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
is
deep
(with respect to
\mathbf{M}
) if it is a r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class and there exists an order function
r\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathbf{M}(P{\upharpoonright}\,r(n))\leq 2^{-n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Such an
r
is a
modulus of depth
for
P
.

Despite the added generality, this more general notion of depth gives the same notion as that of Bienvenu & Porter.

Lemma II.4.7
.

Let
\mathbf{M}_{\{0,1\}}
be a universal left r.e. continuous semimeasure on
\{0,1\}^{\ast}
, and
\mathbf{M}_{\mathbb{N}}
is a universal left r.e. continuous semimeasure on
\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
. Given
P\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\ast}
,
P
is deep with respect
\mathbf{M}_{\{0,1\}}
if and only if
P
is deep with respect to
\mathbf{M}_{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

First suppose
P
is deep with respect to
\mathbf{M}_{\{0,1\}}
, so that there is an order function
r_{\{0,1\}}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathbf{M}_{\{0,1\}}(P{\upharpoonright}\,r_{\{0,1\}}(n))\leq 2^{-n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\nu
be the restriction of
\mathbf{M}_{\mathbb{N}}
to
\{0,1\}^{\ast}
and observe that
\nu
is a left r.e. continuous semimeasure on
\{0,1\}^{\ast}
. By the universality of
\mathbf{M}_{\{0,1\}}
, there exists a
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathbf{M}_{\mathbb{N}}(\sigma)=\nu(\sigma)\leq c\cdot\mathbf{M}_{\{0,1\}}(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
c\leq 2^{m}
and define
r_{\mathbb{N}}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
by
r_{\mathbb{N}}(n)\coloneq r_{\{0,1\}}(n+m)
. Then for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathbf{M}_{\mathbb{N}}(P{\upharpoonright}\,r_{\mathbb{N}}(n))\leq c\cdot\mathbf{M}_{\{0,1\}}(P{\upharpoonright}\,r_{\{0,1\}}(n+m))\leq c\cdot 2^{-(n+m)}\leq 2^{-n}.

Thus,
P
is deep with respect to
\mathbf{M}_{\mathbb{N}}
.

Now suppose
P
is deep with respect to
\mathbf{M}_{\mathbb{N}}
, so that there is an order function
r_{\mathbb{N}}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathbf{M}_{\mathbb{N}}(P{\upharpoonright}\,r_{\mathbb{N}}(n))\leq 2^{-n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\nu
be the extension of
\mathbf{M}_{\{0,1\}}
to
\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
by setting
\nu(\sigma)\coloneq 0
for any
\sigma\notin\{0,1\}^{\ast}
and
\nu(\sigma)=\mathbf{M}_{\{0,1\}}(\sigma)
otherwise. The universality of
\mathbf{M}_{\mathbb{N}}
implies there is a
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\nu(\sigma)\leq c\cdot\mathbf{M}_{\mathbb{N}}(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
c\leq 2^{m}
and define
r_{\{0,1\}}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
by
r_{\{0,1\}}(n)\coloneq r_{\mathbb{N}}(n+m)
. Then for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathbf{M}_{\{0,1\}}(P{\upharpoonright}\,r_{\{0,1\}}(n))=\nu(P{\upharpoonright}\,r_{\{0,1\}}(n))\leq c\cdot\mathbf{M}_{\mathbb{N}}(P{\upharpoonright}\,r_{\mathbb{N}}(n))\leq c\cdot 2^{-(n+m)}\leq 2^{-n}.

Thus,
P
is deep with respect to
\mathbf{M}_{\{0,1\}}
.
∎

Remark II.4.8
.

II.4.7
additionally shows that the definition of depth does not depend on the choice of universal left r.e. semimeasure on
\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
.

The classes
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
for
p
a slow-growing order function provide a plethora of examples of deep r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes.

Theorem II.4.9
.

[
2
, Theorem 7.6]
,
[
29
, Theorem 2.2, Theorem 4.4]

Suppose
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is an order function and
\psi
is a linearly universal partial recursive function.

1.

If
p
is fast-growing, then
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathsf{MLR}
. In particular,
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
is non-negligible.

2.

If
p
is slow-growing, then
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
is deep.

II.4.1
Depth and Difference Randoms

One of the principal properties of deep
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes relates deep
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes to
difference random
sequences.

Definition II.4.10

(difference random)
.

X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is
difference random
if it is Martin-Löf random but incomplete (i.e.,
0^{\prime}\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{T}}}X
).

A theorem of Sacks
[
6
, Corollary 8.12.2]
shows that
\lambda(\{0^{\prime}\}^{\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}})=0
, so almost all members of
\mathsf{MLR}
are difference random in the measure-theoretic sense.

Theorem II.4.11
.

[
2
, Theorem 5.3]

Suppose
P\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is a deep
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class. If
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is difference random, then
X
computes no member of
P
.

A direct application is the following:

Theorem II.4.12
.

[
29
, Theorem 5.4]

Suppose
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is an order function.

1.

If
p
is fast-growing, then
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{<_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathsf{MLR}
.

2.

If
p
is slow-growing, then
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
and
\mathsf{MLR}
are weakly incomparable.

Proof.

1.

Suppose
p
is fast-growing.
II.4.9
(a) shows that for any linearly universal partial recursive
\psi
that
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathsf{MLR}
. As
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)\subseteq\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
, it follows then that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathsf{MLR}
. That this is strict follows from
[
9
, Theorem 5.11]
.

2.

Supose
p
is slow-growing. If
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathsf{MLR}
, then in particular there is an
X\in\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
and a difference random
Y
such that
X\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}Y
. But
X
being a member of
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
means that
X\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
for some linearly universal partial recursive function
\psi
, meaning that
Y
computes a member of the deep (
II.4.9
(b))
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
, contradicting
II.4.11
.

The opposite direction, i.e., that
\mathsf{MLR}\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
, follows from
[
9
, Theorem 5.11]
.

∎

II.4.2
Depth and Strong Reducibility

We used
I.4.23
to show that despite our interests laying in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, considering r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes was safe. We show depth behaves similarly by showing that depth is preserved under recursive homeomorphisms.

Lemma II.4.13
.

Suppose
P
is a r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class and that
\Psi\colon P\to\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
is a recursive functional. Then there exist nondecreasing recursive functions
\psi\colon\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
and
j\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\Psi(X){\upharpoonright}\,n=\psi(X{\upharpoonright}\,j(n))
for all
X\in P
.

Proof.

By
I.4.22
,
\Psi
extends to a total recursive functional
\tilde{\Psi}\colon\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
e\in\mathbb{N}
be an index for
\tilde{\Psi}
(i.e., so that
\varphi_{e}^{X}(n)\simeq\tilde{\Psi}(X)(n)
for all
X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
). Let
h\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
be a nondecreasing recursive function for which
P\subseteq h^{\mathbb{N}}
.

By the compactness of
h^{\mathbb{N}}
, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
there is a
s_{n}
such that
\varphi_{e,s_{n}}^{X{\upharpoonright}\,s_{n}}(n){\downarrow}
for all
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
, and such an
s_{n}
can be found effectively as a function of
n
, so define
j(n)\coloneq\max\{s_{0},s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{n}\}
.

Given
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
, let
n
be the largest natural number for which
j(n)\leq|\sigma|
and define

\psi(\sigma)\coloneq\left\langle\varphi_{e,j(n)}^{\sigma}(0),\varphi_{e,j(n)}^{\sigma}(1),\ldots,\varphi_{e,j(n)}^{\sigma}(n)\right\rangle.

For
\sigma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\setminus h^{\ast}
, let
\psi(\sigma)=\sigma
. Then
\psi(X{\upharpoonright}\,j(n))=\tilde{\Psi}(X){\upharpoonright}\,n
for all
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
, and in particular
\psi(X{\upharpoonright}\,j(n))=\Psi(X){\upharpoonright}\,n
for all
X\in P
.
∎

Proposition II.4.14
.

Suppose
P
is a r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class and that
\Psi\colon P\to\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
is a recursive functional. If
\Psi[P]
is deep, then
P
is deep.

Proof.

Let
Q=\Psi[P]
and let
r
be a modulus of depth for
Q
. Without loss of generality, assume
\Psi
is a total recursive functional.

Let
\psi\colon\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
and
j\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
be as in the proof of
II.4.13
, so that they are nondecreasing recursive functions such that
\Psi(X){\upharpoonright}\,n=\psi(X{\upharpoonright}\,j(n))
for all
X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
and that if
|\sigma|=j(n)
, then
|\psi(\sigma)|=n
. For
\tau\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
, let
\psi^{-1}_{\mathrm{min}}[\{\tau\}]
denote the set of minimal elements of
\psi^{-1}[\{\tau\}]
, and observe that
\psi^{-1}_{\mathrm{min}}[\{\tau\}]=\psi^{-1}[\{\tau\}]\cap\mathbb{N}^{j(|\tau|)}
. Define
\nu\colon\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\to[0,1]
by

\nu(\tau)\coloneq\mathbf{M}(\psi^{-1}[\{\tau\}])=\sum_{\psi(\sigma)=\tau}{\mathbf{M}(\sigma)}.

Assume at the present that
\nu
is a left r.e. continuous semimeasure so that there is a
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
N(\tau)\leq c\cdot\mathbf{M}(\tau)
for all
\tau\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
. Letting
m\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
c\leq 2^{m}
, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\mathbf{M}(P{\upharpoonright}\,j(r(n+m)))\leq\mathbf{M}(\psi^{-1}\left[Q{\upharpoonright}\,r(n+m)\right])=\nu(Q{\upharpoonright}\,r(n+m))\leq c\cdot\mathbf{M}(Q{\upharpoonright}\,r(n+m))\leq c\cdot 2^{-(n+m)}\leq 2^{-n}

so the function
n\mapsto j(r(n+m))
is a modulus of depth for
P
.

Now we show that
\nu
is a left r.e. continuous semimeasure. For any
\tau\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
and
i<i^{\prime}
, we have
\psi^{-1}[\{\tau{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle\}]\cap\psi^{-1}[\{\tau{}^{\frown}\langle i^{\prime}\rangle\}]=\emptyset
. Additionally, every element of
\psi^{-1}[\{\tau{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle\}]
has length
j(|\tau|+1)
(so elements of
\psi^{-1}[\{\tau{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle\}]
are pairwise incompatible) and extends a member of
\psi^{-1}[\{\tau\}]
. Thus,
\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}{\mathbf{M}(\psi^{-1}[\{\tau{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle\}])}\leq\mathbf{M}(\psi^{-1}[\{\tau\}])
and so
\nu
is a continuous semimeasure. For each
\tau\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
, the set
\psi^{-1}[\{\tau\}]
is recursive since
\psi
is recursive, nondecreasing, and
|\sigma|=j(n)
implies
|\psi(\sigma)|=n
. Thus, along with the fact that
\mathbf{M}
is left r.e. we see that
\nu
is left r.e., as desired.
∎

Corollary II.4.15
.

Suppose
P
and
Q
are recursively homeomorphic r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes. Then
P
is deep if and only if
Q
is deep.

This well-behavedness of depth with partial recursive functionals can be summarized nicely:

Proposition II.4.16
.

[
2
, Theorem 6.4]

The collection of deep r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes forms a filter with respect to strong reducibility. I.e., for all r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes
P
and
Q
:

1.

If
P\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}Q
and
P
is deep, then
Q
is deep.

2.

If
P
and
Q
are deep, the
\langle 0\rangle{}^{\frown}P\cup\langle 1\rangle{}^{\frown}Q
is deep.

Proof.

Let
P
and
Q
be r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes. If
P
is deep and
P\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}Q
, then
II.4.14
shows that
Q
is deep.

Now suppose
P
and
Q
are both deep and let
r_{0}
and
r_{1}
be moduli of depth for
P
and
Q
, respectively. For
i\in\{0,1\}
, define
\nu_{i}\colon\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\to[0,1]
by
\nu_{i}(\sigma)\coloneq\mathbf{M}(\langle i\rangle{}^{\frown}\sigma)
.
\nu_{i}
is a left r.e. continuous semimeasure, so there exists
c_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\nu_{i}(\sigma)\leq c_{i}\cdot\mathbf{M}(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
\max\{c_{0},c_{1}\}\leq 2^{m-1}
, and define
r\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
by
r(n)\coloneq\max\{r_{0}(n+m),r_{1}(n+m)\}+1
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}([\langle 0\rangle{}^{\frown}P\cup\langle 1\rangle{}^{\frown}Q]{\upharpoonright}\,r(n))

\displaystyle=\mathbf{M}([\langle 0\rangle{}^{\frown}P]{\upharpoonright}\,r(n))+\mathbf{M}([\langle 1\rangle{}^{\frown}Q]{\upharpoonright}\,r(n))

\displaystyle=\nu_{0}(P{\upharpoonright}\,(r(n)-1))+\nu_{1}(Q{\upharpoonright}\,(r(n)-1))

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\cdot\mathbf{M}(P{\upharpoonright}\,r_{0}(n))+c_{1}\cdot\mathbf{M}(Q{\upharpoonright}\,r_{1}(n))

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-n-1}+2^{-n-1}=2^{-n}.

∎

II.4.3
Depth and Weak Reducibility

II.4.14
shows that depth is especially well-behaved with respect to strong reducibility. However, depth is not as well-behaved with respect to weak reducibility.

Proposition II.4.17
.

[
2
, Theorem 4.7]

For any
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class
P\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
there exists a
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class
Q\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
which is weakly equivalent to
P
but not deep.

There
is
an analog of
II.4.16
if we consider weak degrees of deep
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes.

Definition II.4.18

(deep degree in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
)
.

A weak degree
\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
is a
deep degree (in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
)
if there exists a deep nonempty
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class
P
for which
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P)=\mathbf{p}
.

P\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
is
of deep degree
if
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P)
is a deep degree in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

Proposition II.4.19
.

The collection of deep degrees in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
forms a filter in
\langle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}},\leq\rangle
. I.e., for all
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
:

1.

If
\mathbf{p}\leq\mathbf{q}
and
\mathbf{p}
is a deep degree, then
\mathbf{q}
is a deep degree.

2.

If
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{q}
are deep degrees, then
\inf\{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\}
is a deep degree.

Proof.

Let
P
and
Q
be
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes for which
\mathbf{p}=\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P)
and
\mathbf{q}=\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(Q)
.

If
\mathbf{p}
is a deep degree, then we may assume without loss of generality that
P
is deep. Then
P\times Q=\{X\oplus Y\mid X\in P\wedge Y\in Q\}
is deep by
II.4.16
since
P\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}P\times Q
. Because
P\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}Q
,
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P\times Q)=\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(Q)=\mathbf{q}
, so
\mathbf{q}
is a deep degree.

Now suppose both
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{q}
are deep degrees, and assume without loss of generality that
P
and
Q
are both deep.
II.4.16
shows that
\langle 0\rangle{}^{\frown}P\cup\langle 1\rangle{}^{\frown}Q
is deep. Because
\inf\{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\}=\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P\cup Q)=\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\langle 0\rangle{}^{\frown}P\cup\langle 1\rangle{}^{\frown}Q)
,
\inf\{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\}
is a deep degree.
∎

Lemma II.4.20
.

Suppose
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is nondecreasing,
a\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, and
b\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}<\infty
if and only if
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(an+b)^{-1}}<\infty
.

Proof.

We may assume without loss of generality that
b=0
. Because
p
is nondecreasing,

a\cdot p(a\cdot(n+1))^{-1}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{a-1}{p(an+i)^{-1}}\leq a\cdot p(an)^{-1},

so

a\cdot\sum_{n=1}^{k+1}{p(an)^{-1}}\leq\sum_{n=0}^{a(k+1)-1}{p(n)^{-1}}\leq a\cdot\sum_{n=0}^{k}{p(an)^{-1}}.

Because
p
is a positive-valued function, each of the above summations is nondecreasing as a function of
k
, so if
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}<\infty
, Monotone Convergence would imply
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(an)^{-1}}<\infty
and vice-versa.
∎

Corollary II.4.21
.

Suppose
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is an order function. Then
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
is of deep degree if and only if
p
is slow-growing.

Proof.

Fix a linearly universal partial recursive function
\psi_{0}
. Given
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
, let
p_{a,b}
denote the function defined by
p_{a,b}(x)\coloneq p(ax+b)
for
x\in\mathbb{N}
. By
II.4.20
, the sequence
\langle p_{a,0}\rangle_{a\in\mathbb{N}}
is a recursive sequence of slow-growing order functions. Moreover,
p_{a,0}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}p_{a+1,0}
for every
a\in\mathbb{N}
, so
II.3.2
(b) shows there is a slow-growing order function
q
such that
p_{a,0}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}q
for all
a\in\mathbb{N}
. For any
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
p_{a,b}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}p_{a+1,0}
, so we have
p_{a,b}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}q
more generally.

Given a linearly universal partial recursive
\psi
, there are
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\psi_{0}(x)\simeq\psi(ax+b)
for all
x\in\mathbb{N}
. For such
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{0}}(p_{a,b})\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
by
II.2.17
(b). Because
p_{a,b}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}q
, we have
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{0}}(q)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{0}}(p_{a,b})
by
II.2.17
(a). Thus,
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{0}}(q)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
.
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{0}}(q)
is a deep r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class by
II.4.9
, so
II.4.19
implies
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
is of deep degree.

If
p
were fast-growing, then for any linearly universal partial recursive function
\psi
, we would have
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathsf{MLR}
.
∎

II.4.11
extends to
P\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
of deep degree.

Proposition II.4.22
.

Suppose
P\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
is of deep degree. If
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
