is difference random, then
X
computes no member of
P
.

Proof.

Because
P
is of deep degree, there exists a deep
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class
Q
such that
P\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}Q
. If
Y\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}X
for some
Y\in P
, then the fact that
P\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}Q
implies there is a
Z\in Q
such that
Z\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}Y\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}X
, contradicting
II.4.11
.
∎

II.4.4
Depth for non-r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
Sets

Nothing in our definition of depth necessitates that
P
be a r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class in order for the definition to make sense. However, there are two reasons for our restriction to only r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes. The first is that our interest in depth is ultimately relegated to r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
subsets of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
. The second is that it is unclear whether ‘depth’ is a useful notion for arbitrary subsets of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
, and if so, whether the verbatim extension of the definition of depth to any subset of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
provides the ‘right’ definition.

In fact, we can show that extending our definition of depth even to only
\Pi^{0}_{1}
subsets of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
or
\Pi^{0}_{2}
subsets of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
causes us to lose the guarantee of
II.4.11
that no difference random computes a member of a ‘deep set’.

Lemma II.4.23
.

Suppose
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
X\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}0^{\prime}
. Then
X
is a
\Pi^{0}_{2}
singleton, i.e.,
\{X\}
is
\Pi^{0}_{2}
.

Proof.

X\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}0^{\prime}
implies
X
(as a subset of
\mathbb{N}
) is
\Delta^{0}_{2}
, so there are recursive predicates
R
and
S
such that

x\in X\iff\forall n\exists m\,R(x,n,m)\iff\exists n\forall m\,S(x,n,m).

Then

\{X\}=\bigl\{Y\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\forall x\,\bigl(\bigl(x\in Y\to\forall n\exists m\,R(x,n,m)\bigr)\wedge\bigl(\exists n\forall m\,S(x,n,m)\to x\in Y\bigr)\bigr)\bigr\}

shows that
X
is a
\Pi^{0}_{2}
singleton.
∎

Proposition II.4.24
.

There exists a subset
P\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
which is deep in the sense that there is an order function
r
such that
\mathbf{M}(P{\upharpoonright}\,r(n))\leq 2^{-n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, but for which there are difference random sequences that compute members of
P
. Moreover,
P
may be taken to either be a
\Pi^{0}_{2}
subset of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
or a
\Pi^{0}_{1}
subset of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

Let
Q=\{X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\forall n\,\apc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\geq n-c\}
, where
c
is sufficiently large so that
Q\neq\emptyset
. By the Low Basis Theorem
[
14
]
there is an
A\in Q
such that
A\mathrel{<_{\mathrm{T}}}0^{\prime}
.
II.4.23
then implies
A
is a
\Pi^{0}_{2}
singleton, but being an incomplete Martin-Löf random sequence means that it is also difference random. Since
A\in Q
, we also know that
\mathbf{M}(A{\upharpoonright}\,(n+c))\leq 2^{c}\cdot 2^{-(n+c)}=2^{-n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus,
\{A\}
is deep in the extended sense, but the difference random
A
computes a member of
\{A\}
.

Being a
\Pi^{0}_{2}
singleton, there is a recursive predicate
R
such that
A
is the only sequence
X
satisfying
\forall n\exists m\,R(X,n,m)
. Define
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
by

f(n)\coloneq\text{least $m$ such that $\langle A,n,m\rangle\in R$}.

Then define
B\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
by
B(n)\coloneq\pi^{(2)}(A(n),f(n))
. Given
X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
i\in\{0,1\}
, let
(X)_{i}
be defined by
(X)_{i}(n)=(X(n))_{i}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
(\pi^{(2)}(n_{0},n_{1}))_{i}=n_{i}
. Then
\{B\}=\{X\mid\forall n\,R((X)_{0},n,(X)_{1}(n))\}
is
\Pi^{0}_{1}
and recursively homeomorphic to
\{A\}
.

Let
\Psi\colon\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
be the total recursive functional defined by
\Psi(X)\coloneq(X)_{0}
for
X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
. By
II.4.3
, there is a partial recursive functional
\Psi_{0}\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\mathbf{M}(\sigma)=\lambda(\Psi_{0}^{-1}(\sigma))
. Then define
\nu
to be the left r.e. continuous semimeasure corresponding to the partial recursive functional
\Psi\circ\Psi_{0}
, i.e.,

\nu(\sigma)=\lambda(\{Z\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid(\Psi\circ\Psi_{0})^{Z}\supseteq\sigma\}).

By the universality of
\mathbf{M}
, there is a
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\nu(\sigma)\leq c^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{M}(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
2^{c^{\prime}}\leq m
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}(\{B\}{\upharpoonright}\,(n+c+m))

\displaystyle=\lambda(\{Z\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\Psi_{0}^{Z}\supseteq B{\upharpoonright}\,(n+c+m)\})

\displaystyle\leq\lambda(\{Z\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid(\Psi\circ\Psi_{0})^{Z}\supseteq A{\upharpoonright}\,(n+c+m)\})

\displaystyle=\nu(A{\upharpoonright}\,(n+c+m))

\displaystyle\leq c^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{M}(A{\upharpoonright}\,(n+c+m))

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-n}.

Thus,
\{B\}
is a
\Pi^{0}_{1}
subset of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
which is deep in the extended sense, but the difference random
A
computes a member of
\{B\}
.
∎

Chapter III
Complexity and Fast-Growing Avoidance

Looking downward, the
\mathrm{COMPLEX}
and
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchies are closely coupled based on the following reseult of Kjos-Hanssen, Merkle, and Stephan:

Theorem
.

[
17
, Theorem 2.3.2]

Suppose
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
. The following are equivalent.

1.

X\in\mathrm{COMPLEX}
.

2.

There is a total recursive functional
\Psi\colon\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\Psi(X)\in\mathrm{DNR}
.

In terms of the mass problems
\mathrm{COMPLEX}
and
\mathrm{DNR}_{\mathrm{rec}}\coloneq\bigcup\{\mathrm{DNR}(p)\mid\text{$p$ recursive}\}
,
[
17
, Theorem 2.3.2]
implies:

Corollary III.0.1
.

\mathrm{COMPLEX}\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{DNR}_{\mathrm{rec}}
.

Proof.

Suppose
X\in\mathrm{DNR}_{\mathrm{rec}}
, so that there is an order function
p
such that
X\in\mathrm{DNR}_{p}
. Let
\Psi\colon\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\to p^{\mathbb{N}}
be a recursive homeomorphism. Then
\Psi(\Psi^{-1}(X))=X
shows that
\Psi^{-1}(X)\in\mathrm{COMPLEX}
by
[
17
, Theorem 2.3.2]
. This shows
\mathrm{COMPLEX}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{DNR}_{\mathrm{rec}}
.

Now suppose
X\in\mathrm{COMPLEX}
. By
[
17
, Theorem 2.3.2]
, there is a total recursive functional
\Psi\colon\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
Y\coloneq\Psi(X)\in\mathrm{DNR}
.
II.4.13
shows that there exist nondecreasing recursive functions
\psi\colon\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
and
j\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
Y{\upharpoonright}\,n=\psi(X{\upharpoonright}\,j(n))
. Let
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
be defined by
p(n)\coloneq\max\{\psi(\sigma)(n)\mid\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{j(n+1)}\}+1
, so that
Y(n)<p(n)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
p
is recursive, showing
Y
is recursively bounded, i.e.,
Y\in\mathrm{DNR}_{\mathrm{rec}}
. This shows
\mathrm{DNR}_{\mathrm{rec}}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}
.
∎

Let
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{rec}}\coloneq\bigcup\{\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mid\text{$p$ recursive}\}
.

Corollary III.0.2
.

\mathrm{COMPLEX}\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{rec}}
.

Proof.

By
III.0.1
, it suffices to show that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{rec}}\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{DNR}_{\mathrm{rec}}
.
Because
[
17
, Theorem 2.3.2]
(and by extension
III.0.1
) holds with
\mathrm{DNR}
defined with respect to any admissible enumeration
\varphi_{\bullet}
, we may assume without loss of generality that
\mathrm{DNR}
is interpreted with respect to an admissible enumeration
\varphi_{\bullet}
whose diagonal
\psi
is linearly universal, so that
\mathrm{DNR}_{\mathrm{rec}}\subseteq\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{rec}}
and hence
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{rec}}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{DNR}_{\mathrm{rec}}
. Conversely, given
X\in\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{rec}}
, there is a linearly universal partial recursive function
\tilde{\psi}
and an order function
p
such that
X\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\tilde{\psi}}(p)
. Because
\tilde{\psi}
is linearly universal, there exist
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\tilde{\psi}(an+b)\simeq\psi(n)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
Y\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
be defined by
Y(n)\coloneq X(an+b)
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
Y\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(\lambda n.p(an+b))\subseteq\mathrm{DNR}_{\mathrm{rec}}
, showing
\mathrm{DNR}_{\mathrm{rec}}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{rec}}
.
∎

Knowing
\mathrm{COMPLEX}\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{rec}}
alone does not reveal how the complexity and fast-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchies intertwine (if at all) when going downward, prompting the following questions.

Question III.0.3
.

Given a sub-identical order function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
, is there a fast-growing order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)
?

Question III.0.4
.

Given a fast-growing order function
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
, is there a sub-identical order function
g\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
such that
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
?

We will answer
III.0.3
and
III.0.4
in the affirmative, proving:

Theorem III.0.5
.

To each sub-identical order function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
there is a fast-growing order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)
, and to each fast-growing order function
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
there is a sub-identical order function
g\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
such that
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
.

III.0.5
will follow as a direct consequence of the following theorems, which additionally provide explicit bounds on
q
and
g
in terms of
f
and
p
, respectively.

Theorem
III.1.1
.

Suppose
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
is a sub-identical order function,
k
is a nonzero natural number, and
\varepsilon>0
is a rational number.
Then

\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}\bigl(\lambda n.\exp_{2}\bigl(f^{\mathrm{inv}}(\log_{2}n+\log_{2}^{2}n+\cdots+\log_{2}^{k-1}n+(1+\varepsilon)\log_{2}^{k}n)+1\bigr)\bigr)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f).

Theorem
III.2.1
.

Suppose
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is an order function, and let
r\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
be any order function such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}{r(n)/2^{n}}=\infty
. Then

\mathrm{COMPLEX}\Bigl(\bigl(\lambda n.\raisebox{1.02228pt}{\scalebox{.9}{$\displaystyle\sum$}}_{i<r(n)}{\lfloor\log_{2}p(i)\rfloor}\bigr)^{\mathrm{inv}}\Bigr)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p).

Less is known in the opposite direction.

Question III.0.6
.

Given a sub-identical order function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
, is there a fast-growing order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
?

Question III.0.7
.

Given a fast-growing order function
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
, is there a sub-identical order function
g\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)
?

While we have no general answer to
III.0.6
, we will give a partial affirmative answer to
III.0.7
.

Theorem
III.3.3
.

If
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is a fast-growing order function such that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real, then there exists a convex sub-identical order function
g
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)\neq\mathsf{MLR}
.

III.3.3
follows from a more general result:

Theorem
III.3.4
.

Suppose
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is a fast-growing order function such that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real. Then for any order function
\tilde{p}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
p(n)/\tilde{p}(n)\nearrow\infty
as
n\to\infty
and for which
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\tilde{p}(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real,

\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}\bigl(\lambda n.\log_{2}\tilde{p}\bigl(p^{\mathrm{inv}}(2^{n+1})-1\bigr)\bigr).

Moreover, such a
\tilde{p}
exists and for any such
\tilde{p}
the function
\lambda n.\log_{2}\tilde{p}(p^{\mathrm{inv}}(2^{n+1})-1)
is dominated by a convex sub-identical recursive function
g\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
.

III.1
Extracting Fast-Growing Avoidance from Complexity

In the direction
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)
, we have the following:

Theorem III.1.1
.

Suppose
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
is a sub-identical order function,
k
is a nonzero natural number, and
\varepsilon>0
is a rational number.
Then

\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}\bigl(\lambda n.\exp_{2}\bigl(f^{\mathrm{inv}}(\log_{2}n+\log_{2}^{2}n+\cdots+\log_{2}^{k-1}n+(1+\varepsilon)\log_{2}^{k}n)+1\bigr)\bigr)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f).

We prove
III.1.2
by deducing it from a more general technical result:

Theorem III.1.2
.

Suppose
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
is a sub-identical order function and
h,\hat{h}\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
are order functions such that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{2^{-h(n)}}<\infty
,
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{2^{-\hat{h}(n)}}<\infty
, and
\lim_{n\to\infty}{(h(n)-\hat{h}(n))}=\infty
. Then

\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}\bigl(\lambda n.\exp_{2}((f^{\mathrm{inv}}\circ h)(n)+1)\bigr)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f).

The role of the condition
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{2^{-h(n)}}<\infty
comes from the following result:

Lemma III.1.3
.

[
6
, Lemma 3.12.2]

Suppose
h\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
is recursive. The following are equivalent.

1.

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{2^{-h(n)}}<\infty
.

2.

There exists a
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\pfc(n)\leq h(n)+c
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof of
III.1.2
.

Suppose
X\in\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)
. Define
Y\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
by setting

Y(n)\coloneq\str^{-1}(X{\upharpoonright}\,(f^{\mathrm{inv}}\circ h)(n))

for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
We claim that
Y\in\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}\bigl(\lambda n.\exp_{2}((f^{\mathrm{inv}}\circ h)(n)+1)\bigr)
. Because
X
is
f
-complex, there is
c_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\pfc(\str Y(n))=\pfc(X{\upharpoonright}\,(f^{\mathrm{inv}}\circ h)(n))\geq f\bigl((f^{\mathrm{inv}}\circ h)(n)\bigr)-c_{0}\geq h(n)-c_{0}.

Suppose
\psi
is any partial recursive function. There is
c_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\pfc(\str\psi(n))\leq\pfc(\str n)+c_{1}
for all
n\in\dom\psi
. By
III.1.3
there is an
c_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\pfc(n)\leq\hat{h}(n)+c_{2}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, for every
n\in\dom\psi
we have

\pfc(\str\psi(n))\leq\hat{h}(n)+(c_{1}+c_{2}).

For every
n\in\dom\psi
such that
\psi(n)=Y(n)
we have

h(n)-c_{0}\leq\pfc(\str Y(n))=\pfc(\str\psi(n))\leq\hat{h}(n)+(c_{1}+c_{2}).

Because
\lim_{n\to\infty}{(h(n)-\hat{h}(n))}=\infty
, it follows that
|Y\cap\psi|
is finite. When
\psi
is linearly universal,
II.2.16
(b) shows that
Y\in\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
.

It remains to put a uniform upper bound on
Y
. By definition,

Y(n)=X(0)+X(1)\cdot 2+X(2)\cdot 2^{2}+\cdots+X((f^{\mathrm{inv}}\circ h)(n)-1)\cdot 2^{(f^{\mathrm{inv}}\circ h)(n)-1}+2^{(f^{\mathrm{inv}}\circ h)(n)}

so
Y(n)<\exp_{2}((f^{\mathrm{inv}}\circ h)(n)+1)
and hence
Y\in\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}\bigl(\lambda n.\exp_{2}((f^{\mathrm{inv}}\circ h)(n)+1)\bigr)
.
∎

Corollary III.1.4
.

Suppose
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
is a sub-identical order function and
h\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
is an order function such that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{2^{-h(n)}}
is a recursive real. Then

\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}\bigl(\lambda n.\exp_{2}((f^{\mathrm{inv}}\circ h)(n)+1)\bigr)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f).

Proof.

By
II.3.6
, there exists a fast-growing, recursive order function
\hat{h}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}{2^{h(n)}/2^{\hat{h}(n)}}=\infty
, or equivalently that
\lim_{n\to\infty}{(h(n)-\hat{h}(n))}=\infty
.
III.1.2
now applies.
∎

Using
III.1.2
and
III.1.4
we can prove the special case of
III.1.1
.

Proof of
III.1.1
.

Define
h\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
by setting
h(n)\coloneq\log_{2}n+\log_{2}^{2}n+\cdots+\log_{2}^{k-1}n+(1+\varepsilon)\log_{2}^{k}n
for
n\geq{}^{k}2
and
h(n)\coloneq\log_{2}(2\cdot{}^{2}2\cdot{}^{3}2\mathbin{\cdots}{}^{k}2)
for
n<{}^{k}2
. Then
2^{h(n)}=n\cdot\log_{2}n\cdot\log_{2}^{2}n\mathbin{\cdots}\log_{2}^{k-2}n\cdot(\log_{2}^{k-1}n)^{1+\varepsilon}
for all
n\geq{}^{k}2
and
2^{h(n)}=2\cdot{}^{2}2\cdot{}^{3}2\mathbin{\cdots}{}^{k}2
for
n<{}^{k}2
. By
II.3.11
,
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{2^{-h(n)}}
is a recursive real, so
III.1.4
applies.
∎

Remark III.1.5
.

Let
\varphi_{\bullet}
be an admissible enumeration and take
X\in\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f,c)
, where
f
is a sub-identical order function and
c\in\mathbb{N}
.
The element
Y\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}X
of
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
defined in the proof of
III.1.2
is shown to eventually avoid not just linearly universal partial recursive functions, but
all
partial recursive functions. Moreover, at what point
Y
begins avoiding
\varphi_{e}
is predictable: the partial function
\psi(e,n)\simeq\varphi_{e}(n)
is partial recursive, so there exists
d\in\mathbb{N}
(depending on
e
) such that
\pfc(\varphi_{e}(n))\leq h(n)+d
.
Then for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
h(n)-c>\hat{h}(n)+d
we have
Y(n)\nsimeq\varphi_{e}(n)
.

Example III.1.6
.

Fix a rational
\delta\in(0,1)
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and a rational
\varepsilon>0
. Let
f
be defined by
f(n)\coloneq\delta n
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, so
f^{\mathrm{inv}}(n)=\lceil\frac{1}{\delta}n\rceil
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Observe that

\displaystyle\exp_{2}(\lceil\delta^{-1}\cdot(\log_{2}n+\log_{2}^{2}n+\cdots+\log_{2}^{k}n+(1+\varepsilon)\log_{2}^{k+1}n)\rceil+1)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\exp_{2}(\delta^{-1}\cdot(\log_{2}n+\log_{2}^{2}n+\cdots+\log_{2}^{k}n+(1+\varepsilon)\log_{2}^{k+1}n)+2)

\displaystyle\quad\leq 4\sqrt[\delta]{n\cdot\log_{2}n\mathbin{\cdots}\log_{2}^{k-1}n\cdot(\log_{2}^{k}n)^{(1+\varepsilon)}}.

Thus,
III.1.1
implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}\Bigl(\lambda n.4\sqrt[\delta]{n\cdot\log_{2}n\mathbin{\cdots}\log_{2}^{k-1}n\cdot(\log_{2}^{k}n)^{(1+\varepsilon)}}\Bigr)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\delta).

Example III.1.7
.

Fix rational numbers
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
\varepsilon>0
. Let
f
be defined by
f(n)\coloneq n^{\alpha}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, so
f^{\mathrm{inv}}(n)=\lceil n^{1/\alpha}\rceil
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Observe that

\lceil((1+\varepsilon)\log_{2}n)^{1/\alpha}\rceil+1\leq((1+\varepsilon)\log_{2}n)^{1/\alpha}+2

so
III.1.1
implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}\bigl(\lambda n.4\exp_{2}\bigl(\sqrt[\alpha]{(1+\varepsilon)\log_{2}n}\bigr)\bigr)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.n^{\alpha}).

Example III.1.8
.

Fix rationals
\beta\in(0,\infty)
and
\varepsilon>0
. Let
f
be defined by
f(n)\coloneq\beta\log_{2}n
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, so
f^{\mathrm{inv}}(n)=\lceil 2^{n/\beta}\rceil
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Observe that

\lceil\exp_{2}((1+\varepsilon)(\log_{2}n)/\beta)\rceil+1\leq n^{(1+\varepsilon)/\beta}+2

so
III.1.1
implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}\bigl(\lambda n.4\exp_{2}(n^{(1+\varepsilon)/\beta})\bigr)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.\beta\log_{2}n).

III.2
Extracting Complexity from Fast-Growing Avoidance

In the direction
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
, we have the following:

Theorem III.2.1
.

Suppose
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is an order function, and let
r\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
be any order function such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}{r(n)/2^{n}}=\infty
. Then

\mathrm{COMPLEX}\Bigl(\bigl(\lambda n.\raisebox{1.02228pt}{\scalebox{.9}{$\displaystyle\sum$}}_{i<r(n)}{\lfloor\log_{2}p(i)\rfloor}\bigr)^{\mathrm{inv}}\Bigr)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p).

Proof.

Suppose
X\in\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
, and let
\psi
be a linearly universal partial recursive function for which
X\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
. Define
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
by setting
q(n)\coloneq\sum_{i<n}{\lfloor\log_{2}p(i)\rfloor}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Define
Y\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
to be the unique real for which
X(n)=\sum_{i<\lfloor\log_{2}p(n)\rfloor}{Y(q(n)+i)\cdot 2^{i}}
. We claim that there is
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
Y\in\mathrm{COMPLEX}((\lambda n.q(c\cdot 2^{n}))^{\mathrm{inv}})
.

Let
U\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to\{0,1\}^{\ast}
be the universal prefix-free machine for which
\pfc=\pfc_{U}
. Then define
\theta\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}
by setting

\theta(u,v,n)\simeq\sum_{i<\lfloor\log_{2}p(un+v)\rfloor}{U(\str n)(q(un+v)+i)\cdot 2^{i}}

for
u,v,n\in\mathbb{N}
.

\theta
is partial recursive, so
II.2.12
implies there are
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\psi(an+b)\simeq\sum_{i<\lfloor\log_{2}p(an+b)\rfloor}{U(\str n)(q(an+b)+i)\cdot 2^{i}}

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. By hypothesis,
X\cap\psi=\emptyset
, so for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\sum_{i<\lfloor\log_{2}p(an+b)\rfloor}{Y(q(an+b)+i)\cdot 2^{i}}\nsimeq\sum_{i<\lfloor\log_{2}p(an+b)\rfloor}{U(\str n)(q(an+b)+i)\cdot 2^{i}}.

In other words, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
either
U(\str n){\uparrow}
,
|U(\str n)|<q(an+b+1)
, or
U(\str n)
is incompatible with
Y{\upharpoonright}\,q(an+b+1)
. Note that for any
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
we have
\str^{-1}\sigma\leq 2^{|\sigma|+1}
. Thus, for
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\leq n}
we find that either
U(\sigma){\uparrow}
,
|U(\sigma)|<q(an+b+1)
, or
U(\sigma)
is incompatible with
Y{\upharpoonright}\,q(a\cdot 2^{n+1}+b+1)
. Consequently, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\pfc(Y{\upharpoonright}\,q(a\cdot 2^{n+1}+b+1))>n

so
Y\in\mathrm{COMPLEX}((\lambda n.q(a\cdot 2^{n+1}+b+1))^{\mathrm{inv}})
. If
\lim_{n\to\infty}{r(n)/2^{n}}=\infty
, then
\lambda n.q(a\cdot 2^{n+1}+b+1)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}\lambda n.(q\circ r)(n)
and hence
(\lambda n.(q\circ r)(n))^{\mathrm{inv}}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}(\lambda n.q(a\cdot 2^{n+1}+b+1))^{\mathrm{inv}}
. Thus,
Y\in\mathrm{COMPLEX}((\lambda n.(q\circ r)(n))^{\mathrm{inv}})
.
∎

Example III.2.2
.

Fix a rational
\varepsilon>0
, then let
p
and
r
be defined by
p(n)\coloneq 2^{n}
and
r(n)\coloneq\lfloor 2^{(1+\varepsilon)n}\rfloor
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
q
be as in proof of
III.2.1
, so that
q(n)=\frac{n(n-1)}{2}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
(q\circ r)(n)\leq 2^{2(1+\varepsilon)n}
for almost all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus,
III.2.1
implies

\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.\frac{1}{2+\varepsilon}\log_{2}n)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(\lambda n.2^{n}).

for any rational
\varepsilon>0
.

Remark III.2.3
.

Combining
Examples

III.2.2
and
III.1.8
shows that for any rational
\varepsilon>0
we have

\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(\lambda n.\exp_{2}(n^{2+\varepsilon}))\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.\frac{1}{2+\varepsilon}\log_{2}n)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(\lambda n.2^{n}).

III.3
Finding Complexity Above Fast-Growing Avoidance

In one of the upward directions, we can give a partial answer to
III.0.7
. In addition to being sub-identical, a common additional hypothesis put on order functions
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
is that
f
be convex (see, e.g.,
[
13
]
and
[
12
]
).

Definition III.3.1

(convex)
.

A nondecreasing function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
is
convex
if
f(n+1)-f(n)\leq 1
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

There is a simple characterization of convexity by putting functions into the form
\lambda n.n-j(n)
.

Proposition III.3.2
.

Suppose
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
is a nondecreasing function, and define
j\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
by
j(n)\coloneq n-f(n)
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
f
is convex if and only if
j
is nondecreasing.

Proof.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle f(n+1)-f(n)\leq 1

\displaystyle\iff((n+1)-j(n+1))-(n-j(n))\leq 1

\displaystyle\iff 1+j(n)-j(n+1)\leq 1

\displaystyle\iff j(n)\leq j(n+1).

∎

Theorem III.3.3
.

If
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is a fast-growing order function such that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real, then there exists a convex sub-identical order function
g
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)\neq\mathsf{MLR}
.

III.3.3
follows immediately from the following more general result, which makes use of the downward result
III.2.1
.

Theorem III.3.4
.

Suppose
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is a fast-growing order function such that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real. Then for any order function
\tilde{p}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
p(n)/\tilde{p}(n)\nearrow\infty
as
n\to\infty
and for which
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\tilde{p}(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real,

\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}\bigl(\lambda n.\log_{2}\tilde{p}\bigl(p^{\mathrm{inv}}(2^{n+1})-1\bigr)\bigr).

Moreover, such a
\tilde{p}
exists and for any such
\tilde{p}
the function
\lambda n.\log_{2}\tilde{p}(p^{\mathrm{inv}}(2^{n+1})-1)
is dominated by a convex sub-identical recursive function
g\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
.

It will be convenient to assume that
p
is strictly increasing. The following lemma shows that we may assume this without loss of generality.

Lemma III.3.5
.

If
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is a fast-growing order function such that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real, then there exists a fast-growing order function
\hat{p}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\hat{p}(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real,
\hat{p}
is strictly increasing, and
\hat{p}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}p
.

Proof.

Let
\langle\varepsilon_{n}\rangle_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be any strictly decreasing, recursive sequence of positive rational numbers such that
1<p(0)-\varepsilon_{0}
. Define
\hat{p}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
by setting

\hat{p}(n)=p(n)-\varepsilon_{n}

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

\hat{p}
is recursive.

Immediate.

\hat{p}
is strictly increasing.

Because
\langle\varepsilon_{n}\rangle_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is
strictly
decreasing, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\hat{p}(n)=p(n)-\varepsilon_{n}<p(n+1)-\varepsilon_{n+1}=\hat{p}(n+1).

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\hat{p}(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real.

We start by observing that

\frac{1}{\hat{p}(n)}=\frac{1}{p(n)}+\frac{1}{p(n)(\varepsilon_{n}^{-1}p(n)-1)}

and that

\frac{1}{p(n)(\varepsilon_{n}^{-1}p(n)-1)}\leq\frac{1}{p(n)}

for all sufficiently large
n
. By
II.3.8
, the recursiveness of
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
implies that

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\left(p(n)(\varepsilon_{n}^{-1}p(n)-1)\right)^{-1}}
is recursive. Thus,
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\hat{p}(n)^{-1}}
converges and

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\hat{p}(n)^{-1}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\left(p(n)(\varepsilon_{n}^{-1}p(n)-1)\right)^{-1}}

is recursive, being the sum of two recursive reals.

\hat{p}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}p
.

Immediate since
\varepsilon_{n}>0
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

∎

Lemma III.3.6
.

For any order function
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
and
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
,
p^{\mathrm{inv}}(n)>m
if and only if
p(m)<n
.

Proof.

Straight-forward.
∎

Proof of
III.3.4
.

By
III.3.5
, we may assume without loss of generality that
p
is strictly increasing. Moreover, the proof of
III.3.5
shows that the property that
\lim_{n\to\infty}{\frac{p(n)}{\tilde{p}(n+3)}}=\infty
is preserved. (Note that
II.3.6
implies there is such a
\tilde{p}
.)

Let
\overline{p}\colon{[0,\infty)}\to{[0,\infty)}
be the continuous extension of
p
which is defined linearly on the intervals
[n,n+1]
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, so its inverse
\overline{p}^{-1}\colon{[p(0),\infty)}\to{[0,\infty)}
exists.

Define
h
and
f
by setting
h(n)\coloneq\log_{2}\tilde{p}(n)
and
f(n)\coloneq\log_{2}\tilde{p}(p^{\mathrm{inv}}(2^{n+1})-1)
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{2^{-h(n)}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\tilde{p}(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real,
III.1.4
implies
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(\lambda n.\exp_{2}(f^{\mathrm{inv}}(h(n))+1))\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)
.
For almost all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, applying
III.3.6
shows

\displaystyle f^{\mathrm{inv}}(h(n))=f^{\mathrm{inv}}(\log_{2}\tilde{p}(n))

\displaystyle=\text{least $m$ such that $f(m)\geq\log_{2}\tilde{p}(n)$}

\displaystyle=\text{least $m$ such that $\log_{2}\tilde{p}(p^{\mathrm{inv}}(2^{m+1})-1)\geq\log_{2}\tilde{p}(n)$}

\displaystyle\leq\text{least $m$ such that $p^{\mathrm{inv}}(2^{m+1})-1\geq n$}

\displaystyle=\text{least $m$ such that $p^{\mathrm{inv}}(2^{m+1})>n$}

\displaystyle=\text{least $m$ such that $p(n)>2^{m+1}$}

\displaystyle=\text{least $m$ such that $\log_{2}p(n)-1>m$}

\displaystyle\leq\lfloor\log_{2}p(n)-1\rfloor+1

\displaystyle\leq\log_{2}p(n).

Thus,

\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(\lambda n.\exp_{2}(f^{\mathrm{inv}}(h(n))+1))\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f).

Let
\overline{\tilde{p}}
be the continuous extension of
\tilde{p}
which is defined linearly on the intervals
[n,n+1]
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, observing that
p^{\mathrm{inv}}(n)=\lceil\overline{p}^{-1}(n)\rceil
, we have

\displaystyle f(n)

\displaystyle=\log_{2}\tilde{p}(p^{\mathrm{inv}}(2^{n+1})-1)

\displaystyle=\log_{2}\tilde{p}(\lceil\overline{p}^{-1}(2^{n+1})\rceil-1)

\displaystyle\leq\log_{2}\overline{\tilde{p}}(\overline{p}^{-1}(2^{n+1}))

\displaystyle=\log_{2}\left(\overline{p}(\overline{p}^{-1}(2^{n+1}))\frac{\overline{\tilde{p}}(\overline{p}^{-1}(2^{n+1}))}{\overline{p}(\overline{p}^{-1}(2^{n+1}))}\right)

\displaystyle=n+1-\log_{2}\left(\frac{\overline{p}(\overline{p}^{-1}(2^{n+1}))}{\overline{\tilde{p}}(\overline{p}^{-1}(2^{n+1}))}\right)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Because
p(n)/\tilde{p}(n)\nearrow\infty
as
n\to\infty
, we additionally have
\overline{p}(x)/\overline{\tilde{p}}(x)\nearrow\infty
as
x\to\infty
, so
\log_{2}\left(\frac{\overline{\tilde{p}}(\overline{p}^{-1}(2^{n+1}))}{\overline{p}(\overline{p}^{-1}(2^{n+1}))}\right)-1
is a nondecreasing, unbounded function of
n
, and hence the function
g\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
defined by

g(n)\coloneq n+1-\log_{2}\left(\frac{\overline{p}(\overline{p}^{-1}(2^{n+1}))}{\overline{\tilde{p}}(\overline{p}^{-1}(2^{n+1}))}\right)

is convex by
III.3.2
. Since
g
is also an order function, this completes the proof.

∎

Proof of
III.3.3
.

By
III.3.4
, since such a
\tilde{p}
exists there is a convex sub-identical order function
g
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)
.

It remains to show that
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)\neq\mathsf{MLR}
.
[
13
, Corollary 4.3.5]
shows that there is an
X
which is strongly
g
-complex (hence
X\in\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)
) such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}{(\apc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)-g(n))}\neq\infty
. Suppose for the sake of a contradiction that
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)=\mathsf{MLR}
, so that
X
is Martin-Löf random. Then there is a
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\apc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\geq n-c
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
g
being sub-identical means that
\lim_{n\to\infty}{(n-g(n))}=\infty
, so

\liminf_{n}{(\apc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)-g(n))}\geq\liminf_{n}{(n-g(n)-c)}=\infty.

This implies
\lim_{n\to\infty}{(\apc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)-g(n))}=\infty
, a contradiction.
∎

II.3.11
allows us to answer
III.0.7
for a nice collection of fast-growing order functions which, at least in an aesthetic sense, approach the boundary between fast-growing and slow-growing:

Example III.3.7
.

Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
and a rational
\alpha\in(1,\infty)
, take any natural number
\ell>k
and any rational
\beta\in(1,\infty)
. Define
p
,
\tilde{p}
by setting

\displaystyle p(n)

\displaystyle\coloneq n\cdot\log_{2}n\mathbin{\cdots}\log_{2}^{k-1}n\cdot(\log_{2}^{k}n)^{\alpha},

\displaystyle\tilde{p}(m)

\displaystyle\coloneq m\cdot\log_{2}m\mathbin{\cdots}\log_{2}^{\ell-1}m\cdot(\log_{2}^{\ell}m)^{\beta}

for
n\geq{}^{k}2
(otherwise set
p(n)=2\cdot{}^{2}2\cdot{}^{3}2\mathbin{\cdots}{}^{k}2
) and
m\geq{}^{\ell}2
(otherwise set
\tilde{p}(m)=2\cdot{}^{2}2\cdot{}^{3}2\mathbin{\cdots}{}^{\ell}2
), respectively.

II.3.11
shows both series
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
and
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\tilde{p}(n)^{-1}}
converge to recursive reals. Moreover,

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\frac{p(n)}{\tilde{p}(n+3)}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}{\frac{(\log_{2}^{k}n)^{\alpha-1}}{\log_{2}^{k+1}(n+3)\cdot\log_{2}^{k+2}(n+3)\mathbin{\cdots}\log_{2}^{\ell-1}(n+3)\cdot(\log_{2}^{\ell}(n+3))^{\beta}}}=\infty.

Then
III.3.4
implies

\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.\log_{2}\tilde{p}(p^{\mathrm{inv}}(2^{n+1})-1)).

Although
III.3.3
answers
III.0.7
as stated for order functions
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
with recursive sum
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
,
g
being convex sub-identical does not immediately imply that
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)\mathrel{<_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathsf{MLR}
, though it is necessarily the case that
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)\neq\mathsf{MLR}
. This prompts a refinement of
III.0.7
:

Question III.3.8
.

Given a fast-growing order function
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
, is there a sub-identical order function
g\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)\mathrel{<_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathsf{MLR}
?

By examining the form of
g
in the proof of
III.3.4
, we can give a sufficient condition on
p
for there to be an affirmative answer to
III.3.8
.

Lemma III.3.9
.

Suppose
\alpha>1
and
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is a fast-growing order function. Then there exists a fast-growing order function
\hat{p}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\hat{p}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}p
and
\hat{p}(n+1)/\hat{p}(n)\leq\alpha
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover, if
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real, then
\hat{p}
can be chosen so that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\hat{p}(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real as well.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we may assume
\alpha
is rational. We define
\hat{p}
recursively as follows:

\displaystyle\hat{p}(0)

\displaystyle\coloneq p(0),

\displaystyle\hat{p}(n+1)

\displaystyle\coloneq\min\{\alpha\hat{p}(n),p(n+1)\}.

\hat{p}
is an order function dominated by
p
, so it just remains to show that
\hat{p}
is fast-growing and that if
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real then
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\hat{p}(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real.

Define
I\coloneq\{n\in\mathbb{N}\mid\hat{p}(n)=p(n)\}
. Note that
I
is a recursive nonempty subset of
\mathbb{N}
. We consider two cases:

Case 1:
I
finite.

Let
n_{0}=\max I
. By the definition of
\hat{p}
we then have
\hat{p}(n)=\alpha^{n-n_{0}}p(n_{0})
for all
n\geq n_{0}
. Thus,

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\hat{p}(n)^{-1}}=\sum_{n=0}^{n_{0}-1}{\hat{p}(n)^{-1}}+\frac{1}{p(n_{0})}\sum_{n=n_{0}}^{\infty}{\frac{1}{\alpha^{n-n_{0}}}}=\sum_{n=0}^{n_{0}-1}{\hat{p}(n)^{-1}}+\frac{1}{p(n_{0})}\cdot\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}<\infty.

Moreover, we quickly see that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\hat{p}(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real.

Case 2:
I
infinite.

Let
\langle n_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be the strictly increasing enumeration of
I
. Then

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\hat{p}(n)^{-1}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\left(1+\frac{1}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}+\cdots+\frac{1}{\alpha^{n_{k+1}-n_{k}-1}}\right)\frac{1}{p(n_{k})}}\leq\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\frac{1}{p(n_{k})}}\leq\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}<\infty.

Now suppose
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is a recursive real, so that there is a nondecreasing recursive sequence
\langle N_{m}\rangle_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\sum_{n=N_{m}}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}\leq 2^{-m}
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
j
be minimal such that
\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}\leq 2^{j}
. We now define a recursive sequence
\langle k_{m}\rangle_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
by setting
k_{m}
to be the least
k
such that
N_{m+j}\leq n_{k}
. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{n=n_{k_{m}}}^{\infty}{\hat{p}(n)^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=k_{m}}^{\infty}{\left(1+\frac{1}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}+\cdots+\frac{1}{\alpha^{n_{k+1}-n_{k}-1}}\right)\frac{1}{p(n_{k})}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}\sum_{k=k_{m}}^{\infty}{\frac{1}{p(n_{k})}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}\sum_{n=n_{k_{m}}}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}\sum_{n=N_{m+j}}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}\frac{1}{2^{m+j}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{m}}.

It follows that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\hat{p}(n)^{-1}}
is recursive.

∎

Proposition III.3.10
.

Suppose
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is an order function. If there exists a computable, nondecreasing function
h\colon{[1,\infty)}\to(0,\infty)
such that the series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\frac{1}{nh(n)}}
and
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\frac{h(p(n))}{p(n)}}
converge to recursive reals and
\sup_{x\in{[1,\infty)}}{h(2x)/h(x)}<\infty
, then there exists a convex sub-identical order function
g\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)\mathrel{<_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathsf{MLR}
.

Proof.

Suppose there is such a computable, nondecreasing
h\colon{[1,\infty)}\to(0,\infty)
and let
\tilde{p}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}
be defined by
\tilde{p}(n)\coloneq p(n)/h(p(n))
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. By
III.3.9
, we may assume without loss of generality that
p(n+1)/p(n)\leq 2
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

That
p
and
h
are nondecreasing and unbounded (
p
by hypothesis,
h
because
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\frac{1}{nh(n)}}<\infty
) implies
p(n)/\tilde{p}(n)=h(p(n))\nearrow\infty
as
n\to\infty
. With this choice of
\tilde{p}
, let
g
be as in the proof of
III.3.4
, i.e.,

g(n)\coloneq n+1-\log_{2}\left(\frac{\overline{p}(\overline{p}^{-1}(2^{n+1}))}{\overline{\tilde{p}}(\overline{p}^{-1}(2^{n+1}))}\right)

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, so
III.3.4
implies
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)
.

It remains to show that
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)\mathrel{<_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathsf{MLR}
. By
[
12
, Theorem 5.1]
, it suffices to show that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{2^{\lceil g(n)\rceil-n}}
is a recursive real. Because
2^{\lceil g(n)\rceil-n}\leq 2^{g(n)-n+1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
II.3.8
shows that it suffices to show that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{2^{g(n)-n}}
is a recursive real, or in other words that

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\frac{\overline{\tilde{p}}(\overline{p}^{-1}(2^{n+1}))}{\overline{p}(\overline{p}^{-1}(2^{n+1}))}}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{2^{n}\frac{\overline{\tilde{p}}(\overline{p}^{-1}(2^{n}))}{(2^{n})^{2}}}

is a recursive real. By the Cauchy Condensation Test,
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{2^{n}\frac{\overline{\tilde{p}}(\overline{p}^{-1}(2^{n}))}{(2^{n})^{2}}}
converges if and only if
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\frac{\overline{\tilde{p}}(\overline{p}^{-1}(n))}{n^{2}}}
converges; moreover, a simple analysis of the standard proof of the Cauchy Condesation Test and an appeal to
II.3.8
shows that we can replace both instances of ‘converges’ with ‘converges to a recursive real’ in the previous statement. Given
x\in{[1,\infty)}
, we compare
\overline{\tilde{p}}(x)
to
\frac{\overline{p}(x)}{h(\overline{p}(x))}
. Let
n=\lfloor x\rfloor
; then

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{\tilde{p}}(x)}{\overline{p}(x)/h(\overline{p}(x))}

\displaystyle=h(\overline{p}(x))\frac{\left(\frac{p(n+1)}{h(p(n+1))}-\frac{p(n)}{h(p(n))}\right)(x-n)+\frac{p(n)}{h(p(n))}}{(p(n+1)-p(n))(x-n)+p(n)}

\displaystyle=\frac{h(\overline{p}(x))}{h(p(n))}\frac{\left(\frac{h(p(n))}{h(p(n+1))}p(n+1)-p(n)\right)(x-n)+p(n)}{(p(n+1)-p(n))(x-n)+p(n)}

\displaystyle=\frac{h(\overline{p}(x))}{h(p(n))}\left(1+\frac{\left(\frac{h(p(n))}{h(p(n+1))}-1\right)p(n+1)(x-n)}{(p(n+1)-p(n))(x-n)+p(n)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{h(p(n+1))}{h(p(n))}\left(1+\frac{p(n+1)}{p(n)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 3\frac{h(2p(n))}{h(p(n))}

\displaystyle\leq 3\sup_{y\in{[1,\infty)}}{\frac{h(2y)}{h(y)}}

\displaystyle<\infty.

Thus, for some positive rational
\alpha
and almost all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\frac{\overline{\tilde{p}}(\overline{p}^{-1}(n))}{n^{2}}\leq\alpha\frac{\overline{p}(\overline{p}^{-1}(n))}{n^{2}h(\overline{p}(\overline{p}^{-1}(n)))}=\alpha\frac{1}{nh(n)}.

Since
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\frac{1}{nh(n)}}
is a recursive real by hypothesis,
III.3.4
implies
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{2^{n}\frac{\overline{\tilde{p}}(\overline{p}^{-1}(2^{n}))}{(2^{n})^{2}}}
is a recursive real, completing the proof.
∎

Example III.3.11
.

Fix a rational
\varepsilon>0
. Then

\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(\lambda n.n(\log_{2}n)^{2+\varepsilon})\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)\mathrel{<_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathsf{MLR}

for some convex sub-identical order function
g\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
as the hypotheses of
III.3.10
are satisfied with
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
and
h\colon{[1,\infty)}\to(0,\infty)
defined by

p(n)\coloneq\begin{cases}2&\text{if $n=0$ or $n=1$,}\\
n(\log_{2}n)^{2+\varepsilon}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad h(x)\coloneq\begin{cases}1&\text{if $x\in{[1,2)}$,}\\
(\log_{2}x)^{1+\varepsilon/2}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The extra hypothesis in
III.3.3
that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
be a recursive real prompts the question as to whether a full affirmative answer to
III.0.7
can be provided.

Question III.3.12
.

Does there exist a fast-growing order function
p
such that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{p(n)^{-1}}
is nonrecursive and for which there is no sub-identical order function
g
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)
?

Chapter IV
Complexity and Slow-Growing Avoidance

The results of
Chapter

III
explore the relationships between the complexity and fast-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchies, giving full affirmative answers to
III.0.3
and
III.0.4
which examine the downward direction while in the upward direction we only gave a partial affirmative answer to
III.0.7
and no answer to
III.0.6
. In this chapter, we address the following generalization of
III.0.6
.

Question IV.0.1
.

Given a sub-identical order function
f
, is there an order function
q
such that
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
?

In particular,
IV.0.1
drops the condition in
III.0.6
that
q
be fast-growing. Allowing
q
to be slow-growing, we give a partial affirmative answer to
IV.0.1
for
f
of the form
\lambda n.n-\sqrt{n}\cdot\Delta(n)
(and all sub-identical order functions dominated by a function of that form).

Theorem
IV.4.10
.

Given an order function
\Delta\colon\mathbb{N}\to[0,\infty)
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}{\Delta(n)/\sqrt{n}}=0
and any rational
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,

\mathrm{COMPLEX}\bigl(\lambda n.n-\sqrt{n}\cdot\Delta(n)\bigr)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}\bigl(\lambda n.\exp_{2}\bigl((1-\varepsilon)\Delta(\log_{2}\log_{2}n)\bigr)\bigr).

More generally,
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.n-\sqrt{n}\cdot\Delta(n))\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
for any order function
q
satisfying

q\left(\exp_{2}((1-\varepsilon)^{-1}\cdot[(n+1)^{2}-(n+1)\cdot\Delta((n+1)^{2})]\cdot\ell(n))\right)\leq\ell(n)

for almost all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\ell(n)=\exp_{2}\left((1-\varepsilon)[(n+1)\cdot\Delta((n+1)^{2})-n\cdot\Delta(n^{2})]\right)
.

Our approach in proving
IV.4.10
is motivated by techniques used by Greenberg & Miller to prove a connection between the
\mathrm{DNR}
hierarchy and effective Hausdorff dimension. The
effective Hausdorff dimension
of
Y\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is defined by
\dim(Y)\coloneq\sup\{\delta\mid Y\in\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\delta)\}
.

Theorem
.

[
9
, Theorem 4.9]

For all sufficiently slow-growing order functions
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(0,\infty)
and all
Z\in\mathrm{DNR}_{q}
, there is
Y\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
Y\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}Z
and
\dim(Y)=1
.

The statement that
\dim(Y)=1
is equivalent to the statement that
Y\in\bigcap_{\delta\in(0,1)\cap\mathbb{Q}}{\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\delta)}
, so
[
9
, Theorem 4.9]
gives a affirmative answer to
IV.0.1
when
f(n)\leq\delta n
for almost all
n
, where
\delta\in(0,1)
is rational.

IV.4.10
improves
[
9
, Theorem 4.9]
in two ways: first by strengthening
\dim(Y)=1
to
Y\in\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.n-\sqrt{n}\cdot\Delta(n))
and second by replacing ‘sufficiently slow-growing’ with an explicit bound.

One of the main ideas employed by Greenberg & Miller is to consider partial randomness in the space
h^{\mathbb{N}}=\{X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\forall n\,(X(n)<h(n))\}
for an order function
h\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
, show that
Z\in\mathrm{DNR}_{q}
computes an
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
for which
\dim^{h}(X)=1
(with respect to effective Hausdorff dimenion in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
) and then show that
X
computes a
Y\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
with
\dim(Y)=1
. The benefit of working with randomness in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
instead of randomness in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is that when constructing
Y
we may do so one entry at a time, whereas a direct construction of
X
would likely require we construct it in segments whose lengths grow as the construction progresses.

In
Section

IV.1
, we generalize our notions of partial randomness to
h^{\mathbb{N}}
.
In
Section

IV.2
, we give technical conditions under which partially random elements of
h^{\mathbb{N}}
compute partially random elements of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
.
In
Section

IV.3
, we examine
[
9
, Theorem 4.9]
, using our generalizations and performing a careful analysis of the growth rates inherent to the construction to prove
IV.3.1
:

Theorem
IV.3.1
.

For rationals
\alpha\in(1,\infty)
and
\beta\in(0,1/2)
, we have

\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.n-\alpha\sqrt{n}\log_{2}n)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(\lambda n.(\log_{2}n)^{\beta}).

More generally, if
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is an order function such that
q(2^{(3/2+\varepsilon)n^{2}})\leq n+1
for almost all
n
and some
\varepsilon>0
, then
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.n-\alpha\sqrt{n}\log_{2}n)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
.

Finally, in
Section

IV.4
, we prove a technical result (
IV.4.1
) which implies
IV.4.10
.

IV.1
Partial Randomness in
h^{\mathbb{N}}

The measure-theoretic structure on
h^{\mathbb{N}}
can be defined similarly to that of the fair-coin measure
\lambda
on
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
:

Definition IV.1.1
.

Given a finite prefix-free
S\subseteq h^{\ast}
, define

\mu_{h}(\left\llbracket S\right\rrbracket_{h})\coloneq\sum_{\sigma\in S}{\frac{1}{|h^{|\sigma|}|}}.

The above assignment defines a premeasure on the collection of finite unions of basic open sets, so more generally we let
\mu_{h}
be the outer measure induced by those assignments.

Convention IV.1.2
.

When
h
is understood, we write
\mu
for
\mu_{h}
and
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
for
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket_{h}
. Additionally, given
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
or a finite prefix-free
S\subseteq h^{\ast}
, we write
\mu(\sigma)
and
\mu(S)
for
\mu(\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket)
and
\mu(\left\llbracket S\right\rrbracket)
, respectively.

Let
f\colon\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
be a computable function. Two versions of partial randomness in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
will be relevant,
f
-randomness
and
strong
f
-randomness
.

A quantity that regularly appears is
\mu(\sigma)^{1/|\sigma|}
for
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
.

Definition IV.1.3
.

Define
\gamma\colon h^{\ast}\to[0,1]
by setting
\gamma(\sigma)\coloneq\mu(\sigma)^{1/|\sigma|}
when
\sigma\neq\langle\rangle
and
\gamma(\langle\rangle)\coloneq 1
.

Remark IV.1.4
.

One interpretation of
\gamma(\sigma)
is as the geometric mean of the conditional probabilities

\Prob(X(k)=\sigma(k)\mid X{\upharpoonright}\,k=\sigma{\upharpoonright}\,k)
for
k<|\sigma|
.

Within
\{0,1\}^{\ast}
we have
\gamma(\sigma)=1/2
for each
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}\setminus\{\langle\rangle\}
.

IV.1.1
f
-randomness and
f
-complexity

According to the measure-theoretic paradigm,
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is Martin-Löf random if no uniformly r.e. sequence
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of subsets of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
for which
\lambda(S_{i})\leq 2^{-i}
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
covers
X
. A direct translation suggests defining
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
to be Martin-Löf random in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
if no uniformly r.e. sequence
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of subsets of
h^{\mathbb{N}}
for which
\mu_{h}(S_{i})\leq 2^{-i}
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
covers
X
.

More generally, given a recursive function
f\colon\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
,
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is
f
-random if whenever
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a uniformly r.e. sequence of subsets of
\{0,1\}^{\ast}
such that
\sum_{\sigma\in S_{i}}{2^{-f(\sigma)}}\leq 2^{-i}
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, then
X\notin\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket_{2}}
. It is less obvious how to translate this to the realm of
h^{\mathbb{N}}
. One way would be to use it verbatim (though with
\dom f=h^{\ast}
now), but a consequence would be that Martin-Löf randomness in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
would correspond to
f(\sigma)\coloneq\log_{2}|h^{|\sigma|}|
. Another approach makes use of the observation that
\frac{1}{2}=\lambda(\sigma)^{1/|\sigma|}
for any
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}\setminus\{\langle\rangle\}
.

Definition IV.1.5

(direct
f
-weight in
h^{\ast}
)
.

Suppose
S\subseteq h^{\ast}
. The
direct
f
-weight (in
h^{\ast}
)

\dwt_{f}(S)
of
S
is defined by

\dwt_{f}(S)=\dwt_{f}^{h}(S)\coloneq\sum_{\sigma\in S}{\gamma(\sigma)^{f(\sigma)}}.

Definition IV.1.6

(
f
-randomness in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
)
.

An
f
-ML-test (in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
)
is a uniformly r.e. sequence
\langle S_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of subsets
S_{k}\subseteq h^{\ast}
such that
\dwt_{f}(S_{k})\leq 2^{-k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

An
f
-ML test
\langle S_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

covers

X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
if
X\in\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\left\llbracket S_{k}\right\rrbracket}
. If
X
is covered by an
f
-ML-test, then
X
is said to be
f
-null (in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
)
, and otherwise is
f
-random (in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
)
.

Like in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, there is an equivalent characterization of
f
-randomness in terms of complexity. The definition of a prefix-free machine
M\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to h^{\ast}
in
h^{\ast}
is analogous to that of a prefix-free machine
M\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to\{0,1\}^{\ast}
.

Definition IV.1.7

(prefix-free machines in
h^{\ast}
)
.

A
prefix-free machine
is a partial recursive function
M\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to h^{\ast}
such that
\dom M
is prefix-free.

A prefix-free machine
U
is
universal
if for any prefix-free machine
M
there exists
\rho\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
such that
U(\rho{}^{\frown}\tau)\simeq M(\tau)
for all
\tau\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
.

Proposition IV.1.8
.

There exists a universal prefix-free machine
U\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to h^{\ast}
.

Proof.

The proof given in
[
26
, Theorem 6.2.3]
easily generalizes to
h^{\ast}
.
∎

Definition IV.1.9

(prefix-free complexity in
h^{\ast}
)
.

Fix a universal prefix-free machine
U
. Then the

prefix-free complexity of
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
is defined by

\pfc(\sigma)=\pfc_{U}^{h}(\sigma)\coloneq\min\{|\tau|\mid U(\tau)\simeq\sigma\}.

Many of the standard properties or facts about prefix-free complexity in
\{0,1\}^{\ast}
continue to hold in
h^{\ast}
.

Proposition IV.1.10
.

1.

If
U
and
V
are universal prefix-free machines, then there exists
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
|\pfc_{U}(\sigma)-\pfc_{V}(\sigma)|\leq c
for all
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
.

2.

Kraft’s Inequality:
\sum_{\sigma\in h^{\ast}}{2^{-\pfc(\sigma)}}\leq 1
.

3.

KC Theorem: Suppose
\langle d_{k},\sigma_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a recursive sequence of pairs
\langle d_{k},\sigma_{k}\rangle\in\mathbb{N}\times h^{\ast}
such that
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{2^{-d_{k}}}\leq 1
. Then there is a recursive sequence
\langle\tau_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of binary strings such that
|\tau_{k}|=d_{k}
.

Consequently, there exists
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\pfc(\sigma_{k})\leq d_{k}+c
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

1.

The universality of
U
implies there is a
\rho\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
such that
U(\rho{}^{\frown}\sigma)\simeq V(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
. Fix
\tau\in h^{\ast}
. If
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
is such
V(\sigma)\simeq\tau
and
|\sigma|=\pfc_{V}(\tau)
, then
U(\rho{}^{\frown}\sigma)\simeq\tau
shows
\pfc_{U}(\tau)\leq\pfc_{V}(\tau)+|\rho|
, or equivalently
\pfc_{U}(\tau)-\pfc_{V}(\tau)\leq|\rho|
, with this final inequality being independent of
\tau
. By symmetry, there is a
\rho^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
such that
\pfc_{V}(\tau)-\pfc_{U}(\tau)\leq|\rho^{\prime}|
for all
\tau\in h^{\ast}
, so we may set
c=\max\{|\rho|,|\rho^{\prime}|\}
.

2.

To each
\tau\in h^{\ast}
there is a (not necessarily unique)
\sigma\in\dom U
such that
U(\sigma)=\tau
and
|\sigma|=\pwt(\tau)
. This observation shows that
\sum_{\tau\in h^{\ast}}{2^{-\pfc(\tau)}}\leq\sum_{\sigma\in\dom U}{2^{-|\sigma|}}
. Because
\dom U
is prefix-free, we have

\sum_{\tau\in h^{\ast}}{2^{-\pfc(\tau)}}\leq\sum_{\sigma\in\dom U}{2^{-|\sigma|}}\leq 1.

3.

The proof of
[
6
, Theorem 3.6.1]
shows that there exists a recursive sequence
\langle\tau_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of pairwise-incompatible binary strings
\tau_{k}
with
|\tau_{k}|=d_{k}
.

To show the “consequently” statement holds, define
M\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to h^{\ast}
by setting
M(\tau_{k})=\sigma_{k}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
M(\tau){\uparrow}
for all other
\tau
. Then
M
is a prefix-free machine, so there is
\rho\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
such that
U(\rho{}^{\frown}\tau)\simeq M(\tau)
for all
\tau\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
. In particular,
U(\rho{}^{\frown}\tau_{k})=\sigma_{k}
, so

\pfc(\sigma_{k})\leq|\rho{}^{\frown}\tau_{k}|=d_{k}+|\rho|.

∎

Given
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
, it makes sense to consider how the prefix-free complexity of an initial segment of
X
grows as a function of length. Within
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
,
X
is
f
-complex if there is
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\pfc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\geq f(n)-c
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. It can be shown
[
10
, Theorem 2.6]
that
f
-randomness and
f
-complexity in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
are equivalent, so a natural question is whether this continues to hold in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
once we define ‘
f
-complexity’ in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
. For the equivalence to go through an additional factor (depending on
h
) must be introduced.

Definition IV.1.11

(
f
-complexity in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
)
.

X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
is
f
-complex (in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
)
if there exists
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\pfc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\geq(\log_{1/2}\gamma(X{\upharpoonright}\,n))\cdot f(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)-c.

Adapting
[
10
, Theorem 2.6]
yields the equivalence between
f
-randomness and
f
-complexity in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Theorem IV.1.12
.

For all
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
,
X
is
f
-random in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
if and only if it is
f
-complex in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

For
i\in\mathbb{N}
, let
S_{i}=\{\sigma\in h^{\ast}\mid\pfc(\sigma)<(\log_{1/2}\gamma(X{\upharpoonright}\,n))\cdot f(\sigma)-i\}
. We claim that
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
forms an
f
-ML test. Indeed, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\dwt_{f}(S_{i})=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{i}}{\gamma(\sigma)^{f(\sigma)}}<\sum_{\sigma\in S_{i}}{\gamma(\sigma)^{(\pfc(\sigma)+i)\cdot\log_{\gamma(\sigma)}(1/2)}}=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{i}}{2^{-\pfc(\sigma)}\cdot 2^{-i}}\leq 2^{-i}.

where the final inequality follows from
IV.1.10
(b). If
X
is
f
-random, then
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
does not cover
X
, meaning there is an
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\pfc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\geq(\log_{1/2}\gamma(X{\upharpoonright}\,n))\cdot f(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)-i
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and hence
X
is
f
-complex.

Conversely, suppose
X
is not
f
-random, and let
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be an
f
-ML test covering
X
. Then

\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}{\sum_{\sigma\in S_{2i}}{\exp_{2}\bigl(-\bigl((\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma))\cdot f(\sigma)-(i+1)\bigl)\bigr)}}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}{2^{i}\gamma(\sigma)^{f(\sigma)}}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}{2^{i+1}\cdot 2^{-2i}}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}{2^{-i-1}}=1.

Suppose
g_{i}\colon\mathbb{N}\to S_{2i}
is a recursive surjection for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and let
g\colon\mathbb{N}\to\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{S_{2i}}
be defined by
g(\pi^{(2)}(i,j))\coloneq g_{i}(j)
. Write
\sigma_{k}=g(k)
and
d_{k}=\lceil(\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma_{k}))\cdot f(\sigma_{k})-(i+1)\rceil
. Then
IV.1.10
(c) implies there exists
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\pfc(\sigma_{k})\leq\lceil(\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma_{k}))\cdot f(\sigma_{k})-(i+1)\rceil+c\leq(\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma_{k}))\cdot f(\sigma_{k})-i+c

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Because
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
covers
X
,
\langle S_{2i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
does as well. Thus, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists an
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\pfc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\leq(\log_{1/2}\gamma(X{\upharpoonright}\,n))\cdot f(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)-i+c
, so
X
is not
f
-complex.
∎

Corollary IV.1.13
.

There exists a
universal
f
-ML test
, i.e., an
f
-ML test
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
is
f
-random if and only if
X
is not covered by
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

The proof of
IV.1.12
shows that setting
S_{i}=\{\sigma\in h^{\ast}\mid\pfc(\sigma)<(\log_{1/2}\gamma(X{\upharpoonright}\,n))\cdot f(\sigma)-i\}
yields a universal
f
-ML test.
∎

Corollary IV.1.14
.

Suppose
f(\sigma)=\tilde{f}(\sigma)
for almost all
\sigma
. Then
f
-randomness in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
is equivalent to
\tilde{f}
-randomness in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

Suppose
f(\sigma)=\tilde{f}(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
for which
|\sigma|>N
. Let
d=\max_{\sigma\in h^{\ast},|\sigma|\leq N}{\pfc(\sigma)}
. Then
\pfc(\sigma)\geq(\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma))\cdot f(\sigma)-c
if and only if
\pfc(\sigma)\geq(\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma))\cdot\tilde{f}(\sigma)-c
for all
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
. It follows that
f
-complexity and
\tilde{f}
-complexity are equivalent, and so
IV.1.12
shows
f
-randomness and
\tilde{f}
-randomness are equivalent.
∎

Prior to defining
f
-randomness in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
, an alternate definition was suggested in which the definition of direct
f
-weight was unmodified when passing from
\{0,1\}^{\ast}
to
h^{\ast}
aside from changing the domain of
f
. Likewise, an alternative definition of
f
-complexity can be given in which the factor
\log_{1/2}{\gamma(\sigma)}
is removed, more closely resembling
f
-complexity in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Passing between these alternative definitions can be done in a uniform manner:

Proposition IV.1.15
.

Suppose
f,g\colon h^{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
are computable and
g(\sigma)=(\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma))\cdot f(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
.

1.

X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
is
f
-random if and only if there exists no uniformly r.e. sequence
\langle S_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}{2^{-g(\sigma)}}\leq 1/2^{k}
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
X\in\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\left\llbracket S_{k}\right\rrbracket}
.

2.

X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
is
f
-complex if and only if there exists a
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\pfc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\geq g(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)-c
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

Straight-forward.
∎

IV.1.2
Strong
f
-Randomness

A related variant of partial randomness can be defined similarly. First, from the measure-theoretic paradigm:

Definition IV.1.16

(prefix-free
f
-weight in
h^{\ast}
)
.

The
prefix-free
f
-weight
of a set of strings
S\subseteq h^{\ast}
is defined by

\pwt_{f}(S)\coloneq\sup\{\dwt_{f}(A)\mid\text{prefix-free $A\subseteq S$}\}.

Definition IV.1.17

(strong
f
-randomness in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
)
.

A
weak
f
-ML-test (in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
)
is a uniformly r.e. sequence
\langle A_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of subsetes
A_{k}\subseteq h^{\ast}
such that
\pwt_{f}(A_{k})\leq 2^{-k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

A weak
f
-ML-test
\langle A_{k}\rangle_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

covers

X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
if
X\in\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\left\llbracket A_{k}\right\rrbracket}
. If
X
is covered by no weak
f
-ML-test, then
X
is
strongly
f
-random (in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
)
.

Like in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, strong
f
-randomness in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
is associated with an analog of a priori complexity in
h^{\ast}
.

Definition IV.1.18

(continuous semimeasure on
h^{\ast}
)
.

A
continuous semimeasure on
h^{\ast}
is a function
\nu\colon h^{\ast}\to[0,1]
such that
\nu(\langle\rangle)=1
and for every
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
,

\nu(\sigma)\geq\sum_{i\in<h(|\sigma|)}{\nu(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)}.

A continuous semimeasure
\nu
is
left recursively enumerable
, or
left r.e.
, if it is left r.e. as a function
h^{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
. A left r.e. continuous semimeasure
\nu
is
universal
if for every left r.e. continuous semimeasure
\xi
on
h^{\ast}
there exists
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\xi(\sigma)\leq c\cdot\nu(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
.

Proposition IV.1.19
.

There exists a universal left r.e. semimeasure
\mathbf{M}
on
h^{\ast}
.

Proof.

The proof given in
[
6
, Theorem 3.16.2]
easily generalizes to
h^{\ast}
.
∎

Definition IV.1.20

(a priori complexity in
h^{\ast}
)
.

Fix a universal left r.e. semimeasure
\mathbf{M}
. The
a priori complexity
of a string
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
is defined by

\apc(\sigma)=\apc_{\mathbf{M}}(\sigma)\coloneq-\log_{2}\mathbf{M}(\sigma).

Akin to the well-definedness of prefix-free complexity, if
\mathbf{N}
were another universal left r.e. semimeasure, then
\apc_{\mathbf{M}}
and
\apc_{\mathbf{N}}
differ by at most a constant.

Definition IV.1.21

(strong
f
-complexity in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
)
.

X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
is
strongly
f
-complex (in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
)
if there exists a
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\apc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\geq(\log_{1/2}\gamma(X{\upharpoonright}\,n))\cdot f(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)-c.

We will show that strong
f
-complexity is equivalent to strong
f
-randomness. Before doing so, we introduce a third approach to defining strong
f
-randomness/complexity, this time in terms of supermartingales as in the unpredictability paradigm.

Definition IV.1.22

(supermartingale)
.

A
supermartingale (over
h^{\ast}
)
is a function
d\colon h^{\ast}\to{[0,\infty)}
such that

\sum_{i<h(|\sigma|)}{d(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)}\leq h(|\sigma|)d(\sigma)

for all
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
.

A supermartinagle
d
is
left recursively enumerable
, or
left r.e.
, if it is left r.e. as a function
h^{\ast}\to{[0,\infty)}
.

Definition IV.1.23

(
f
-success)
.

Suppose
d
is a left r.e. supermartingale and
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
.
d
is said to
f
-succeed on
X
if

\limsup_{n}{\left(d(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\cdot\gamma(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)^{n-f(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)}\right)}=\infty.

The following lemma reveals the close connection between continuous semimeasures
\nu
and supermartingales
d
such that
d(\langle\rangle)=1
.

Lemma IV.1.24
.

Given
\nu\colon h^{\ast}\to[0,1]
, let
d_{\nu}\colon h^{\ast}\to{[0,\infty)}
be defined by
d_{\nu}(\sigma)\coloneq|h^{|\sigma|}|\cdot\nu(\sigma)
for
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
.

1.

\nu
is left r.e. if and only if
d_{\nu}
is left r.e.

2.

\nu
is a continuous semimeasure if and only if
d_{\nu}
is a supermartingale.

3.

\nu
is a universal left r.e. continuous semimeasure if and only if
d_{\nu}
is a
universal left r.e. supermartinagle
, in the sense that if
d
were another left r.e. supermartingale then there is a
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
d(\sigma)\leq c\cdot d_{\nu}(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
.

Proof.

1.

This follows from the fact that
h
is recursive.

2.

Given
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
, we have

\sum_{i<h(|\sigma|)}{d_{\nu}(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)}=\sum_{i<h(|\sigma|)}{|h^{|\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle|}|\cdot\nu(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)}=|h^{|\sigma|+1}|\cdot\sum_{i<h(|\sigma|)}{\nu(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)}=h(|\sigma|)\cdot\left(|h^{|\sigma|}|\cdot\sum_{i<h(|\sigma|)}{\nu(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)}\right).

By comparing the first and last expressions with the definitions of what it means for
d_{\nu}
to be a supermartingale or for
\nu
to be a continuous semimeasure shows that
d_{\nu}
is a supermartinagle if and only if
\nu
is a continuous semimeasure.

3.

Straight-forward.

∎

Lemma IV.1.25
.

Suppose
S
and
T
are subsets of
h^{\ast}
.

1.

If
S\subseteq T
, then
\dwt_{f}(S)\leq\dwt_{f}(T)
and
\pwt_{f}(S)\leq\pwt_{f}(T)
.

2.

\dwt_{f}(S\cup T)=\dwt_{f}(S)+\dwt_{f}(T)-\dwt_{f}(S\cap T)
.

3.

\pwt_{f}(S\cup T)\leq\pwt_{f}(S)+\pwt_{f}(T)
, with equality if the strings in
S
and
T
are pairwise incompatible.

Proof.

1.

Straight-forward.

2.

Straight-forward.

3.

If
P\subseteq S\cup T
is prefix-free, then
P\cap S
and
P\cap T
are prefix-free subsets of
S
and
T
, respectively, so

\dwt_{f}(P)\leq\dwt_{f}(P\cap S)+\dwt_{f}(P\cap T)\leq\pwt_{f}(S)+\pwt_{f}(T).

Taking the supremum among all prefix-free
P\subseteq S\cup T
yields
\pwt_{f}(S\cup T)\leq\pwt_{f}(S)+\pwt_{f}(T)
.

If the strings in
S
and
T
are pairwise incompatible, then given prefix-free subsets
A\subseteq S
and
B\subseteq T
,
A\cap B=\emptyset
and
A\cup B
is a prefix-free subset of
S\cup T
, so

\dwt_{f}(A)+\dwt_{f}(B)=\dwt_{f}(A\cup B)\leq\pwt_{f}(S\cup T).

Taking the supremum among all prefix-free
A\subseteq S
and
B\subseteq T
yields
\pwt_{f}(S)+\pwt_{f}(T)\leq\pwt_{f}(S\cup T)
.

∎

Theorem IV.1.26
.

Suppose
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
. The following are equivalent.

1.

X
is strongly
f
-random.

2.

X
is strongly
f
-complex.

3.

d^{h}
does not
f
-succeed on
X
, where
d^{h}
is the universal left r.e. supermartingale corresponding to
\mathbf{M}
as in
IV.1.24
.

4.

No left r.e. supermartingale
f
-succeeds on
X
.

Proof.

(i)\iff(ii)

Suppose
X
is strongly
f
-random. Let
S_{i}=\{\sigma\in h^{\ast}\mid\apc(\sigma)<(\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma))\cdot f(\sigma)-i\}
. If
P\subseteq S_{i}
is prefix-free, then

\dwt_{f}(P)=\sum_{\sigma\in P}{\gamma(\sigma)^{f(\sigma)}}\leq\sum_{\sigma\in P}{\gamma(\sigma)^{(\apc(\sigma)+i)\cdot(\log_{\gamma(\sigma)}1/2)}}\leq\frac{1}{2^{i}}\sum_{\sigma\in P}{\mathbf{M}(\sigma)}\leq\frac{1}{2^{i}}\mathbf{M}(\langle\rangle)\leq\frac{1}{2^{i}}.

Thus,
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
forms a weak
f
-ML test. Because
X
is strongly
f
-random,
X
is not covered by
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and so there is an
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that
X\notin\left\llbracket S_{i}\right\rrbracket
, i.e., for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\apc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\geq(\log_{1/2}\gamma(X{\upharpoonright}\,n))\cdot f(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)-i
, so
X
is strongly
f
-complex.

If
X
is not strongly
f
-random, then there is a weak
f
-ML test
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
which covers
X
. Uniformly in
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
\nu_{i}
be defined by
\nu_{i}(\sigma)=\pwt_{f}(\{\tau\in S_{i}\mid\tau\supseteq\sigma\})
.
\nu_{i}
is a continuous semimeasure; using
IV.1.25
we have

\displaystyle\nu_{i}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\pwt_{f}(\{\tau\in S_{i}\mid\tau\supseteq\sigma\})

\displaystyle\geq\pwt_{f}(\{\tau\in S_{i}\mid\tau\supset\sigma\})

\displaystyle=\pwt_{f}\left(\bigcup_{j<h(|\sigma|)}{\{\tau\in S_{i}\mid\tau\supseteq\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle j\rangle\}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j<h(|\sigma|)}{\pwt_{f}(\{\tau\in S_{i}\mid\tau\supseteq\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle j\rangle\})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j<h(|\sigma|)}{\nu_{i}(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle j\rangle)}.

That
\nu_{i}
is left r.e. follows from the fact that
S_{i}
is r.e.
Observe that for
\tau\in S_{i}
we have
\dwt_{f}(\tau)\leq\nu_{i}(\tau)
. Because
\nu_{i}(\langle\rangle)=\pwt_{f}(S_{i})\leq 2^{-i}
for each
i
, the map
\overline{\nu}\colon h^{\ast}\to[0,1]
defined by

\overline{\nu}(\sigma)\coloneq\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}{2^{i}\nu_{2i}(\sigma)}

for
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
is a left r.e. semimeasure, and hence there is a
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\overline{\nu}(\sigma)<c\cdot\mathbf{M}(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
. Then for
\sigma\in S_{2i}
, we have

\exp_{2}(i-(\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma))\cdot f(\sigma))=2^{i}\dwt_{f}(\sigma)\leq 2^{i}\nu_{2i}(\sigma)\leq\overline{\nu}(\sigma)<c\cdot\mathbf{M}(\sigma)=\exp_{2}(-(\apc(\sigma)+\log_{1/2}c))

and hence

\apc(\sigma)+i+\log_{1/2}c<(\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma))\cdot f(\sigma).

Being covered by
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and hence by
\langle S_{2i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
as well,
X
is not strongly
f
-complex.

(ii)\iff(iii)

Let
d^{h}
be the universal left r.e. supermartingale corresponding to
\mathbf{M}
, as in
IV.1.24
. Now observe that for any
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle d^{h}(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\cdot\mu(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)^{1-f(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)/n}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{M}(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\cdot\mu(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)^{-1}\cdot\mu(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)^{1-f(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)/n}

\displaystyle=\exp_{2}(-(\apc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)-(\log_{1/2}\gamma(X{\upharpoonright}\,n))\cdot f(X{\upharpoonright}\,n))).

Thus,

\limsup_{n}{d_{0}(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\cdot\mu(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)^{1-f(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)/n}}=\infty\iff\forall c\exists n\,(\apc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)<(\log_{1/2}\gamma(X{\upharpoonright}\,n))\cdot f(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)-c.

In other words,
d^{h}

f
-succeeds on
X
if and only if
X
is not strongly
f
-complex.

(iii)\iff(iv)

If no left r.e. supermartingale
f
-succeeds on
X
, then in particular
d^{h}
does not
f
-succeed on
X
. Conversely, if
d^{h}
does not
f
-succeed on
X
, then the universality of
d^{h}
shows that no left r.e. supermartingale
f
-succeeds on
X
.

∎

Corollary IV.1.27
.

There exists a
universal weak
f
-ML test
, i.e., a weak
f
-ML test
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
is strongly
f
-random in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
if and only if
X
is not covered by
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

The proof of
IV.1.26
shows that letting
S_{i}=\{\sigma\in h^{\ast}\mid\apc(\sigma)<(\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma))\cdot f(\sigma)-i\}
yields a universal weak
f
-ML test.
∎

Corollary IV.1.28
.

Suppose
f(\sigma)=\tilde{f}(\sigma)
for almost all
\sigma
. Then strong
f
-randomness is equivalent to strong
\tilde{f}
-randomness.

Proof.

Suppose
f(\sigma)=\tilde{f}(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
for which
|\sigma|>N
. Let
c=\max_{\sigma\in h^{\ast},|\sigma|\leq N}{\apc(\sigma)}
. Then for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\apc(\sigma)\geq(\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma))\cdot f(\sigma)-c-i
if and only if
\apc(\sigma)\geq(\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma))\cdot\tilde{f}(\sigma)-c-i
for all
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
. It follows that strong
f
-complexity and strong
\tilde{f}
-complexity are equivalent, and so
IV.1.26
shows strong
f
-randomness and strong
\tilde{f}
-randomness are equivalent.
∎

Remark IV.1.29
.

All of the above results hold with
\mu
replaced by any computable measure on
h^{\mathbb{N}}
for which
\mu(\sigma)>0
for all
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
, with one adjustment – a supermartingale
d

f
-succeeds on
X
with respect to
\mu
if and only if

\limsup_{n}{\left(d(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\cdot\gamma(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)^{-f(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)}\cdot|h^{n}|^{-1}\right)}=\infty.

IV.1.3
Relationships between randomness notions

[
9
, Proposition 2.5]
and
[
10
, Theorem 3.5]
show that (in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
) if
g
grows sufficiently faster than
f
, then
g
-randomness implies strong
f
-randomness. This prompts the following question:

Question IV.1.30
.

Suppose
h
is an order function and
f\colon h^{\ast}\to{[0,\infty)}
is a nondecreasing computable function such that
\lim_{x\to\infty}{(x-f(x))}=\infty
. For what nondecreasing computable functions
g\colon h^{\ast}\to{[0,\infty)}
such that
g
-randomness implies strong
f
-randomness?

Although we will not make use of it, an analog of
[
9
, Proposition 2.5]
and
[
10
, Theorem 3.5]
holds for
h^{\mathbb{N}}
with the growth rate of
h
factoring heavily into how much faster
g
must grow than
f
.

A simplifying assumption we will make is that
f
is of the form
f\colon h^{\ast}\to{[0,\infty)}
.

Proposition IV.1.31
.

Suppose
f\colon h^{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
is a recursive function. Then there exists a recursive function
\hat{f}\colon h^{\ast}\to{[0,\infty)}
such that (strong)
f
-randomness is equivalent to (strong)
\hat{f}
-randomness.

Proof.

Let
\hat{f}(\sigma)\coloneq\max\{0,f(\sigma)\}
. For
K
representing either
\pfc
or
\apc
,
K(\sigma)\geq 0
for all
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
, so
K(\sigma)\geq(\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma))\cdot f(\sigma)-c
implies

K(\sigma)\geq\max\{0,(\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma))\cdot f(\sigma)-c\}\geq(\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma))\cdot\max\{0,f(\sigma)\}-c=(\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma))\cdot\hat{f}(\sigma)-c.

Conversely, if
K(\sigma)\geq(\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma))\cdot\hat{f}(\sigma)-c
, then

K(\sigma)\geq(\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma))\cdot\hat{f}(\sigma)-c\geq(\log_{1/2}\gamma(\sigma))\cdot f(\sigma)-c.

This suffices to show that (strong)
f
-randomness is equivalent to (strong)
\hat{f}
-randomness.
∎

Notation IV.1.32
.

Let
\gamma_{0}=\gamma(\langle\rangle)=1
and
\gamma_{n}=\gamma(0^{n})=|h^{n}|^{-1/n}
for
n\geq 1
.

Convention IV.1.33
.

f
will denote a computable, unbounded function of the form
f\colon h^{\ast}\to{[0,\infty)}
such that for every
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
the sequence
\langle\gamma(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)^{f(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)}\rangle_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is eventually decreasing.
g
and variations of
f
and
g
will similarly denote such functions unless otherwise specified.

Proposition IV.1.34
.

Suppose
f,g\colon h^{\ast}\to{[0,\infty)}
are recursive functions and there exists a nondecreasing function
j\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
such that
g(\sigma)\geq f(\sigma)+j(|\sigma|)
for all
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
and for which
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\gamma_{n}^{j(n)}}<\infty
. Then
g
-randomness implies strong
f
-randomness.

Proof.

We start by showing that there is a
c>0
such that
\dwt_{g}(S)\leq c\cdot\pwt_{f}(A)
for all
A\subseteq h^{\ast}
. This allows us to convert a weak
f
-ML test
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
into a
g
-ML test by taking a tail of
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
. Noting that
S\cap h^{n}
is a prefix-free subset of
S
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\dwt_{g}(S)=\sum_{\sigma\in S}{\gamma(\sigma)^{g(\sigma)}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\sum_{\sigma\in S\cap h^{n}}{\gamma(\sigma)^{f(\sigma)+j(n)}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\gamma_{n}^{j(n)}}\cdot\sum_{\sigma\in S\cap h^{n}}{\gamma(\sigma)^{f(\sigma)}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\gamma_{n}^{j(n)}}\cdot\pwt_{f}(S)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\gamma_{n}^{j(n)}}\right)\cdot\pwt_{f}(S).

Thus, we may let
c=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\gamma_{n}^{j(n)}}
, which is finite by hypothesis.
∎

Corollary IV.1.35
.

Fix a rational
\varepsilon>0
, a
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
, and a computable
f\colon\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to{[0,\infty)}
. Then for any computable
g\colon\{0,1\}^{\ast}\to{[0,\infty)}
satisfying

f(\sigma)+(\log_{\gamma_{|\sigma|}}1/2)\cdot\bigl(\log_{2}|\sigma|+\log_{2}^{2}|\sigma|+\cdots+\log_{2}^{k-1}|\sigma|+(1+\varepsilon)\log_{2}^{k}|\sigma|\bigr)\leq g(\sigma)

for all
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
, if
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
is
g
-random, then
X
is strongly
f
-random.

Remark IV.1.36
.

IV.1.34
can be generalized to the case of an arbitrary computable measure
\mu
for which
\mu(\sigma)>0
for all
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
by requiring
j
to satisfy
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\gamma_{n}^{h(n)}}<\infty
, where
\gamma_{n}=\max_{\sigma\in h^{n}}{\gamma(\sigma)}
.

IV.2
Randomness in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
versus
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}

Algorithmic randomness and complexity is traditionally done within
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, and our use of partial randomness in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
for an order function
h
is ultimately a tool in proving facts about partial randomness in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
. To facilitate that, we want to translate randomness in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
to randomness in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
. In general,
h(n)
is not necessarily a power of two for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. For that reason, if we wish to relate randomness in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
with randomness in
\{0,1\}^{\ast}
, it is more convenient to pass through
[0,1]
on the way to
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
where we may associate a string
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
with a closed subinterval of
[0,1]
having rational endpoints.

IV.2.1
Randomness in
[0,1]
versus
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}

For the translation to and from
[0,1]
and
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, the ‘obvious’ approach works.

Definition IV.2.1
.

Suppose
x\in[0,1]
.
\bin(x)
is the unique infinite binary sequence such that
x=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}{\bin(x)(i)\cdot 2^{-i-1}}
which does not end in an infinite sequence of
1
’s except for in the case where
x=1
.

Suppose
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
.
0.X
denotes the real number
\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}{X(i)\cdot 2^{-i-1}}
. Given
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
,
0.\sigma
denotes the real number
\sum_{i=0}^{|\sigma|-1}{X(i)\cdot 2^{-i-1}}
.

The map
X\mapsto 0.X
is a surjection but not an injection, with
x=0.X=0.Y
for distinct
X
and
Y
if and only if
x
is a dyadic rational in
(0,1)
and
X
and
Y
are the binary representations of
x
, one ending in an infinite sequence of
0
’s and the other in an infinite sequence of
1
’s.

Definition IV.2.2
.

Suppose
I\subseteq[0,1]
is a closed interval with rational endpoints. The
norm
of
I
is defined by

|I|\coloneq-\log_{2}(\max I-\min I)=-\log_{2}\lambda(I).

\mathcal{J}
denotes the set of closed subintervals
I\subseteq[0,1]
with rational endpoints for which
|I|\in\mathbb{N}
(equivalently,
\max I-\min I
is a nonnegative power of
1/2
). Given
I\in\mathcal{J}
, a
code
for
I
is a
4
-tuple
\langle a,b,c,d\rangle\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
where
[a/b,c/d]=I
.

Given
S\subseteq\mathcal{J}
, write
\left\llbracket S\right\rrbracket\coloneq\bigcup{S}
.
S\subseteq\mathcal{J}
is
recursively enumerable
, or
r.e.
, if the set of codes of elements of
S
is recursively enumerable. A sequence
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of subsets of
\mathcal{J}
is
uniformly r.e.
if the set of all
5
-tuples
\langle a,b,c,d,i\rangle
where
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
\langle a,b,c,d\rangle
is a code for an element of
S_{i}
is r.e.

To make sense of direct
f
-weight of an interval
I\in\mathcal{J}
, we will require that
f
be
length invariant
, i.e.,
|\sigma|=|\tau|
implies
f(\sigma)=f(\tau)
for
\sigma,\tau\in h^{\ast}
. Thus, the map
n\mapsto f(0^{n})
completely characterizes
f
. Considering only the length invariant case allows us to use
f
regardless of whether we are working within
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
,
h^{\mathbb{N}}
, or
[0,1]
.

Convention IV.2.3
.

Unless otherwise specified, from this point forward we will assume that the ‘
f
’ in (strong)
f
-randomness is of the form
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
.

Definition IV.2.4

(direct
f
-weight &
f
-randomness in
[0,1]
)
.

Given
S\subseteq\mathcal{J}
, its
direct
f
-weight
is defined by

\dwt_{f}(S)\coloneq\sum_{I\in S}{2^{-f(|I|)}}=\sum_{I\in S}{(\lambda(I)^{|I|})^{f(|I|)}}.

An
f
-ML test (in
[0,1]
)
is a uniformly r.e. sequence
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of subsets of
\mathcal{J}
such that
\dwt_{f}(S_{i})\leq 1/2^{i}
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Such an
f
-ML test
covers

x\in[0,1]
if
x\in\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\left\llbracket S_{i}\right\rrbracket}
.
x\in[0,1]
is
f
-random (in
[0,1]
)
if no
f
-ML test in
[0,1]
covers
x
.

Lemma IV.2.5
.

[
12
, Lemma 6.1]

There is a partial recursive function
\psi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{4}\to(\{0,1\}^{\ast})^{2}
such that if
\langle a,b,c,d\rangle
is a code for
I\in\mathcal{J}
, then
\psi(a,b,c,d){\downarrow}=\langle\sigma,\tau\rangle
where
I\subseteq\{0.X\mid\sigma\subset X\vee\tau\subset X\}
and
|\sigma|=|\tau|=|I|
.

Proposition IV.2.6
.

[
12
