, Lemma 6.2, essentially]

Suppose
x\in[0,1]
. Then
x
is
f
-random in
[0,1]
if and only if
\bin(x)
is
f
-random in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

Let
\psi
be as in
IV.2.5
.

Suppose
I\in\mathcal{J}
is given, and let
\langle a,b,c,d\rangle
be a code for
I
. Define
\bin(I)\coloneq\{\sigma,\tau\}
, where
\psi(a,b,c,d){\downarrow}=\langle\sigma,\tau\rangle
, and observe that for any
x\in I
we have
\bin(x)\in\bin(I)
and that

\dwt_{f}(\{\sigma,\tau\})\leq 2^{-f(|\sigma|)}+2^{-f(|\tau|)}=2\cdot 2^{-f(|I|)}=2\dwt_{f}(\{I\}).

Given
S\subseteq\mathcal{J}
, we define
\bin(S)\coloneq\bigcup\{\bin(I)\mid I\in S\}
. Then
\dwt_{f}(\bin(S))\leq 2\cdot\dwt_{f}(S)
; the uniformity of the assignment
S\mapsto\bin(S)
implies that if
S
is r.e. then
\bin(S)
is r.e., and if
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly r.e. then
\langle\bin(S_{i})\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly r.e. Moreover, if
x\in\left\llbracket S\right\rrbracket
then
\bin(x)\in\left\llbracket\bin(S)\right\rrbracket
, so if
x
is covered by an
f
-ML test then
\bin(x)
is covered by an
f
-ML test. Thus, if
\bin(x)
is
f
-random in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
then
x
is
f
-random in
[0,1]
.

Conversely, given
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
, let
I_{\sigma}=\{0.X\mid\sigma\subset X\}
. If
\sigma=\langle\rangle
then
I_{\sigma}=[0,1]
. Otherwise,
I_{\sigma}=[0.\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle 0,0,\ldots\rangle,0.\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle 1,1,\ldots\rangle]
. Observe that
|\sigma|=|I_{\sigma}|
, so
2^{-f(|\sigma|)}=2^{-f(|I|)}
. Additionally,
I_{\sigma}=I_{\tau}
if and only if
\sigma=\tau
. Given
S\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\ast}
, let
0.S=\{I_{\sigma}\mid\sigma\in S\}
. Then
\dwt_{f}(S)=\dwt_{f}(0.S)
; the uniformity in the assignment
S\mapsto 0.S
implies that if
S
is r.e. then
0.S
is r.e., and if
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly r.e. then
\langle 0.S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly r.e. Moreover, if
X\in\left\llbracket S\right\rrbracket
then
0.X\in\left\llbracket 0.S\right\rrbracket
, so if
X
is covered by an
f
-ML test in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, then
0.X
is covered by an
f
-ML test in
[0,1]
. Thus, if
x
is
f
-random in
[0,1]
then
\bin(x)
is
f
-random in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
.
∎

IV.2.2
Randomness in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
versus
[0,1]

The map
X\mapsto 0.X
from
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
to
[0,1]
can be described in a different way. With
I_{\sigma}
as in the proof of
IV.2.6
,
0.X
is the unique element of
\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{I_{X{\upharpoonright}\,n}}
. Said another way,
[0,1]
is split into two intervals of length
1/2
corresponding to
\langle 0\rangle
and
\langle 1\rangle
, each of those intervals are split into two intervals of length
1/4
corresponding to
\langle 0,0\rangle
,
\langle 0,1\rangle
,
\langle 1,0\rangle
, and
\langle 1,1\rangle
, and so on, then we take the intersection of the intervals corresponding to the initial segments of
X
to get
0.X
.

Repeating this methodology for
h^{\ast}
produces closed subintervals of
[0,1]
with rational endpoints, but not necessarily elements of
\mathcal{J}
. For that reason, we let
\mathcal{I}
be the set of
all
closed intervals
I\subseteq[0,1]
with rational endpoints.

Definition IV.2.7
.

Given
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
, define
\pi^{h}(\sigma)\coloneq[k/|h^{|\sigma|}|,(k+1)/|^{|\sigma|}|]
, where
k=\sum_{i=0}^{|\sigma|-1}{\sigma(i)\cdot|h^{i}|}
; in other words,
\pi^{h}(\langle\rangle)=[0,1]
and, for
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
and
0\leq i<h(|\sigma|)
,
\pi^{h}(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)
is the
i
-th subinterval of
\pi^{h}(\sigma)
after splitting
\pi^{h}(\sigma)
into
h(|\sigma|)
-many consecutive closed subintervals of equal length
1/|h^{|\sigma|+1}|
.

The map
\pi^{h}\colon h^{\mathbb{N}}\to[0,1]
is then defined by setting, for
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
,

\pi^{h}(X)\coloneq\text{unique element of $\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\pi^{h}(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)}$}.

Lemma IV.2.8
.

\pi^{h}
is a measure-preserving surjection of
h^{\mathbb{N}}
onto
[0,1]
. However,
\pi^{h}
is not injective, and for distinct
X,Y\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
,
\pi^{h}(X)=\pi^{h}(Y)
if and only if there is
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
such that
\{X,Y\}=\{\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle 0,0,\ldots\rangle,\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle h(|\sigma|)-1,h(|\sigma|+1)-1,\ldots\rangle\}
.

Proof.

Straight-forward.
∎

For an interval
I\in\mathcal{I}
, we wish to consider
f(|I|)
, although
|I|\in\mathbb{N}
only for
I\in\mathcal{J}
, requiring the following convention:

Convention IV.2.9
.

Given
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
, we implicitly extend
f
to a function
{[0,\infty)}\to{[0,\infty)}
by letting
f(x)=(f(\lfloor x\rfloor+1)-f(\lfloor x\rfloor))(x-\lfloor x\rfloor)+f(\lfloor x\rfloor)
.

We extend the definition of
\dwt_{f}
to
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{I})
in the obvious manner, and the definitions of
f
-ML tests and by extension
f
-randomness can likewise be extended. We will term these extended definitions by adding the prefix ‘extended’, as in, “
x\in[0,1]
is extended
f
-random in
[0,1]
if no extended
f
-ML test covers
x
.
”

An additional assumption we must make regards
f
and the sequence
\langle f(n)/n\rangle_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}}
. Later we will strengthen this assumption further.

Convention IV.2.10
.

Given
f
, we assume
\langle f(n)/n\rangle_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}}
is nondecreasing. As such, the function
x\in(0,\infty)\mapsto f(x)/x\in{[0,\infty)}
is nondecreasing as well.

Notation IV.2.11
.

Let
s\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
be the unique nondecreasing computable function such that
s(0)=1
and for which
|h^{n}|=2^{n\cdot s(n)}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Consequently,
|\pi^{h}(\sigma)|=n\cdot s(n)
for all
\sigma\in h^{n}
.

Proposition IV.2.12
.

For any
S\subseteq h^{\ast}
, the set
\pi^{h}[S]=\{\pi^{h}(\sigma)\mid\sigma\in S\}
satisfies
\dwt_{f}(\pi^{h}[S])\leq\dwt_{f}(S)
. Moreover, if
S
is r.e. then
\pi^{h}[S]
is r.e.

Proof.

Because
\pi^{h}
is measure preserving,
\mu_{h}(\sigma)=\lambda(\pi^{h}(\sigma))
. Given
\sigma\in h^{n}
,
n\leq n\cdot s(n)
implies
f(|\sigma|)/|\sigma|\leq f(|\pi^{h}(\sigma)|)/|\pi^{h}(\sigma)|
, and consequently
s(n)\cdot f(n)\leq f(n\cdot s(n))
. In particular,

2^{-f(|\pi^{h}(\sigma)|)}=2^{-f(n\cdot s(n))}\leq 2^{-s(n)\cdot f(n)}=(2^{-n\cdot s(n)})^{f(n)/n}=\gamma(\sigma)^{f(|\sigma|)}.

Thus,
\dwt_{f}(\pi^{h}[S])\leq\dwt_{f}(S)
. That
\pi^{h}[S]
is r.e. if
S
is r.e. is immediate.
∎

Corollary IV.2.13
.

If
\pi^{h}(X)\in[0,1]
is extended
f
-random in
[0,1]
, then
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
is
f
-random in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

The uniformity of the assignment
S\mapsto\pi^{h}[S]
implies that if
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a uniformly r.e. sequence of subsets of
h^{\ast}
, then
\langle\pi^{h}[S_{i}]\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a uniformly r.e. sequence of subsets of
[0,1]
. With
IV.2.12
, it follows that if
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is an
f
-ML test in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
then
\langle\pi^{h}[S_{i}]\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is an extended
f
-ML test in
[0,1]
.
∎

The proof of
IV.2.12
suggests that if wish to convert a extended
f
-ML test in
[0,1]
into an
f
-ML test in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
then we want to pull intervals in
\mathcal{I}
back into strings in
h^{\ast}
. However, the map
\pi^{h}\colon h^{\ast}\to\mathcal{I}
is not surjective, so given
I\in\mathcal{I}
we must instead cover
I
with intervals of the form
\pi^{h}(\sigma)
for
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
. This procedure must be sufficiently regular for an extended
f
-ML test in
[0,1]
to be pulled back to a
g
-ML test in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
for some appropriate
g
.

Definition IV.2.14
.

Given
f\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}g
, then we say that the regularity condition
(\ast)(g,f)
holds for
h
if

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\frac{\exp_{h(n-1)}(1-f(n\cdot s(n))\cdot(n\cdot s(n))^{-1})}{\exp_{2}(s(n)\cdot g(n)-f(n\cdot s(n)))}}<\infty.

(\ast)(g,f)

Remark IV.2.15
.

In
[
9
]
, the regularity condition
(\ast)(g,f)
is simplified by the fact that
f
is linear, and hence
\frac{f(n\cdot s(n))}{n\cdot s(n)}
simplifies into an expression independent of
n
or
s(n)
.

Proposition IV.2.16
.

Suppose
(\ast)(g,f)
holds for
h
and let

\alpha=3\cdot\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\frac{\exp_{h(n-1)}(1-f(n\cdot s(n))\cdot(n\cdot s(n))^{-1})}{\exp_{2}(s(n)\cdot g(n)-f(n\cdot s(n)))}}.

Then for each
I\in\mathcal{I}
there exists
\tilde{I}\subseteq h^{\ast}
such that
I\subseteq\bigcup{\pi^{h}[\tilde{I}]}
and
\dwt_{g}(\tilde{I})\leq\alpha\cdot 2^{-f(|I|)}
. Moreover,
\tilde{I}
can be uniformly computed from a code for
I
.

Proof.

We start by setting notation. For each
I\in\mathcal{I}
, let
n_{I}
be the unique
n\geq 1
such that
|h^{n}|^{-1}<\lambda(I)\leq|h^{n-1}|^{-1}
and
k_{I}
be the greatest integer
k
such that
k/|h^{n_{I}}|\leq\lambda(I)
. Then
k_{I}<h(n_{I}-1)
and there is a set
\hat{I}\subseteq\pi^{h}[h^{n_{I}}]
computable from a code of
I
of size
\leq k_{I}+2
such that
I\subseteq\bigcup{\hat{I}}
, namely the intervals in
\pi^{h}[h^{n_{I}}]
intersecting
I
nontrivially (i.e., intervals which intersect
I
at more than just an endpoint).

Suppose
I\in\mathcal{I}
is given, and let
n=n_{I}
and
k=k_{I}
. Because
k/|h^{n}|\leq\lambda(I)
, we have
|h^{n}|^{-1}/\lambda(I)\leq k
. Given
J\in\hat{I}
,
\lambda(J)\leq\lambda(I)
and so
|I|\leq|J|=n\cdot s(n)
. Then

\displaystyle\dwt_{g}(\tilde{I})=\sum_{\sigma\in\tilde{I}}{\gamma_{n}^{-g(n)}}

\displaystyle\leq(k+2)2^{-s(n)\cdot g(n)}

\displaystyle\leq 3k\cdot\exp_{2}(-\bigl(s(n)\cdot g(n)-f(n\cdot s(n))\bigr))\cdot(\lambda(I)/k)^{f(n\cdot s(n))\cdot(n\cdot s(n))^{-1}}

\displaystyle\leq 3\cdot\frac{\exp_{k}(1-f(n\cdot s(n))\cdot(n\cdot s(n))^{-1})}{\exp_{2}(s(n)\cdot g(n)-f(n\cdot s(n)))}\cdot\lambda(I)^{f(n\cdot s(n))\cdot(n\cdot s(n))^{-1}}

\displaystyle\leq 3\cdot\frac{\exp_{h(n-1)}(1-f(n\cdot s(n))\cdot(n\cdot s(n))^{-1})}{\exp_{2}(s(n)\cdot g(n)-f(n\cdot s(n)))}\cdot\lambda(I)^{f(|I|)/|I|}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\cdot 2^{-f(|I|)}.

∎

Corollary IV.2.17
.

Suppose
(\ast)(g,f)
holds for
h
and let
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
. If
X
is
g
-random in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
then
\pi^{h}(X)
is generalized
f
-random in
[0,1]
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha
be as in the statement of
IV.2.16
. Given
I\in\mathcal{I}
, let
\tilde{I}
and
\hat{I}
be as in the statement and proof of
IV.2.16
. Given
S\subseteq\mathcal{I}
r.e., let
\hat{S}=\bigcup\{\hat{I}\mid I\in S\}
and
\tilde{S}=\bigcup\{\tilde{I}\mid I\in S\}
. Then
\tilde{S}
is r.e. and
\dwt_{g}(\tilde{S})=\dwt_{g}(\hat{S})\leq\alpha\cdot\dwt_{f}(S)
.

Suppose for the sake of a contradiction that
\pi^{h}(X)
is not generalized
f
-random in
[0,1]
, and so let
\langle S_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a generalized
f
-ML test covering
\pi^{h}(X)
. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
satisfy
\alpha\leq 2^{m}
. That
\tilde{I}
can be computed uniformly from a code for
I
implies
\langle\tilde{S}_{i+m}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly r.e. Then
\langle\tilde{S}_{i+m}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a
g
-ML test covering
X
, contradicting the hypothesis that
X
is
g
-random in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
.

∎

Corollary IV.2.18
.

Suppose
x\in[0,1]
. Then
x
is
f
-random in
[0,1]
if and only if
x
is generalized
f
-random in
[0,1]
.

Proof.

Being generalized
f
-random in
[0,1]
clearly implies being
f
-random in
[0,1]
.

In the opposite direction, suppose
x
is
f
-random in
[0,1]
, so that
\bin(x)
is
f
-random in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
by
IV.2.6
. With
h(n)\coloneq 2
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
s(n)=1
, so
|\pi^{h}(\sigma)|=|\sigma|
for all
\sigma\in h^{\ast}=\{0,1\}^{\ast}
. The condition
(\ast)(g,f)
for
h
is then the statement that

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\frac{\exp_{2}(1-f(n)/n)}{\exp_{2}(g(n)-f(n))}}<\infty.

Then we may observe that
(\ast)(f,f)
holds for
h
, and so
IV.2.17
implies
\pi^{h}(\bin(x))=x
is generalized
f
-random in
[0,1]
.
∎

Corollary IV.2.19
.

Suppose
\lim_{n\to\infty}{\frac{s(n-1)}{s(n)}}=1
and
\varepsilon>0
. If
f(n)=n-j(n)
and
g(n)=n-(1-\varepsilon)\frac{j(n\cdot s(n))}{s(n)}
then
(\ast)(g,f)
holds for
h
. Consequently, if
X
is
g
-random in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
then
\pi^{h}(X)
is
f
-random in
[0,1]
.

Proof.

If
|h^{n}|=n\cdot s(n)
, then
h(n-1)=|h^{n}|/|h^{n-1}|=2^{n\cdot s(n)-(n-1)\cdot s(n-1)}
. Then

\displaystyle\log_{2}\left(\frac{\exp_{h(n-1)}(1-f(n\cdot s(n))\cdot(n\cdot s(n))^{-1})}{\exp_{2}(s(n)\cdot g(n)-f(n\cdot s(n)))}\right)

\displaystyle=(n\cdot s(n)-(n-1)\cdot s(n-1))\cdot\left(1-\frac{f(n\cdot s(n))}{n\cdot s(n)}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-s(n)\cdot g(n)+f(n\cdot s(n))

\displaystyle=n\cdot s(n)-(n-1)\cdot s(n-1)-f(n\cdot s(n))

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(n-1)\cdot s(n-1)}{n\cdot s(n)}\cdot(n\cdot s(n)-j(n\cdot s(n)))

\displaystyle\quad-s(n)\cdot(n-(1-\varepsilon)\frac{j(n\cdot s(n))}{s(n)})+f(n\cdot s(n))

\displaystyle=\left((1-\varepsilon)-\frac{n-1}{n}\frac{s(n-1)}{s(n)}\right)\cdot j(n\cdot s(n)).

Because
\lim_{n\to\infty}{\frac{s(n-1)}{s(n)}}=1
by hypothesis, for all sufficiently large
n
we have
1-\varepsilon<\frac{n-1}{n}\frac{s(n-1)}{s(n)}
and hence
\left((1-\varepsilon)-\frac{n-1}{n}\frac{s(n-1)}{s(n)}\right)\cdot j(n\cdot s(n))<0
. It follows that
(\ast)(g,f)
holds for
h
.
∎

Remark IV.2.20
.

The condition that
\lim_{n\to\infty}{\frac{s(n-1)}{s(n)}}=1
is equivalent to
\lim_{n\to\infty}{\frac{\log_{2}|h^{n}|}{\log_{2}|h^{n-1}|}}=1
, which is equivalent to
\lim_{n\to\infty}{\frac{\log_{2}h(n-1)}{\log_{2}|h^{n}|}}=0
.

IV.2.3
Improving Greenberg & Miller’s Conclusion

The motivating theorem
[
9
, Theorem 4.9]
proceeds by showing that for any sufficiently slow-growing
\mathrm{DNR}
function
Z
,
Z
computes an
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
which is
(\lambda n.\delta n)
-random in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
for each rational
\delta<1
, where
h(n)=(n+1)\cdot 2^{n}
. By showing that
\lim_{n\to\infty}{\frac{\log_{2}|h^{n}|}{\log_{2}|h^{n-1}|}}=1
and noting that
\frac{\delta n\cdot s(n)}{s(n)}=\delta n
,
IV.2.19
shows that
\pi^{h}(X)
is
(\lambda n.\delta n)
-random in
[0,1]
(and hence
\bin(\pi^{h}(X))
is
(\lambda n.\,\delta n)
-random in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
) for each rational
\delta<1
. To arrange for
X
being
(\lambda n.\delta n)
-random in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
for each rational
\delta<1
,
X
is constructed entry by entry so that
d^{h}(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\leq n!
for all
n
, where
d^{h}
is a fixed universal r.e. supermatingale
d^{h}\colon h^{\ast}\to{[0,\infty)}
. By carefully examining the relevant calculations and using the full power of
IV.2.19
, it can be shown that
\pi^{h}(X)
exhibits more partial randomness than just having effective Hausdorff dimension
1
.

Theorem IV.2.21
.

Let
h(n)\coloneq(n+1)\cdot 2^{n}
.

1.

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\frac{\log_{2}|h^{n}|}{\log_{2}|h^{n-1}|}}=1
.

2.

Suppose
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
satisfies
d^{h}(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\leq n!
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
X
is
(\lambda n.n-\beta\log_{2}n)
-random in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
for all
\beta>2
. Consequently,
\pi^{h}(X)
is
(\lambda n.n-\alpha\sqrt{n}\log_{2}n)
-random in
[0,1]
for all
\alpha>1
.

Proof.

1.

To aid in finding the corresponding
s(n)
, recall Stirling’s Approximation:

Lemma IV.2.22

(Stirling’s Approximation)
.

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\frac{n!}{\sqrt{2\pi n}(n/e)^{n}}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}{\frac{n!}{\exp_{2}(n\log_{2}n+n\log_{2}e+\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}n+\log_{2}\sqrt{2\pi})}}=1.

Then
\log_{2}|h^{n}|\approx n\cdot\left(\frac{n-1}{2}+\log_{2}n+\log_{2}e+\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}n^{1/n}+\log_{2}(2\pi)^{2/n}\right)
, so
\lim_{n\to\infty}{\frac{s(n-1)}{s(n)}}=1
.

2.

We start by computing
d(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\mu_{h}(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)^{1-g(n)/n}
:

\displaystyle d(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\mu_{h}(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)^{1-g(n)/n}

\displaystyle\leq n!\cdot(n!2^{n(n-1)/2})^{\frac{g(n)}{n}-1}

\displaystyle=(n!)^{\frac{g(n)}{n}}\cdot 2^{\left(\frac{g(n)}{n}-1\right)\cdot\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}

\displaystyle\approx\exp_{2}\bigg(\frac{g(n)}{n}\left(n\log_{2}n+n\log_{2}e+\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}n+\log_{2}\sqrt{2\pi}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad~~~~~+\left(\frac{g(n)}{n}-1\right)\cdot\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\exp_{2}\left(g(n)\left(\frac{n-1}{2}+\log_{2}\left(n^{1+1/n}\cdot e\cdot\sqrt[2n]{2\pi}\right)\right)-\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\right).

We want this last expression to be bounded above, so there must be a
c\in\mathbb{N}
for which

g(n)\left(n-1+2\log_{2}\left(n^{1+1/n}\cdot e\cdot\sqrt[2n]{2\pi}\right)\right)\leq n^{2}-n+c.

Writing
g(n)=n-\tilde{j}(n)
, we find that if

\tilde{j}(n)\geq\frac{2\log_{2}\left(n^{1+1/n}\cdot e\cdot\sqrt[2n]{2\pi}\right)-\frac{c}{n}}{1-\frac{1}{n}+\frac{2}{n}\log_{2}\left(n^{1+1/n}\cdot e\cdot\sqrt[2n]{2\pi}\right)}

then
X
is
g
-random. Hence, for any
\beta>2
,
X
is
g
-random for
g(n)=n-\beta\cdot\log_{2}n
.

By
IV.2.19
, if
X
is
\left(\lambda n.n-(1-\varepsilon)\frac{j(n\cdot s(n))}{s(n)}\right)
-random in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
then
\pi^{h}(X)
is
\left(\lambda n.n-j(n)\right)
-random in
[0,1]
. To show that
\pi^{h}(X)
is
\left(\lambda n.n-\alpha\sqrt{n}\log_{2}n\right)
-random in
[0,1]
for any
\alpha>1
, it suffices to show that there is
\beta>2
and
\varepsilon>0
such that
n-\beta\log_{2}n\geq n-(1-\varepsilon)\frac{\alpha\sqrt{n\cdot s(n)}\log_{2}(n\cdot s(n))}{s(n)}
for all sufficiently large
n
, or equivalently that
\beta\log_{2}n\leq(1-\varepsilon)\frac{\alpha\sqrt{n\cdot s(n)}\log_{2}(n\cdot s(n))}{s(n)}
for all sufficiently large
n
. Using the approximations
\frac{1}{2}n\leq s(n)\leq(\frac{1}{2}+\delta)n
for
\delta>0
, we have

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon)\frac{\alpha\sqrt{n\cdot s(n)}\log_{2}(n\cdot s(n))}{s(n)}

\displaystyle\geq(1-\varepsilon)\frac{\alpha\sqrt{n\cdot\frac{1}{2}n}\log_{2}(n\cdot\frac{1}{2}n)}{(\frac{1}{2}+\delta)n}

\displaystyle\geq 2(1-\varepsilon)\frac{\sqrt{1/2}}{\sqrt{1/2+\delta}}\alpha\log_{2}n-(1-\varepsilon)\frac{\sqrt{1/2}}{\sqrt{1/2+\delta}}\alpha.

Thus, it suffices for there to be
\beta>2
,
\varepsilon>0
, and
\delta>0
such that
\beta<2(1-\varepsilon)\frac{\sqrt{1/2}}{\sqrt{1/2+\delta}}\alpha
. This is possible whenever
\alpha>1
.

∎

IV.3
Quantifying the Reduction of Avoidance to Complexity – Preliminary Case

IV.2.21
addresses the question of the degree of partial randomness we may extract within the proof of
[
9
, Theorem 4.9]
and serves as a precursor to a more general result putting a lower bound on which order functions
f
have slow-growing order functions
q
for which
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
. Our precursor to addressing the growth rate of such
q
is the following refinement of
[
9
, Theorem 4.9]
:

Theorem IV.3.1
.

For rationals
\alpha\in(1,\infty)
and
\beta\in(0,1/2)
, we have

\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.n-\alpha\sqrt{n}\log_{2}n)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(\lambda n.(\log_{2}n)^{\beta}).

More generally, if
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is an order function such that
q(2^{(3/2+\varepsilon)n^{2}})\leq n+1
for almost all
n
and some
\varepsilon>0
, then
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.n-\alpha\sqrt{n}\log_{2}n)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
.

We start by fixing some notation and definitions.

Definition IV.3.2
.

For
a,b,c\in\mathbb{N}
, the class
P_{a}^{b,c}
is defined by

P_{a}^{b,c}\coloneq\{F\colon\mathbb{N}\to[a]^{b}\mid\forall n\forall j<c\,(j\in\dom\varphi_{n}\to\varphi_{n}(j)\notin F(n))\}

where
[a]^{b}\coloneq\{S\subseteq\{0,1,2,\ldots,a-1\}\mid|S|=b\}
.

In particular,
P_{a}^{1,c}=\{F\colon\mathbb{N}\to a\mid\forall n\forall j<c\,(j\in\dom\varphi_{n}\to\varphi_{n}(j)\neq F(n))\}
.

Given
a\in\mathbb{N}
,
P^{1,1}_{a}=\{X\in a^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\forall n\,(F(n)\nsimeq\varphi_{n}(0))\}
. By the Parametrization Theorem, there is a total recursive
f\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\varphi_{f(e,x)}(y)\simeq\varphi_{e}(x)
for all
e,x,y\in\mathbb{N}
. Then given
X\in P_{a}^{1,1}
, the sequence
Y\in a^{\mathbb{N}}
defined by
Y(n)\coloneq X(f(n,n))
is a member of
\mathrm{DNR}(a)
. Conversely, given
Y\in\mathrm{DNR}(a)
, the sequence
X\in a^{\mathbb{N}}
defined by
X(n)\coloneq Y(f(n,0))
is a member of
P_{a}^{1,1}
. It is also relevant to observe that in both directions, each entry of the output sequence depends on only a single entry of the input sequence. Moreover, this one-to-one correspondence is uniform in
a
.

Convention IV.3.3
.

\mathrm{DNR}(a)
will be identified with
P_{a}^{1,1}
.

[
9
, Corollary 4.6]
shows that
P_{ca}^{1,c}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{DNR}(a)
, uniformly in
a,c\in\mathbb{N}
. In order to analyze a related result of Khan
[
15
, Theorem 6.3]
(see
Section

V.1.2
), in 2020 Simpson performed a detailed analysis of this strong reduction with an eye towards generalization and to put an explicit and uniform bound on the number of entries of an element of
\mathrm{DNR}(a)
are needed to compute a given bit of the corresponding element of
P_{ca+b}^{b+1,c}
.

Proposition IV.3.4
.

Uniformly in
a,b,c\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a recursive functional
\Psi\colon\mathrm{DNR}(a)\to P_{ca+b}^{b+1,c}
and a recursive function
U\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathbb{N})
such that for every
X\in\mathrm{DNR}(a)
,
\Psi(X)(n)
depends only on
X{\upharpoonright}\,U(n)
. Moreover,
|U(n)|\leq c\binom{ca+b}{a}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

With the identification of
\mathrm{DNR}(a)
with
P_{a}^{1,1}
, the reduction
P_{ca+b}^{b+1,c}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{DNR}(a)
will result from a sequence of strong reductions

P_{ca+b}^{b+1,c}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}P_{a+d}^{d+1,1}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}P_{a}^{1,1},

where
d=(c-1)a+b
. These reductions result from the following lemmas:

Lemma IV.3.5
.

P_{a+d}^{d+1,c}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}P_{a}^{1,c}
, uniformly in
a\geq 2
,
d\geq 0
, and
c\geq 1
.

Proof.

The partial function
\theta\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
defined by

\theta(\sigma,n,j)\simeq\min\{i<|\sigma|\mid\varphi_{n}(j){\downarrow}=\sigma(i)\}

for each
\sigma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
and
n,j\in\mathbb{N}

is partial recursive, so there exists a total recursive function
f\colon\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\varphi_{f(\sigma,n)}(j)\simeq\theta(\sigma,n,j)
for all
\sigma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
and
n,j\in\mathbb{N}
. Given
S\subseteq\mathbb{N}
with
|S|=a
, let
\sigma_{S}\in\mathbb{N}^{a}
be the enumeration of
S
in increasing order.

Suppose
X\in P_{a}^{1,c}
. Recursively in
d
, we define
F_{d}\in P_{a+d}^{d+1,c}
.

Base Case.

For
d=0
,
F_{0}=X
.

Induction Step.

Given
F_{d}\in P_{a+d}^{d+1,c}
has been defined, let
F_{d+1}(n)=F_{d}(n)\cup\{\sigma_{S}(X(f(\sigma_{S},n)))\}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
S=(a+d+1)\setminus F_{d}(n)
(note that
|S|=a
). Because
X\in P_{a}^{1,c}
, for all
j<c

X(f(\sigma_{S},n))\nsimeq\varphi_{f(\sigma_{S},n)}(j)\simeq\theta(\sigma_{S},n,j)\simeq\min\{i<a\mid\varphi_{n}(j){\downarrow}=\sigma_{S}(i)\}.

Thus,
\sigma_{S}(X(f(\sigma_{S},n)))\nsimeq\varphi_{n}(j)
for all
j<c
, and so
F_{d+1}\in P_{a+d+1}^{d+2,c}
.

∎

Lemma IV.3.6
.

Let
d=(c-1)a+b
. Then
P_{ca+b}^{c+b,c+e}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}P_{a+d}^{d+1,e+1}
, uniformly in
a\geq 2
,
c\geq 1
,
b\geq 0
, and
e\geq 0
.

Proof.

Suppose
F\in P_{a+d}^{d+1,e+1}
. Because
a+d=ca+b
, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
j<e+1
we have
\varphi_{n}(j)\notin F(n)\in[ca+b]^{d+1}
. The partial function
\theta\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}
defined by

\theta(n,j,y)\simeq\varphi_{n}(j)

for each
n,j,y\in\mathbb{N}

is partial recursive, so there exists a total recursive function
g\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\varphi_{g(n,j)}(y)\simeq\varphi_{n}(j)
for all
n,j,y\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular,
\varphi_{n}(j)\simeq\varphi_{g(n,j)}(0)\notin F(g(n,j))
for all
n,j\in\mathbb{N}
.

Define
H(n)\coloneq F(n)\cap\bigcap_{i<c-1}{F(g(n,e+i+1))}
, so that for all
j<e+1
we have
\varphi_{n}(j)\notin F(n)\supseteq H(n)
, and for all
i<c-1
we have
\varphi_{n}(e+i+1)\notin F(g(n,e+i+1))\supseteq H(n)
. Thus, for every
j<c+e
we have
\varphi_{n}(j)\notin H(n)
. The only obstacle to
H
being a member of
P_{ca+b}^{c+b,c+e}
is that
H
need not be of size
c+b
. However, as long as
|H(n)|\geq c+b
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
then we can let
G(n)
consist of the first
c+b
elements of
H(n)
. To that effect,

\displaystyle|(ca+b)\setminus H(n)|

\displaystyle=\left|\bigl[(ca+b)\setminus F(n)\bigr]\cup\bigcup_{i<c-1}{\bigl[(ca+b)\setminus F(g(n,e+i+1))\bigr]}\right|

\displaystyle\leq c\cdot\bigl((ca+b)-(d+1)\bigr)

\displaystyle=c(a-1)

so
|H(n)|\geq(ca+b)-c(a-1)=b+c
. With
G(n)
consisting of the first
c+b
elements of
H(n)
, we have
G\in P_{ca+b}^{c+b,c+e}
.
∎

In the proof of
IV.3.5
, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}

F_{d}(n)
depends only on the values
X(f(\sigma_{S},n))
for certain
S\in[a+d]^{a}
. In the proof of
IV.3.6
,
H(n)
(and hence
G(n)
) depends on
F(n)
and
F(g(n,e+i+1))
for
i<c-1
. Thus, in the reduction
X\in P_{a}^{1,1}\mapsto F_{d}\in P_{a+d}^{d+1,1}\mapsto G\in P_{ca+b}^{b+1,c}
,
G(n)
is determined by
X{\upharpoonright}\,U(n)
, where

U(n)=\{f(\sigma_{S},n)\mid S\in[a+d]^{a}\}\cup\{f(\sigma_{S},g(n,i+1))\mid S\in[a+d]^{a},i<c-1\}

and
|U(n)|\leq c\binom{a+d}{a}=c\binom{ca+b}{a}
.
∎

Proof of
IV.3.1
.

Let
h(n)=(n+1)2^{n}
and let
d=d^{h}
be a universal left r.e. supermartingale for
h^{\mathbb{N}}
. As
d
is left r.e., uniformly in
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
we can simultaneously and uniformly approximate
d(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)
from below for all
i<h(n)
. Thus, there is a total recursive function
\sigma\mapsto m_{\sigma}
such that for all
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
and
x<2^{|\sigma|}
,
\varphi_{m_{\sigma}}(x)\downarrow=i
if and only if
\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle
is the
x
-th immediate successor
\tau
of
\sigma
found with respect to the aforementioned procedure with
d(\tau)>(n+1)!
.

Let
\#\colon h^{\ast}\to\mathbb{N}
be the inverse of the enumeration of
h^{\ast}
according to the shortlex ordering. In particular, for
\sigma\in h^{n}
,

\#(\sigma)\leq|h^{0}|+|h^{1}|+\cdots+|h^{n}|=\sum_{i=0}^{n}{i!\cdot 2^{i(i-1)/2}}.

By potentially modifying our enumeration
\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\ldots
of partial recursive functions, we can assume without loss of generality that
m_{\sigma}=2\#(\sigma)
. Fix
\varepsilon>0
and let
m_{n}^{\ast}=2^{(1/2+\varepsilon)n^{2}}
, so that

1+\sup\{m_{\sigma}\mid\sigma\in h^{n}\}\leq m_{n}^{\ast},

which follows from the computations:

\displaystyle 1+2\cdot\#(\sigma)

\displaystyle\leq 1+2\left(|h^{0}|+|h^{1}|+\cdots+|h^{n}|\right)

\displaystyle=1+2\sum_{i=0}^{n}{\left(i!\cdot 2^{i(i-1)/2}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq n\cdot\left(n!\cdot 2^{n(n-1)/2}\right)

\displaystyle\approx\exp_{2}(n^{2}/2+n\log_{2}n+n(\log_{2}e-1/2)+(1/2)\log_{2}n+\log_{2}\sqrt{2\pi}+1)

\displaystyle<2^{(1/2+\varepsilon)n^{2}}.

IV.3.4
shows that, uniformly in
n
, there is a recursive functional
\Psi_{n}\colon\mathrm{DNR}(n+1)\to P_{h(n)}^{1,2^{n}}
and recursive function
U_{n}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathbb{N})
such that for any
Z\in\mathrm{DNR}(n+1)
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
Z{\upharpoonright}\,U_{n}(i)
determines
\Psi_{n}(Z)(i)
and
|U_{n}(i)|\leq 2^{n}\binom{(n+1)2^{n}}{n+1}
.

We are principally interested in initial segments
\rho
of elements of
P_{h(n)}^{1,2^{n}}
of length
m_{n}^{\ast}
(in fact, we are only concerned with the values at the inputs
m_{\sigma}
for
\sigma\in h^{n}
), so that:

1.

\rho(m_{\sigma})<h(n)=h(|\sigma|)
.

2.

For all
x<2^{n}
, if
\varphi_{m_{\sigma}}(x){\downarrow}
, then
\rho(m_{\sigma})\neq\varphi_{m_{\sigma}}(x)
.

Define
U\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathbb{N})
by
U(n)\coloneq\bigcup_{i<m_{n}^{\ast}}{U_{n}(i)}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and subsequently define
\overline{u}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
recursively by

\displaystyle\overline{u}(0)

\displaystyle\coloneq 0,

\displaystyle\overline{u}(n+1)

\displaystyle\coloneq\overline{u}(n)+|U(n)|.

Finally, define
\psi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
by letting

\psi(\overline{u}(n)+j)\simeq\varphi_{\text{$j$-th element of $U(n)$}}(0)

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
j<|U(n)|
.
By construction, for any
Z\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(n+1)
,
Z{\upharpoonright}\,\overline{u}(n+1)
can be used to compute an initial segment of an element of
P_{h(n)}^{1,2^{n}}
of length
m_{n}^{\ast}
, and this is uniform in
n
.

If
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is an order function satisfying

p(\overline{u}(n+1))\leq n+1

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then uniformly in
n
and
Z\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
,
Z{\upharpoonright}\,\overline{u}(n+1)
can be used to compute an initial segment of an element of
P_{h(n)}^{1,2^{n}}
of length
m_{n}^{\ast}
. Given
Z\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
, define
G\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
by setting the value of
G(m_{\sigma})
according to this uniform process for each
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
; for
n
not of the form
m_{\sigma}
(which can be recursively checked) set
G(n)\coloneq 0
. Then define
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
recursively by

\displaystyle X(0)

\displaystyle\coloneq G(m_{\langle\rangle}),

\displaystyle X(n+1)

\displaystyle\coloneq G(m_{\langle X(0),X(1),\ldots,X(n)\rangle}).

In particular, for all
x<2^{n}
, if
\varphi_{m_{X{\upharpoonright}\,n}}(x)\downarrow=i
(which is equivalent to
X{\upharpoonright}\,n{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle
being the
x
-th immediate successor of
X{\upharpoonright}\,n
found such that
d(X{\upharpoonright}\,n{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)\geq(n+1)!
) then
X(n+1)=G(m_{X{\upharpoonright}\,n})\neq i
. We make the following observation: if
d(\sigma)\leq n!
for some
\sigma\in h^{n}
, then there at most
2^{n}
many immediate successors
\tau
of
\sigma
such that
d(\tau)\geq(n+1)!
since

n!\geq d(\sigma)\geq\frac{1}{h(n)}\sum_{k<h(n)}{d(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle k\rangle)}=\frac{1}{(n+1)2^{n}}\sum_{k<h(n)}{d(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle k\rangle)}.

Thus, by induction on
n
we find that
d(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\leq n!
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
IV.2.21
then implies that
Y=\bin(\pi^{h}(X))\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is
f
-random.

It remains to finish the analysis of
q
. Suppose
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is an order function such that for each
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
we have
q(an+b)\leq p(n)
for almost all
n
. Then
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
. So if
q
is such that for all
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
we have
q(a\overline{u}(n+1)+b)\leq n+1
for almost all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
. We show that if
q(2^{(3/2+\varepsilon)n^{2}})\leq n+1
for almost all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then
q
satisfies this aforementioned condition.

First, we must find an upper bound of
\overline{u}
. Recall that
|U_{n}(i)|\leq 2^{n}\binom{(n+1)2^{n}}{n+1}
for all
n,i\in\mathbb{N}
, so

\displaystyle|U(n)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i<m_{n}^{\ast}}{|U_{n}(i)|}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i<m_{n}^{\ast}}{2^{n}\cdot\binom{(n+1)2^{n}}{n+1}}

\displaystyle=m_{n}^{\ast}\cdot 2^{n}\cdot\binom{(n+1)2^{n}}{n+1}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{(1/2+\varepsilon)n^{2}}\cdot\binom{(n+1)\cdot 2^{n}}{n+1}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{(1/2+\varepsilon)n^{2}}\cdot\exp_{2}(n^{2}+(1+\log_{2}e)n+\log_{2}e)

\displaystyle=\exp_{2}((3/2+\varepsilon)n^{2}+(1+\log_{2}e)n+\log_{2}e).

By slightly increasing
\varepsilon
, we find
|U(n)|\leq 2^{(3/2+\varepsilon)n^{2}}
.
Next,
\overline{u}(n+1)=\sum_{m\leq n}{|U(m)|}\leq(n+1)|U(n)|
, so
a\cdot\overline{u}(n+1)+b\leq a(n+1)|U(n)|+b\leq 3a\cdot n\cdot|U(n)|
for all sufficiently large
n
. As before, a slight increase in
\varepsilon
allows us to absorb the
3an
term, so that

a\overline{u}(n+1)+b\leq 2^{(3/2+\varepsilon)n^{2}}.

Thus, if
q(2^{(3/2+\varepsilon)n^{2}})\leq n+1
, then

q(a\overline{u}(n+1)+b)\leq q(2^{(3/2+\varepsilon)n^{2}})\leq n+1.

If
\alpha
is a positive rational less than
1/2
then for almost all
n
we have
(\log_{2}(2^{(3/2+\varepsilon)n^{2}}))^{\alpha}\leq n+1
, proving the ‘in particular’ statement.
∎

Remark IV.3.7
.

We assumed without loss of generality that
m_{\sigma}=2\cdot\#(\sigma)
by choosing an appropriate enumeration of the partial recursive functions. In general, if
\theta\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is a total recursive, injective function with recursive coinfinite image (as in the case of
n\mapsto 2n
), then for any total recursive function
g\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
there is an admissible enumeration
\tilde{\varphi}_{0},\tilde{\varphi}_{1},\ldots
such that
\tilde{\varphi}_{\theta(e)}\simeq\varphi_{g(e)}
for all
e\in\mathbb{N}
.

IV.4
Quantifying the Reduction of Avoidance to Complexity – General Case

Within the proof of
IV.3.1
, how much does our result depend on the particular choice of
h
?

Use I.

For
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
, we defined
m_{\sigma}=2\#(\sigma)
(where
\#(\sigma)
is the enumeration of
h^{\ast}
according to the shortlex ordering) and assumed without loss of genearlity that
\varphi_{m_{\sigma}}(x)\downarrow=k
if and only if
\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle k\rangle
is the
x
-th immediate successor
\tau
of
\sigma
such that
d(\tau)\geq(n+1)!
.

Use II.

The reduction
P_{h(n)}^{1,2^{n}}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{DNR}(n+1)
, uniform in
n
, is used to define the recursive function
\overline{u}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
and the partial recursive function
\psi\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that, uniformly in
n
,
Z{\upharpoonright}\,\overline{u}(n+1)
can be used to compute an initial segment of an element of
P_{h(n)}^{1,2^{n}}
of length
m_{n}^{\ast}
given any
Z\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(n+1)
.

Use III.

For
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
a recursive order function satisfying
p(\overline{u}(n+1))\leq n+1
, a
Z\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
computes an
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
d(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\leq n!
for all
n
, based on the observation that if
d(\sigma)\leq n!
for some
\sigma\in h^{n}
, then there are at most
2^{n}
many immediate successors
\tau
of
\sigma
such that
d(\tau)\geq(n+1)!
.

Use IV.

Using the fact that
d(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\leq n!
for all
n
, show that
d(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\mu^{h}(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)^{1-g(n)/n}
is bounded above for any
g
of the form
g(n)=n-\beta\log_{2}n
for
\beta>2
. Hence,
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
is (strongly)
g
-random in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Use V.

Because
\lim_{n\to\infty}{\frac{\log_{2}|h^{n}|}{\log_{2}|h^{n-1}|}}=1
and for each
\alpha>1
there are
\beta>2
and
\varepsilon>0
such that
\beta\log_{2}n\leq(1-\varepsilon)\left(\alpha\sqrt{n\cdot s(n)}\log_{2}(n\cdot s(n))\right)\cdot s(n)^{-1}
for almost all
n
(where
2^{n\cdot s(n)}=|h^{n}|
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
), it follows that
Y=\bin(\pi^{h}(X))
is
(\lambda n.n-\alpha\sqrt{n}\log_{2}n)
-random in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

By analyzing the necessary and sufficient conditions for an
h
of the form
h(n)=k(n)\cdot\ell(n)
to satisfy the properties corresponding to each Use and subsequently choosing
k
and
\ell
satisfying those conditions with specific
f
and
g
in mind, we can prove the following technical result:

Theorem IV.4.1
.

Suppose
j\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is an order function such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}{j(n)/n}=0
and for which the function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
defined by
f(n)\coloneq n-j(n)
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
is an order function. Given
s\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[1,\infty)}
and a rational
\varepsilon>0
, define

\displaystyle\tilde{j}(n)

\displaystyle\coloneq(1-\varepsilon)\frac{j(s(n)\cdot n)}{s(n)},

\displaystyle\ell(n)

\displaystyle\coloneq\exp_{2}(j(s(n+1)\cdot(n+1))-j(s(n)\cdot n))^{1-\varepsilon},

\displaystyle h(n)

\displaystyle\coloneq\exp_{2}(s(n+1)\cdot(n+1)-s(n)\cdot n).

If there are
s\colon\mathbb{N}\to[1,\infty)
and rational
\varepsilon>0
such that

1.

\im h\subseteq\mathbb{N}
,

2.

\lim_{n\to\infty}{s(n+1)/s(n)}=1
, and

3.

\tilde{j}
is an order function,

then
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
for any order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
such that, for almost all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

q\left(\exp_{2}((1-\varepsilon)^{-1}\cdot[s(n+1)\cdot(n+1)-j(s(n+1)\cdot(n+1))]\cdot\ell(n))\right)\leq\ell(n).

For the remainder of this subsection, we use the following notation:

Notation IV.4.2
.

k
and
\ell
denote recursive functions
\mathbb{N}\to{[1,\infty)}
.
h
,
K
,
L
, and
H
are defined by, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
h(n)\coloneq k(n)\cdot\ell(n)
and

H(n)\coloneq h(0)\cdot h(1)\mathbin{\cdots}h(n-1),\quad K(n)\coloneq k(0)\cdot k(1)\mathbin{\cdots}k(n-1),\quad L(n)\coloneq\ell(0)\cdot\ell(1)\mathbin{\cdots}\ell(n-1).

It is most convenient for
h
to take image in
\mathbb{N}
so that
|h^{n}|=H(n)
, so we make the following conventions:

Convention IV.4.3
.

k
and
\ell
will always be such that
h
is an order function of the form
\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
. As a result,
|h^{n}|=H(n)=K(n)\cdot L(n)
.

Additionally, if
\alpha\in(1,\infty)
, then
\mathrm{DNR}(\alpha)=\{X\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\forall n\,(X(n)<\alpha\wedge X(n)\nsimeq\varphi_{n}(n))\}=\mathrm{DNR}(\lceil\alpha\rceil)
.

Notation IV.4.4
.

d^{h}
is a fixed universal left r.e. supermartingale on
h^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Among the explicit uses of
h
in the proof of
IV.3.1
, Uses I, II, and III only depended on writing
h
in the form
h(n)=k(n)\cdot\ell(n)=2^{n}\cdot(n+1)
and observing that if
d^{h}(\sigma)\leq n!
for some
\sigma\in h^{n}
, then there are at most
2^{n}
many immediate successors
\tau
of
\sigma
such that
d^{h}(\tau)\geq(n+1)!
. This last observation holds in general:

Proposition IV.4.5
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
\sigma\in h^{n}
such that
d^{h}(\sigma)\leq L(n)
, there are at most
k(n)
-many immediate extensions
\tau
of
\sigma
such that
d^{h}(\tau)>L(n+1)
.

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of a contradiction that there are more than
k(n)
immediate extensions
\tau
of
\sigma
such that
d^{h}(\tau)>L(n+1)
. Thus,

\sum_{i<h(n)}{d^{h}(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)}>k(n)\cdot L(n+1)=h(n)\cdot L(n)\geq h(n)\cdot d^{h}(\sigma)

which contradicts the fact that
d^{h}
is a supermartingale.
∎

Now we turn our attention to Use IV.

Proposition IV.4.6
.

Let
g\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
be an order function. Then
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
is
g
-random in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
if the following two conditions hold:

1.

L(n)\leq K(n)^{\frac{n-g(n)}{g(n)}}
for almost all
n
.

2.

d^{h}(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\leq L(n)
for almost all
n
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
d^{h}
does not
g
-succeed on
X
, i.e., that
\limsup_{n}{d^{h}(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\cdot|h^{n}|^{\frac{g(n)}{n}-1}}<\infty
.
For all sufficiently large
n
,

\displaystyle L(n)\leq K(n)^{\frac{n}{g(n)}-1}

\displaystyle\iff L(n)\leq K(n)^{\frac{1-g(n)/n}{g(n)/n}}

\displaystyle\iff L(n)^{g(n)/n}\leq K(n)^{1-g(n)/n}

\displaystyle\iff\frac{L(n)^{g(n)/n}}{K(n)^{1-g(n)/n}}\leq 1

\displaystyle\iff\frac{L(n)^{\frac{g(n)}{n}-1+1}}{K(n)^{-(\frac{g(n)}{n}-1)}}\leq 1

\displaystyle\iff L(n)(K(n)L(n))^{\frac{g(n)}{n}-1}\leq 1

\displaystyle\iff L(n)|h^{n}|^{\frac{g(n)}{n}-1}\leq 1

\displaystyle\implies d^{h}(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\cdot|h^{n}|^{\frac{g(n)}{n}-1}\leq 1.

Thus,
\limsup_{n}{d^{h}(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\cdot|h^{n}|^{\frac{g(n)}{n}-1}}<\infty
, as desired.
∎

Given
f(n)=n-j(n)
,
IV.2.19
suggests we find a
g
of the form
g(n)=n-(1-\varepsilon)\frac{j(s(n)\cdot n)}{s(n)}
for some
\varepsilon>0
, where
|h^{n}|=H(n)=2^{s(n)\cdot n}
.
IV.4.6
suggests that if we wish for the
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
we construct to be (strongly)
g
-random, then we might as well start with
K
and define
L
by

L(n)\coloneq K(n)^{\frac{n-g(n)}{g(n)}}.

k
and
\ell
are then defined by
k(n)\coloneq K(n+1)/K(n)
and
\ell(n)\coloneq L(n+1)/L(n)
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Note that
H(n)=K(n)L(n)=K(n)^{n/g(n)}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Lemma IV.4.7
.

If
t\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
is an order function, then the function
\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
defined by
r(n)\coloneq(n+1)\cdot t(n+1)-n\cdot t(n)
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
is a recursive function which dominates an order function.

Proof.

That
r
is recursive is immediate. For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

r(n)=(n+1)\cdot t(n+1)-n\cdot t(n)=n\cdot(t(n+1)-t(n))+t(n+1).

Since
t
is nondecreasing,
n\cdot(t(n+1)-t(n))\geq 0
, and so
t(n+1)\leq r(n)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The function
\tilde{t}\colon\mathbb{N}\to{[0,\infty)}
defined by
\tilde{t}(n)\coloneq t(n+1)
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
is an order function such that
\tilde{t}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}r
.
∎

Proposition IV.4.8
.

Let
j
,
f
,
s
,
\varepsilon
, and
\tilde{j}
satisfy the conditions of
IV.4.1
. Let
g(n)\coloneq n-\tilde{j}(n)
and
K(n)=2^{s(n)\cdot g(n)}
.

1.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
k(n),\ell(n),h(n)\geq 1
.

2.

\ell
and
h
dominate order functions.

3.

If
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
is such that
d^{h}(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\leq L(n)
for almost all
n
, then
X
is strongly
g
-random in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
.

4.

If
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
is
g
-random in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
, then
\pi^{h}(X)\in[0,1]
is
f
-random in
[0,1]
.

Proof.

The functions
K,L,H,k,\ell,h
can all be written as powers of two whose exponents involve
s
,
g
, and
\tilde{j}
:

\displaystyle K(n)

\displaystyle=2^{s(n)\cdot g(n)},

\displaystyle k(n)

\displaystyle=\exp_{2}(s(n+1)\cdot g(n+1)-s(n)\cdot g(n)),

\displaystyle L(n)

\displaystyle=2^{s(n)\cdot\tilde{j}(n)},

\displaystyle\ell(n)

\displaystyle=\exp_{2}(s(n+1)\cdot\tilde{j}(n+1)-s(n)\cdot\tilde{j}(n)),

\displaystyle H(n)

\displaystyle=2^{s(n)\cdot n},

\displaystyle h(n)

\displaystyle=\exp_{2}(s(n+1)\cdot(n+1)-s(n)\cdot n).

Condition (iii) of
IV.4.1
implies
\tilde{j}
is an order function. The hypothesis that
j(n)\leq n
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
implies
\tilde{j}(n)\leq n
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
as well, so
g
is a nondecreasing function such that
g(n)\leq n
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Condition (i) implies
\im h\subseteq\mathbb{N}
.

1.

That
s
,
g
, and
\tilde{j}
are all nondecreasing implies that
k
,
\ell
, and
h
are bounded below by
1
.

2.

IV.4.7
shows that
h
and
\ell
each dominate order functions.

3.

This is simply
IV.4.6
.

4.

Using Condition (ii) of
IV.4.1
, this is simply
IV.2.19
.

∎

It remains to generalize the construction of
X
in
IV.3.1
and establish our bounds on
q
as a function of
j
.

Proof of
IV.4.1
.

Fix a rational
\varepsilon>0
and observe that fulfillment of the conditions on
s
,
j
, and
\varepsilon
do not depend the value of
\varepsilon
.

d^{h}
is left r.e., so uniformly in
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
we can simultaneously and uniformly approximate
d(\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle)
from below for all
i<h(n)
.
Uniformly in
\sigma\in h^{n}
, let
m_{\sigma}
be such that for all
x<k(n)
,
\varphi_{m_{\sigma}}(x)\downarrow=i
if and only if
\sigma{}^{\frown}\langle i\rangle
is the
x
-th immediate successor
\tau
of
\sigma
found with respect to the aforementioned procedure such that
d(\tau)>L(n)
.

Let
\#\colon h^{\ast}\to\mathbb{N}
be the inverse of the enumeration of
h^{\ast}
according to the shortlex ordering. In particular, for almost all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
\sigma\in h^{n}
,

\#(\sigma)\leq|h^{0}|+|h^{1}|+\cdots+|h^{n}|=\sum_{i=0}^{n}{2^{s(i)\cdot i}}<(n+1)\cdot 2^{s(n)\cdot n}.

By potentially modifying our enumeration
\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\ldots
of partial recursive functions, we can assume without loss of generality that
m_{\sigma}=2\#(\sigma)
. Let
m_{n}^{\ast}=(n+1)\cdot 2^{s(n)\cdot n+1}
, so that
1+\sup\{m_{\sigma}\mid\sigma\in h^{n}\}\leq m_{n}^{\ast}
for almost all
n
.

Let
\Psi_{n}\colon\mathrm{DNR}(\ell(n))\to P_{h(n)}^{1,k(n)}
be uniformly recursive functionals realizing the reductions
P_{h(n)}^{1,k(n)}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{DNR}(\ell(n))
from
IV.3.4
, and let
U_{n}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathbb{N})
be the associated recursive functions (so that
|U_{n}(i)|\leq k(n)\binom{h(n)}{\ell(n)}
). We are principally interested in initial segments
\rho
of elements of length
m_{n}^{\ast}
(in fact, we are only concerned with the values at the inputs
m_{\sigma}
for
\sigma\in h^{n}
), so that:

1.

\rho(m_{\sigma})<h(n)=h(|\sigma|)
.

2.

For all
x<k(n)
, if
\varphi_{m_{\sigma}}(x){\downarrow}
, then
\rho(m_{\sigma})\neq\varphi_{m_{\sigma}}(x)
.

Define
U\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathbb{N})
by setting
U(n)\coloneq\bigcup_{i<m_{n}^{\ast}}{U_{n}(i)}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and subsequently define
\overline{u}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
by

\displaystyle\overline{u}(0)

\displaystyle\coloneq 0,

\displaystyle\overline{u}(n+1)

\displaystyle\coloneq\overline{u}(n)+|U(n)|.

Finally, define
\psi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
by letting, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
j<|U(n)|
,

\psi(\overline{u}(n)+j)\simeq\varphi_{\text{$j$-th element of $U(n)$}}(0).

By construction, for any
Z\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(\ell(n))
,
Z{\upharpoonright}\,\overline{u}(n+1)
can be used to compute an initial segment of an element of
P_{h(n)}^{1,k(n)}
of length
m_{n}^{\ast}
, and this is uniform in
n
.

If
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is a recursive order function satisfying

p(\overline{u}(n+1))\leq\ell(n)

for almost all
n
, then uniformly in
n
and
Z\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
,
Z{\upharpoonright}\,\overline{u}(n+1)
can be used to compute an initial segment of
P_{h(n)}^{1,k(n)}
of length
m_{n}^{\ast}
. Given
Z\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
, define
G\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
by setting the value of
G(m_{\sigma})
according to this uniform process for each
\sigma\in h^{\ast}
; for
n
not of the form
m_{\sigma}
(which can be recursively checked), set
G(n)\coloneq 0
. Then define
X\in h^{\mathbb{N}}
recursively by

\displaystyle X(0)

\displaystyle\coloneq G(m_{\langle\rangle}),

\displaystyle X(n+1)

\displaystyle\coloneq G(m_{\langle X(0),X(1),\ldots X(n)\rangle}).

We claim that
d^{h}
does not
g
-succeed on
X
. We start by showing that
d^{h}(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\leq L(n)
by induction. For
n=0
, this follows since
d^{h}(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)=d^{h}(\langle\rangle)=1
. Now suppose for our induction hypothesis that
d(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\leq L(n)
. By construction, for
x<k(n)
, if
\varphi_{m_{X{\upharpoonright}\,n}}(x){\downarrow}=i
then
X(n+1)=G(m_{X{\upharpoonright}\,n})\neq i
; in combination with the induction hypothesis and
IV.4.5
, it follows that
d(X{\upharpoonright}\,(n+1))\leq L(n+1)
. By our definition of
L
and
IV.4.6
,
d^{h}
does not
g
-succeed on
X
. Equivalently,
X
is (strongly)
g
-random in
h^{\mathbb{N}}
.

IV.4.8
shows that
\pi^{h}(X)=Y\in[0,1]
is
f
-random. In other words,
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
. By
IV.4.8
,
\ell
dominates an order function, and hence there exists a recursive order function
p
satisfying
p(\overline{u}(n+1))\leq\ell(n)
. If
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is a recursive order function such that for all
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
we have
q(an+b)\leq p(n)
for almost all
n
, then

\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q).

To get a more explicit condition on
q
, we find an upper bound for
a\overline{u}(n+1)+b
. For all
n
and
i
,

|U_{n}(i)|\leq k(n)\binom{h(n)}{\ell(n)}\leq k(n)\left(\frac{h(n)e}{\ell(n)}\right)^{\ell(n)}=e^{\ell(n)}k(n)^{\ell(n)+1}=\exp_{2}((\log_{2}k(n)+\log_{2}e)\ell(n)+\log_{2}k(n)).

Thus, for all
n
,

\displaystyle|U(n)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i<m_{n}^{\ast}}{|U_{n}(i)|}

\displaystyle\leq m_{n}^{\ast}\cdot\max_{i}{U_{n}(i)}

\displaystyle\leq(n+1)\cdot 2^{s(n)\cdot n+1}\cdot\exp_{2}((\log_{2}k(n)+\log_{2}e)\ell(n)+\log_{2}k(n))

\displaystyle\leq\exp_{2}\left(\log_{2}H(n)+(\log_{2}k(n)+\log_{2}e)\ell(n)+\log_{2}k(n)+\log_{2}(n+1)+1\right).

For any
a
and
b
and almost all
n
,

\displaystyle\log_{2}\bigl(a\overline{u}(n+1)+b\bigr)

\displaystyle=\log_{2}\bigl(a\sum_{m\leq n}{|U(m)|}+b\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\log_{2}\bigl(a(n+1)\cdot|U(n)|+b\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\log_{2}\bigl(3an\cdot|U(n)|\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\log_{2}H(n)+(\log_{2}k(n)+\log_{2}e)\ell(n)+\log_{2}k(n)+2\log_{2}(n+1)+\log_{2}(3a)+1.

Substituting
\log_{2}H(n)
and
\log_{2}k(n)
with expressions in terms of
s
,
g
, and
\tilde{j}
gives

\displaystyle\log_{2}\bigl(a\overline{u}(n+1)+b\bigr)

\displaystyle=s(n)\cdot n+\bigl(s(n+1)\cdot g(n+1)-s(n)\cdot g(n)+\log_{2}e\bigr)\cdot\ell(n)+s(n+1)\cdot g(n+1)

\displaystyle\quad-s(n)\cdot g(n)+2\log_{2}(n+1)+\log_{2}(3a)+1

\displaystyle\leq 2s(n)\cdot g(n+1)+\bigl(s(n+1)\cdot g(n+1)-s(n)\cdot g(n)+\log_{2}e\bigr)\cdot\ell(n)+s(n+1)\cdot g(n+1)

\displaystyle\quad-s(n)\cdot g(n)+s(n)\cdot g(n+1)+\log_{2}(3a)+1

\displaystyle\leq s(n+1)\cdot g(n+1)\cdot(\ell(n)+4)

\displaystyle=s(n+1)\cdot\left(n+1-(1-\varepsilon)\frac{j(s(n+1)\cdot(n+1))}{s(n+1)}\right)\cdot(\ell(n)+4)

\displaystyle\leq\left(s(n+1)\cdot(n+1)-(1-\varepsilon)j(s(n+1)\cdot(n+1))\right)\cdot(\ell(n)+4)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}\left(s(n+1)\cdot(n+1)-j(s(n+1)\cdot(n+1))\right)\cdot\ell(n)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}f(s(n+1)\cdot(n+1))\cdot\ell(n)

for almost all
n
, where the final line follows from the fact that
\lim_{n\to\infty}{j(n)/n}=0
. Thus, if
q
satisfies

q\left(\exp_{2}((1-\varepsilon)^{-1}\cdot f(s(n+1)\cdot(n+1))\cdot\ell(n))\right)\leq\ell(n)

then
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
.
∎

Example IV.4.9
.

Suppose
j(n)=\sqrt{n}\log_{2}n
and let
s(n)=n
. Simplifying
\ell
gives

\displaystyle\ell(n)

\displaystyle=\exp_{2}\left(2(1-\varepsilon)\left(\sqrt{(n+1)^{2}}\log_{2}(n+1)-\sqrt{n^{2}}\log_{2}n\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\exp_{2}\left(2(1-\varepsilon)\log_{2}\left((n+1)\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\right)^{n}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left((n+1)\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\right)^{n}\right)^{2(1-\varepsilon)}.

This provides the bounds

(n+1)^{2(1-\varepsilon)}\leq\ell(n)\leq(n+1)^{2(1-\varepsilon)}\cdot e^{2(1-\varepsilon)}

and consequently

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon)^{-1}\cdot f((n+1)^{2})\cdot\ell(n)

\displaystyle\leq(1-\varepsilon)^{-1}\cdot[(n+1)^{2}-2(n+1)\log_{2}(n+1)]\cdot(n+1)^{2(1-\varepsilon)}\cdot e^{2(1-\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{e^{2(1-\varepsilon)}}{1-\varepsilon}\cdot(n+1)^{2(2-\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\leq(n+1)^{4-2\varepsilon}.

Thus,
IV.4.1
implies
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.n-\sqrt{n}\log_{2}n)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
whenever

q\left(\exp_{2}((n+1)^{2(2-\varepsilon)})\right)\leq(n+1)^{2(1-\varepsilon)}.

In particular, we may take
q(n)\coloneq\left(\log_{2}n\right)^{\beta}
for any
\beta<1/2
. In other words, whereas
IV.3.1
shows that
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.n-\alpha\sqrt{n}\log_{2}n)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(\lambda n.(\log_{2}n)^{\beta})
for any
\alpha>1
and
\beta<1/2
,
IV.4.1
shows that for the same order functions
q
, we in fact are able to compute
(\lambda n.n-\sqrt{n}\log_{2}n)
-complex sequences.

Example

IV.4.9
can be generalized further to address functions of the form
f(n)=n-\sqrt{n}\cdot\Delta(n)
:

Theorem IV.4.10
.

Given an order function
\Delta\colon\mathbb{N}\to[0,\infty)
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}{\Delta(n)/\sqrt{n}}=0
and any rational
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,

\mathrm{COMPLEX}\bigl(\lambda n.n-\sqrt{n}\cdot\Delta(n)\bigr)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}\bigl(\lambda n.\exp_{2}\bigl((1-\varepsilon)\Delta(\log_{2}\log_{2}n)\bigr)\bigr).

More generally,
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.n-\sqrt{n}\cdot\Delta(n))\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
for any order function
q
satisfying

q\left(\exp_{2}((1-\varepsilon)^{-1}\cdot[(n+1)^{2}-(n+1)\cdot\Delta((n+1)^{2})]\cdot\ell(n))\right)\leq\ell(n)

for almost all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\ell(n)=\exp_{2}\left((1-\varepsilon)[(n+1)\cdot\Delta((n+1)^{2})-n\cdot\Delta(n^{2})]\right)
.

Proof.

Let
s(n)\coloneq n
and
j(n)\coloneq\sqrt{n}\cdot\Delta(n)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We show that the conditions of
IV.4.1
are fulfilled with these choices of
s
and
j
:

1.

(n+1)^{2}-n^{2}=2n+1
and
2^{2n+1}\in\mathbb{N}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

2.

Immediate.

3.

\frac{j(n^{2})}{n}=\frac{\sqrt{n^{2}}\cdot\Delta(n^{2})}{n}=\Delta(n^{2})
shows that the function
n\mapsto j(s(n)\cdot n)/s(n)
is an order function.

The condition given by
IV.4.1
requires that for some
\varepsilon>0
and almost all
n

q\left(\exp_{2}((1-\varepsilon)^{-1}\cdot[(n+1)^{2}-(n+1)\cdot\Delta((n+1)^{2})]\cdot\ell(n))\right)\leq\ell(n),

where
\ell(n)=\exp_{2}((1-\varepsilon)((n+1)\cdot\Delta((n+1)^{2})-n\cdot\Delta(n^{2})))
. Rearranging the exponent of
\ell(n)
gives a simple lower bound:

\log_{2}\ell(n)=(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\left(\Delta((n+1)^{2})+n\cdot(\Delta((n+1)^{2})-\Delta(n^{2}))\right)\geq(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\Delta((n+1)^{2})\geq(1-\varepsilon)\Delta(n^{2}).

Concerning the exponent of
q
’s argument, we have the following: for almost all
n
,

(1-\varepsilon)\cdot[(n+1)^{2}-(n+1)\cdot\Delta((n+1)^{2})]\cdot\ell(n)\leq(1-\varepsilon)\cdot(n+1)^{2}\cdot\exp_{2}\left((1-\varepsilon)\cdot(n+1)\cdot\Delta((n+1)^{2})\right)\leq 2^{n^{2}}.

Thus,
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.n-\sqrt{n}\cdot\Delta(n))\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
if
q(\exp_{2}\exp_{2}n^{2})\leq\exp_{2}((1-\varepsilon)\Delta(n^{2}))
and hence if the following stronger condition holds:

q(n)\leq\exp((1-\varepsilon)\Delta(\log_{2}\log_{2}n)).

∎

Remark IV.4.11
.

Setting
q(n)\coloneq\exp_{2}((1-\varepsilon)\Delta(\log_{2}\log_{2}n))
can be very inefficient; when
\Delta(n)=\log_{2}n
, this gives
q(n)=\left(\log_{2}\log_{2}n\right)^{1-\varepsilon}
, significantly slower than the lower bound on
q
established in
Example

IV.4.9
.

A better bound can be given for well-behaved
\Delta
which are dominated by
\log_{2}
. Suppose
\Delta\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}\log_{2}
and that

c\coloneq\lim_{n\to\infty}{n[\Delta((n+1)^{2})-\Delta(n^{2})]}<\infty.

Then we may give the following lower and upper bounds for
\ell
: for almost all
n
,

\exp_{2}((1-\varepsilon)\Delta((n+1)^{2}))\leq\ell(n)\leq\exp_{2}((1-\varepsilon)\Delta((n+1)^{2})+c)\leq 2^{c}\cdot(n+1)^{2(1-\varepsilon)}.

Consequently,
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.n-\sqrt{n}\cdot\Delta(n))\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
whenever
q
is such that, for almost all
n
,

q(n)\leq\exp_{2}\left((1-\varepsilon)\Delta\left(\left[(1-\varepsilon)\cdot 2^{-c}\cdot\log_{2}n\right]^{1/(2-\varepsilon)}\right)\right).

IV.5
Open Questions

IV.4.1
and
IV.4.10
only provide partial answers to
IV.0.1
, leaving that question open in general.

It is unclear what the full extent of the coverage of
IV.4.1
is, suggesting the following question:

Question IV.5.1
.

For what sub-identical order functions
f
is there a computable function
s\colon\mathbb{N}\to[1,\infty)
satisfying the conditions of
IV.4.1
?

E.g., does such an
s
exist when
f=\lambda n.n-\log_{2}n
, or even when
f=\lambda n.n-\sqrt[3]{n}
?

Questions about the optimality of
q
in
IV.4.1
and
IV.4.10
also remain:

Question IV.5.2
.

For a given sub-identical order function
f
, can we provide an upper bound on how slowly
q
must grow for
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
to hold?

An affirmative answer to
III.0.6
would put a strong bound on how slow-growing
q
must be.

Question IV.5.3
.

Is there a fast-growing order function
q
such that
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.n-\sqrt{n})\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
?

Currently, there is little in the direction of answering
III.0.6
or
IV.5.3
. In fact, the following specific – seemingly tame – instance of
III.0.6
remains open:

Question IV.5.4
.

Does there exist a fast-growing order function
p
for which
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(1/2)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
?

Chapter V
Generalized Shift Complexity

\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)
is non-negligible for every order function
f
such that
\limsup_{n}{(f(n)-n)}<\infty
. One notion of complexity with corresponding weak degrees that lies in the deep region of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
is that of
shift
complexity.

Definition V.0.1

(shift complexity)
.

X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is…

…

\langle\delta,c\rangle
-shift complex
(where
\delta\in(0,1)
and
c\in\mathbb{N}
) if
\pfc(\tau)\geq\delta|\tau|-c
for every substring
\tau
of
X
. The set of all
\langle\delta,c\rangle
-shift complex sequences is denoted by
\mathrm{SC}(\delta,c)
.

…

\delta
-shift complex
if
X
is
\langle\delta,c\rangle
-shift complex for some
c\in\mathbb{N}
. The set of all
\delta
-shift complex sequences is denoted by
\mathrm{SC}(\delta)
.

…

shift complex
if
X
is
\delta
-shift complex for some
\delta\in(0,1)
. The set of all shift complex sequences is denoted by
\mathrm{SC}
.

For
f
an order function satisfying
\limsup_{n}{(f(n)-n)}<\infty
, we know that
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)\neq\emptyset
since
\lambda(\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f))=1
. In contrast, it is nontrivial that
\mathrm{SC}(\delta)\neq\emptyset
. The existence of a
1/3
-shift complex sequence was first shown by Durand, Levin, & Shen in
[
7
, Lemma 1]
in an investigation about complex tilings. Later, in a paper of the same name, Durand, Levin, & Shen proved more generally the existence of a
\delta
-shift complex sequence for each
\delta\in(0,1)
(
[
8
, Lemma 1]
) by explicitly constructing such a
\delta
-shift complex real segment by segment, using prefix-free symmetry of information at each stage of the construction. In
[
22
, Proposition 2]
, Rumyantsev & Ushakov gave a probabilistic approach to the existence of
\delta
-shift complex sequences, using the Lovasz Local Lemma to prove that arbitrarily long finite
\delta
-shift complex strings (defined in the obvious way) exist and then appealing to compactness to show that a
\delta
-shift complex sequence exists. Yet another approach by Miller & Khan (
[
18
, Corollary 2.4]
,
[
15
, Corollary 3.3]
) proves existence from the perspective of subshifts.

In
[
23
]
, Rumyantsev observes that the proof in
[
8
, Lemma 1]
actually gives a stronger existence result:

Theorem
.

[
7
, Lemma 1, essentially]

[
23
, Lemma 2]

For each
\delta\in(0,1)
, there exists
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\pfc(\langle X(k),X(k+1),\ldots,X(k+n-1)\rangle\mid k,n)\geq\delta n-c

for all
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
.

In light of the above theorem and the results
V.3.3
and
V.3.14
, we propose the following definition:

Notation V.0.2
.

Suppose
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
i\leq j
are natural numbers. Define
X({[i,j)})
to be the string
\langle X(i),X(i+1),\ldots,X(j-1)\rangle
of length
j-i
.

Definition V.0.3

(strong shift complexity)
.

X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is…

…

strongly
\langle\delta,c\rangle
-shift complex
(where
\delta\in(0,1)
and
c\in\mathbb{N}
) if
\pfc(X({[k,k+n)})\mid k,n)\geq\delta n-c
for all
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
. The set of all strongly
\langle\delta,c\rangle
-shift complex sequences is denoted by
\mathrm{SSC}(\delta,c)
.

…

strongly
\delta
-shift complex
if
X
is strongly
\langle\delta,c\rangle
-shift complex for some
c\in\mathbb{N}
. The set of all strongly
\delta
-shift complex sequences is denoted by
\mathrm{SSC}(\delta)
.

…

strongly shift complex
if
X
is strongly
\delta
-shift complex for some
\delta\in(0,1)
. The set of all strongly shift complex sequences is denoted by
\mathrm{SSC}
.

In
Section

V.1
, we examine the mass problems
\mathrm{SC}(\delta)
, showing that
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{SC}(\delta))\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
and examining where in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
the weak degrees
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{SC}(\delta))
lie. To that end, we prove:

Theorem
V.1.3
.

[
23
, Theorem 3, essentially]

\mathrm{SC}(\delta,c)
is a deep
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class for all rational
\delta\in(0,1)
and
c\in\mathbb{N}
.

Our proof of
V.1.3
follows the proof of
[
23
, Theorem 3]
and the remark of Bievenu & Porter that Rumyantsev’s proof exhibits the uniformity necessary to prove depth instead of just negligibility, but providing further detail. Another known fact about
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{SC}(\delta))
is the following:

Theorem
.

[
15
, Theorem 6.3]

For each rational
\delta\in(0,1)
there is an order function
h
such that
\mathrm{SC}(\delta)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{DNR}(h)
.

We improve
[
15
, Theorem 6.3]
by replacing
\mathrm{DNR}
with
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
and providing explicit bounds:

Theorem
V.1.7
.

Given rational numbers
0<\delta<\alpha<1
, define
\pi\colon\mathbb{N}\to(0,\infty)
by
\pi(n)\coloneq 2^{(\alpha-\delta)n}
. Then
\mathrm{SC}(\delta)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
for any order function
q
such that
q(2^{(n+1)\cdot\pi(n)})\leq\pi(n)
for almost all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Corollary
V.1.8
.

Fix a rational
\varepsilon>0
. For all rational
\delta\in(0,1)
we have
\mathrm{SC}(\delta)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(\lambda n.(\log_{2}n)^{1-\varepsilon})
.

In
Section

V.2
, we provide generalizations of the notions of
\delta
-shift complexity and strong
\delta
-shift complexity in analogy with that of
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(f)
, giving rise to the class
\mathrm{SC}(f)
and
\mathrm{SSC}(f)
for
f
a sub-identical order function.

In
Section

V.3
, we give a detailed proof of a result of Rumyantsev (
[
23
, Theorem 4]
) that shows that for certain sufficiently slow-growing order functions
f
,
\mathrm{SC}(f)
is non-negligible. This is strengthened to build a relationship between
\mathrm{SC}(f)
and
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)
for another sub-identical order function
g
.

Theorem
V.3.7
.

Suppose
f
and
g
are sub-identical order functions such that
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}{f(2^{m})/2^{m}}<\infty
and there is a recursive sequence
\langle\varepsilon_{m}\rangle_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
of positive rationals for which
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}{\varepsilon_{m}}<\infty
and such that

\liminf_{m}{\frac{g(2^{m}\varepsilon_{m})-f(2\cdot 2^{m})}{m}}>1.

Then
\mathrm{SC}(f)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)
.

We also show that for sufficiently slow-growing order functions
f
there is an order function
g
such that
\mathrm{SC}(f)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)
and which is
sublinear
(i.e.,
\lim_{n\to\infty}{g(n)/n}=0
):

Theorem
V.3.12
.

Suppose
f
is a sub-identical order function such that
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}{\frac{f(2^{m})}{2^{m}}}
converges to a recursive real. Then there is a sublinear order function
g
such that
\mathrm{SC}(f)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(g)
.

In contrast, Rumyantsev has shown (
[
23
, Theorem 5]
) that for every order function
f
,
\mathrm{SSC}(f)
is negligible. We give a careful and detailed presentation of Rumyantsev’s proof to give a slightly stronger result:

Theorem
V.3.14
.

\mathrm{SSC}(f,c)
is a deep
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class for any order function
f
satisfying
\limsup_{n}{\frac{f(n)}{n}}<1
and any
c\in\mathbb{N}
.

V.1
\delta
-Shift Complexity as a Mass Problem

The weak degrees of
\mathrm{SC}(\delta,c)
,
\mathrm{SC}(\delta)
, and
\mathrm{SC}
all lie in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

Proposition V.1.1
.

Suppose
\delta\in(0,1)
is rational and
c\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
\mathrm{SC}(\delta,c)
is
\Pi^{0}_{1}
, uniformly in
\delta
,
c
. Consequently,
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{SC}(\delta,c))
(for
c
sufficiently large),
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{SC}(\delta))
, and
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{SC})
all lie in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

Proof.

Let
U
be the universal prefix-free machine for which
\pfc=\pfc_{U}
and then let
e\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
\varphi_{e}(\str^{-1}\sigma)\simeq\str^{-1}U(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
. Then

\mathrm{SC}(\delta,c)=\{X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\forall n\forall k\forall\sigma\forall s\,(\varphi_{e,s}(\str^{-1}\sigma){\downarrow}=X{\upharpoonright}\,n\to|\sigma|>\delta n-c)\}

shows
\mathrm{SC}(\delta,c)
is
\Pi^{0}_{1}
. For all sufficiently large
c
,
\mathrm{SC}(\delta,c)
is nonempty, so for such
c
we have
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{SC}(\delta,c))\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

\mathrm{SC}(\delta,c)
being
\Pi^{0}_{1}
(uniformly in
\delta
and
c
) implies
\mathrm{SC}(\delta)=\bigcup_{c\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathrm{SC}(\delta,c)}
and
\mathrm{SC}=\bigcup_{n,c\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathrm{SC}(2^{-n},c)}
are both
\Sigma^{0}_{2}
, so the
Proposition I.4.17

(Embedding Lemma)
.
implies
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{SC}(\delta))
and
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{SC})
both lie in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.
∎

The same is true of
\mathrm{SSC}(\delta,c)
,
\mathrm{SSC}(\delta)
, and
\mathrm{SSC}
.

Proposition V.1.2
.

Suppose
\delta\in(0,1)
is rational and
c\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
\mathrm{SSC}(\delta,c)
is
\Pi^{0}_{1}
, uniformly in
\delta
,
c
. Consequently,
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{SSC}(\delta,c))
(for
c
sufficiently large),
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{SSC}(\delta))
, and
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{SSC})
all lie in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

Proof.

Let
U
be the universal oracle prefix-free machine for which conditional prefix-free complexity is defined with respect to. Let
e\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
\varphi_{e}^{\tau}(\str^{-1}\sigma)\simeq\str^{-1}U^{\overline{\tau}}(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
. Then

\mathrm{SSC}(\delta,c)=\{X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\forall n\forall k\forall\sigma\forall s\,(\varphi_{e,s}^{\str\pi^{(2)}(n,k)}(\str^{-1}\sigma){\downarrow}=X{\upharpoonright}\,n\to|\sigma|>\delta n-c)\}

shows
\mathrm{SSC}(\delta,c)
is
\Pi^{0}_{1}
. For all sufficiently large
c
,
\mathrm{SSC}(\delta,c)
is nonempty, so for such
c
we have
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{SSC}(\delta,c))\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

\mathrm{SSC}(\delta,c)
being
\Pi^{0}_{1}
(uniformly in
\delta
and
c
) implies
\mathrm{SSC}(\delta)=\bigcup_{c\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathrm{SSC}(\delta,c)}
and
\mathrm{SSC}=\bigcup_{n,c\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathrm{SSC}(2^{-n},c)}
are both
\Sigma^{0}_{2}
, so the
Proposition I.4.17

(Embedding Lemma)
.
implies
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{SSC}(\delta))
and
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{SSC})
both lie in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.
∎

V.1.1
Shift Complexity and Depth

Unlike
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\delta,c)
,
\mathrm{SC}(\delta,c)
is a deep
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class for every computable
\delta\in(0,1)
and
c\in\mathbb{N}
. Our proof is essentially a more detailed presentation of the proof given by Rumyantsev in
[
23
, Theorem 3, essentially]
plus the uniformity observation by Bienvenu & Porter given in
[
2
]
.

Theorem V.1.3
.

[
23
, Theorem 3, essentially]

\mathrm{SC}(\delta,c)
is a deep
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class for all rational
\delta\in(0,1)
and
c\in\mathbb{N}
.

To prove
V.1.3
, we make use of the following probabilistic lemma:

Lemma V.1.4
.

[
23
, Lemma 6]

Suppose
\delta\in(0,1)
is rational. For every rational
\varepsilon>0
and
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
there exist natural numbers
n<N
and random variables
\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{A}_{n+1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{N}
such that

1.

\mathcal{A}_{i}
is a subset of
\{0,1\}^{i}
of size at most
2^{\delta i}
,

2.

for every string
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{N}
, the probability that
\sigma
has no substring in
\bigcup_{i=n}^{N}{\mathcal{A}_{i}}
is less than
\varepsilon
, and

3.

n\geq n_{0}
.

Moreover, the natural numbers
n,N\in\mathbb{N}
and the (probability distributions of the) random variables
\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{A}_{n+1},\\
\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{N}
can be found effectively as functions of
\varepsilon
and
n_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
m\geq 2
be such that
\delta>\frac{1}{m}
. We define natural numbers

n=n_{1}<n_{2}<\cdots<n_{m}=N

satisfying the following properties:

1.

n_{k}
divides
n_{k+1}
for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\}
.

2.

\delta n_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
. (Assuming (i) holds, it suffices for
\delta n
to be a natural number.)

When
i
is not of the form
n_{k}
for
k\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
, we will define
\mathcal{A}_{i}
to take the constant value
\emptyset
, leaving only
\mathcal{A}_{n_{k}}
to define. Roughly speaking, we will define the random variables
\mathcal{A}_{n_{k}}
to address strings
\sigma
which exhibit relatively few substrings of increasingly greater lengths.

Suppose
\sigma
is a string of length
p
,
q
divides
p
, and
\alpha\in(0,1)
satisfies
\alpha q\in\mathbb{N}
. If
\sigma
has less than
2^{\alpha q}
substrings of length
q
, then we may encode
\sigma
in the following way. Writing
\sigma=\sigma_{1}{}^{\frown}\sigma_{2}{}^{\frown}\cdots{}^{\frown}\sigma_{p/q}
where
|\sigma_{k}|=q
, our hypothesis implies
|\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{p/q}\}|<2^{\alpha q}
(our hypothesis is much stronger than this, but is the natural hypothesis for the remaining arguments in the proof). Let
\rho
be a string encoding, in lexicographical order, the distinct elements of
\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{p/q}\}
; for exactness, we may encode a finite sequence
\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{n}
of strings by

\nu_{1}^{0}{}^{\frown}\langle 1,1\rangle{}^{\frown}\nu_{2}^{0}{}^{\frown}\langle 1,1\rangle{}^{\frown}\cdots{}^{\frown}\nu_{n}^{0}{}^{\frown}\langle 1,1\rangle

where
\nu_{i}^{0}
denotes the string
\langle\nu_{i}(0),0,\nu_{i}(1),0,\nu_{i}(2),0,\ldots,\nu_{i}(|\nu_{i}|-1),0\rangle
. Then we may encode
\sigma
by

\tau=\tau_{1}{}^{\frown}\tau_{2}{}^{\frown}\cdots{}^{\frown}\tau_{p/q}{}^{\frown}\rho

where
\tau_{i}
is the binary representation of the index of
\sigma_{i}
in
\rho
(regarding
\rho
as a finite sequence of strings). It is convenient to ensure
\tau
has a length depending only on
p
and
q
, so we appropriately pad each
\tau_{i}
by
0
’s to ensure that
|\tau_{i}|=\alpha q
(
\rho
encodes at most
2^{\alpha q}-1
strings and hence requires at most
\alpha q
bits to describe) and appropriately pad
\rho
by
1
’s to ensure that
|\rho|=2(q+1)(2^{\alpha q}-1)
(the length of
\rho
if it encodes the maximum number of
2^{\alpha q}-1
strings). Thus, to each such string
\sigma
of length
p
we associate with it a unique string
\tau
of length
\alpha p+2(q+1)(2^{\alpha q}-1)
.

Supposing
n_{k}
has already been defined and
n_{k}
divides
i
, we say that a string
\sigma
of length
i
is
k
-sparse
if it has less than
2^{\frac{m-k}{m}n_{k}}
substrings of length
n_{k}
. As observed above, we may encode a
k
-sparse
\sigma
with a string of length
\frac{m-k}{m}|\sigma|+2(n_{k}+1)(2^{\frac{m-k}{m}n_{k}}-1)
. For simplicity, we write
c_{k+1}\coloneq 2(n_{k}+1)(2^{\frac{m-k}{m}n_{k}}-1)
.

We may now define
n_{k}
and the associated random variable
\mathcal{A}_{n_{k}}
for
k\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
.

k=1
.

Let
n_{1}=n
be the least natural number greater than
n_{0}
such that
\delta n\in\mathbb{N}
and

\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{n/m}}\right)^{2^{\delta n}}<\varepsilon.

Note that such an
n
exists as

\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{n/m}}\right)^{2^{\delta n}}=\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{n/m}}\right)^{2^{n/m}\cdot 2^{(\delta-1/m)n}}=\left(\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{n/m}}\right)^{2^{n/m}}\right)^{2^{(\delta-1/m)n}}\approx\left(\frac{1}{e}\right)^{2^{(\delta-1/m)n}}

with both the approximation getting tighter and the final expression tending toward
0
as
n\to\infty
.

Then
\mathcal{A}_{n_{1}}=\mathcal{A}_{n}
is defined to be randomly chosen uniformly among all subsets of
\{0,1\}^{n}
of size
2^{\delta n}
(i.e., each such subset of
\{0,1\}^{n}
of size
2^{\delta n}
has an equal probability
\binom{2^{n}}{2^{\delta n}}
of being the value of
\mathcal{A}_{n}
).

1<k<m
.

Suppose
n_{k-1}
has been defined. Then
n_{k}
is the least multiple of
n_{k-1}
such that

\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{n_{k}/m+c_{k}}}\right)^{2^{\delta n_{k}}}=\left(\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{n_{k}/m+c_{k}}}\right)^{2^{n_{k}/m+c_{k}}}\right)^{2^{(\delta-1/m)n_{k}-c_{k}}}<\varepsilon.

(That such an
n_{k}
exists is analogous to the case where
k=1
.)

Then
\mathcal{A}_{n_{k}}
is defined to be randomly chosen uniformly among all subsets of
\{0,1\}^{n_{k}}
of size
2^{\delta n_{k}}
which consist only of
(k-1)
-sparse strings.

k=m
.

Let
n_{k}=n_{m}=N
to be the least multiple of
n_{m-1}
such that
\frac{1}{m}N+c_{m}<\delta N
(by hypothesis,
1/m<\delta
and
c_{m}
is constant with respect to
N
, so there is such an
N
).

Then
\mathcal{A}_{N}
is defined to be constantly equal to the set of all
(m-1)
-sparse strings of length
N
(note that an
(m-1)
-sparse string is described uniquely by a string of length
\frac{m-(m-1)}{m}N+c_{m}=\frac{1}{m}N+c_{m}
, so
|\mathcal{A}_{N}|\leq 2^{N/m+c_{m}}<2^{\delta N}
).

Finally, we show that for every
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{N}
,

\Prob(\textsf{$\mathcal{A}_{i}$ has no substring of $\sigma$ for all $i\in\{n,\ldots,N\}$})<\varepsilon.

It suffices to show that
\Prob(\textsf{$\mathcal{A}_{i}$ has no substring of $\sigma$})<\varepsilon
for at least one
i\in\{n,\ldots,N\}
.

Case 1:

Suppose
\sigma
is not
1
-sparse, so that
\sigma
has at least
2^{\frac{m-1}{m}n}
substrings of length
n
. Because of the definition of the output distribution of
\mathcal{A}_{n}
, the probability that
\sigma
has no substring in
\mathcal{A}_{n}
is at most the probability that
\sigma
has no substring among
2^{\delta n}
strings chosen uniformly and indepedently at random (the latter probability may be higher because we allow duplicates). The independence and uniformity of those
2^{\delta n}
random choices means that the latter probability is equal to
\Prob(\textsf{$\tau\in\{0,1\}^{n}$ is not a substring of $\sigma$})^{2^{\delta n}}
.
\Prob(\textsf{$\tau\in\{0,1\}^{n}$ is not a substring of $\sigma$})
is at most the probability that a random
\tau\in\{0,1\}^{n}
(chosen uniformly) is not in a set of size
2^{\frac{m-1}{m}n}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\Prob(\textsf{$\mathcal{A}_{n}$ has no substring of $\sigma$})

\displaystyle\quad\leq\Prob(\textsf{$2^{\delta n}$ random strings of length $n$ are not substrings of $\sigma$})

\displaystyle\quad\leq\Prob(\textsf{random string of length $n$ is not substring of $\sigma$})^{2^{\delta n}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left(1-\frac{2^{\frac{m-1}{m}n}}{2^{n}}\right)^{2^{\delta n}}

\displaystyle\quad=\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{n/m}}\right)^{2^{\delta n}}

\displaystyle\quad<\varepsilon.

Case 2:

Suppose
\sigma
is
1
-sparse but is not
k
-sparse for some
k\in\{2,\ldots,m-1\}
. Assume
k
is minimal with that property, so
\sigma
is not
k
-sparse (and hence has at least
2^{\frac{m-k}{m}n_{k}}
substrings of length
n_{k}
) but is
(k-1)
-sparse. As in Case 1, we have

\displaystyle\Prob(\textsf{$\mathcal{A}_{n_{k}}$ has no substring of $\sigma$})

\displaystyle\quad\leq\Prob(\textsf{$2^{\delta n_{k}}$ random $(k-1)$-sparse strings of length $n_{k}$ are not substrings of $\sigma$})

\displaystyle\quad\leq\Prob(\textsf{random $(k-1)$-sparse string of length $n_{k}$ is not substring of $\sigma$})^{2^{\delta n_{k}}}.

The probability
\Prob(\textsf{random $(k-1)$-sparse string of length $n_{k}$ is not substring of $\sigma$})
is of the form

\Prob(E\setminus F)
, where
E=\{\tau\in\{0,1\}^{n_{k}}\mid\text{$\tau$ is $(k-1)$-sparse}\}
and
F=\{\tau\in\{0,1\}^{n_{k}}\mid\text{$\tau$ is a substring of $\sigma$}\}
. Observe that
F\subseteq E
: if a substring of
\sigma
is not
(k-1)
-sparse, then it contains at least
2^{\frac{m-(k-1)}{m}n_{k-1}}
substrings of length
n_{k-1}
, and hence
\sigma
does as well, contrary to the hypothesis that
\sigma
is
(k-1)
-sparse. Because
E
is finite, we have
\Prob(E\setminus F)=1-\frac{|F|}{|E|}
. To get an upper bound on
\Prob(E\setminus F)
, it suffices to have an upper bound on
|E|
and a lower bound on
|F|
. Thus,

\displaystyle\Prob(\textsf{random $(k-1)$-sparse string of length $n_{k}$ is not substring of $\sigma$})^{2^{\delta n_{k}}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left(1-\frac{2^{\frac{m-k}{m}n_{k}}}{2^{\frac{m-k+1}{m}n_{k}+c_{k}}}\right)^{2^{\delta n_{k}}}

\displaystyle\quad=\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{n_{k}/m+c_{k}}}\right)^{2^{\delta n_{k}}}

\displaystyle\quad<\varepsilon.

Case 3:

Suppose
\sigma
is
k
-sparse for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\}
. In particular,
\sigma
is
(m-1)
-sparse and so an element of
\mathcal{A}_{N}
. Thus,
\Prob(\textsf{$\mathcal{A}_{N}$ has no substring of $\sigma$})=0<\varepsilon
.

∎

Proof of
V.1.3
.

With
\mathbf{M}
a universal left r.e. continuous semimeasure on
\{0,1\}^{\ast}
, by
II.4.3
there is a partial recursive functional
\Psi
such that for every
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}
,

\mathbf{M}(\sigma)=\lambda(\Psi^{-1}(\sigma))=\lambda(\{X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\Psi^{X}\supseteq\sigma\}).

Say that
Y\in\{0,1\}^{\ast}\cup\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}

avoids
a
k
-tuple of finite sets of strings
\langle A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{k}\rangle
if
|Y|\geq\max\{|\sigma|\mid\sigma\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}{A_{i}}\}
and no substring of
Y
is an element of
\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}{A_{i}}
. Finite sets of strings in
\{0,1\}^{\ast}
are implicitly Gödel numbered by some recursive bijection
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\{0,1\}^{\ast})\to\mathbb{N}
.

Our approach, roughly, involves us finding natural numbers
n<N
and sets
A_{n},A_{n+1},\ldots,A_{N}
satisfying the following conditions:

1.

A_{i}
is a subset of
\{0,1\}^{i}
of size at most
2^{\delta i}
for each
i\in\{n,n+1,\ldots,N\}
and

2.

\lambda(\{X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\text{$\Psi(X)$ avoids $A_{n},A_{n+1},\ldots,A_{N}$}\})<\varepsilon
.

The claim is then that each element
\tau
of
\bigcup_{i=n}^{N}{A_{i}}
satisfies
\pfc(\tau)<\delta|\tau|-c
, so
\Psi(X)
being
\langle\delta,c\rangle
-shift complex implies that
\Psi(X)
avoids the sets
A_{n},A_{n+1},\ldots,A_{N}
. Then

\lambda(\{X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\Psi(X)\in\mathrm{SC}(\delta,c)\})\leq\lambda(\{X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\text{$\Psi(X)$ avoids $A_{n},A_{n+1},\ldots,A_{N}$}\})<\varepsilon.

There are two issues that we must work around, the first being that
\Psi(X)
need not be an element of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, and the second of which is that an element
\tau
of
\bigcup_{j=n}^{N}{A_{j}}
need not necessarily satisfy
\pfc(\tau)<\delta|\tau|-c
.

We start with addressing the first issue. Our use of avoidance only requires that
|\Psi^{X}|\geq N
.
V.1.4
shows that as a recursive function of
\langle n_{0},m\rangle\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, we can find natural numbers
n<N
and random variables
\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{A}_{n+1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{N}
such that:

1.

n\geq n_{0}
,

2.

\mathcal{A}_{k}
is a subset of
\{0,1\}^{k}
of size at most
2^{\delta k/3}
(the use of
\delta/3
instead of
\delta
will become apparent when dealing with the second issue) for each
k\in\{n,n+1,\ldots,N\}
, and

3.

for every string
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{N}
, the probability that
\sigma
has no substring in
\bigcup_{i=n}^{N}{\mathcal{A}_{i}}
is less than
2^{-m}
.

Fix
n_{0}
and
m
and let
n<N
and
\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{A}_{n+1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{N}
be as above.

Let
S\coloneq\{X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid|\Psi^{X}|\geq N\}
.

S
is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
, so there exists a recursive sequence
\langle\sigma_{n}\rangle_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of pairwise incompatible strings such that
S=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\left\llbracket\sigma_{n}\right\rrbracket_{2}}
. Let
\alpha\coloneq\lambda(S)
, so that
\langle\lambda(\bigcup_{k\leq n}{\left\llbracket\sigma_{k}\right\rrbracket_{2}})\rangle_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a recursive sequence converging monotonically to
\alpha
from below. For
i\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\alpha_{i}\coloneq i\cdot 2^{-m}/3
, and let
i_{0}
be the largest
i
for which
\alpha_{i}<\alpha
, so that
\alpha-\alpha_{i_{0}}<2^{-(m+1)}
. Finally, define

\tilde{S}\coloneq\bigcup_{k\leq p}{\left\llbracket\rho_{k}\right\rrbracket_{2}}

where
p
is the smallest natural number for which
\lambda(\tilde{S})\geq\alpha_{i_{0}}
.
\tilde{S}
is a recursive subset of
S
and
\lambda(S\setminus\tilde{S})<2^{-(m+1)}
. By virtue of being a subset of
S
,
|\Psi^{X}|\geq N
for all
X\in\tilde{S}
.
\tilde{S}
is recursive and an index for
\tilde{S}
can be computed from
n_{0}
,
m
, and
i_{0}
. Define a probability measure
\mu
on
\{0,1\}^{N}
by setting

\mu(\{\sigma\})\coloneq\lambda(\tilde{S})^{-1}\cdot\lambda(\{X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\text{$X\in\tilde{S}$ and $\Psi^{X}\supseteq\sigma$}\})

for each
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{N}
.
\mu
is a computable measure and an index for
\mu
can be computed from
n_{0}
,
m
, and
i_{0}
.

Let
\nu
be the probability measure on
\mathcal{P}(\{0,1\}^{n})\times\mathcal{P}(\{0,1\}^{n+1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{P}(\{0,1\}^{N})
defined by

\nu(\{\langle A_{n},A_{n+1},\ldots,A_{N}\rangle\})\coloneq\Prob(\textsf{$\mathcal{A}_{i}=A_{i}$ for each $i\in\{n,n+1,\ldots,N\}$})

(i.e., the joint probability distribution made up of the output distributions of the random variables
\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{A}_{n+1},\\
\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{N}
). Write

E\coloneq\{\langle X,\langle A_{n},\ldots,A_{N}\rangle\rangle\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\times\prod_{i=n}^{N}{\mathcal{P}(\{0,1\}^{i})}\mid\text{$X\in\tilde{S}$ and $\Phi^{X}$ avoids $A_{n},\ldots,A_{N}$}\}.

By Fubini’s Theorem,

\displaystyle\int{\left(\int{\chi_{E}(X,(A_{n},\ldots,A_{N}))\,\mathrm{d}\lambda}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\nu}
