p_{2}(k)\geq(\varphi_{e}(N_{i})+1)\cdot p_{2}(n_{i})
(
k
is defined because
\varphi_{e}(N_{i}){\downarrow}
and
N_{i}\in\im{u}
). Let
M
be the least natural number such that

1.

M>\max_{\sigma\in p_{2}^{k}}{{u}(\theta_{2}(\sigma,i,e,N_{i}))}
,

2.

M\in\im({u})
,

3.

and
\sum_{N_{i}<{u}(j)<M}{Q(e,N_{i})^{-1}}\geq 1
.

If the inequality
N_{i}<x<M
fails, then
Q(e,x)
,
s(e,x)
, and
i(e,x)
all diverge. Otherwise:

Subcase 1.

If
x<M-1
, then set
Q(e,x)\coloneq Q(e,x-1)
,
s(e,x)\coloneq s(e,x-1)
, and
i(e,x)\coloneq i(e,x-1)
.

Subcase 2.

If
x=M-1
, then set
Q(e,x)\coloneq Q(e,x-1)
,
s(e,x)\coloneq 0
,
i(e,x)\coloneq i(e,x-1)+1
, and
N(e,i(e,x))\coloneq M
.

By the Recursion Theorem, there exists an
e\in\mathbb{N}
such that
Q(e,x)\simeq\varphi_{e}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{N}
. From the construction of
Q
, if
Q(e,j)
is defined for all
j<x
then
Q(e,x)
is defined; along with the fact that
Q(e,0)
is defined, it follows that
\varphi_{e}
is a total recursive function. The construction of
N
also ensures that
N(e,i)\in\im u
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. We will write

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c c c c c}q\coloneq\varphi_{e},&s(x)\coloneq s(e,x),&i(x)\coloneq i(e,x),&N_{i}\coloneq N(e,i),&n_{i}\coloneq u^{-1}(N_{i}),\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{c c c}\theta_{1}(\sigma,i)\coloneq\theta_{1}(\sigma,i,e),&\overline{\theta}_{1}(x)\coloneq\max\limits_{j<x,\sigma\in q^{x}}{\theta_{1}(\sigma,j,e)},&\theta_{2}(\sigma,i)\coloneq\theta_{2}(\sigma,i,e,N_{i}).\end{array}\end{array}

Each of these are total recursive functions.

Both
s(x)=0
and
s(x)=1
occur for infinitely many
x\in\mathbb{N}
. That Case 1 (
s(x-1)=0
) occurs infinitely often implies that
q
is unbounded. That Case 2 (
s(x-1)=1
) occurs infinitely often implies that
q\circ{u}
is slow-growing. In both Case 1 and Case 2, the definition of
Q
enforces that
q(x)\leq q(x+1)
for all
x\in\mathbb{N}
, so
q
and
q\circ{u}
are slow-growing order functions.

Step 3: Showing
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p_{1})\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{1}}(q)
.

We use basic bushy tree forcing. Let
\mathbb{P}
be the set of all pairs
\langle\sigma,B\rangle\in q^{\ast}\times\mathcal{P}(q^{\ast})
where
\sigma\neq\langle\rangle
and
B
is
k
-small above
\sigma
,
k
-closed, and upward closed for some
k\leq q(|\sigma|-1)
.
\langle\tau,C\rangle

extends

\langle\sigma,B\rangle
if
\sigma\subseteq\tau
and
B\subseteq C
.
For
i\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathcal{D}_{i}
denote the set of
\langle\sigma,B\rangle\in\mathbb{P}
such that for all
X\in\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket_{q}\setminus\left\llbracket B\right\rrbracket_{q}
,
\Gamma_{i}^{X}\notin\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p_{1})
.

Claim 1.

For each
m
,
\mathcal{T}_{m}=\{\langle\sigma,B\rangle\in\mathbb{P}\mid|\sigma|\geq m\}
is dense open in
\mathbb{P}
.

Proof.

\mathcal{T}_{m}
is clearly open in
\mathbb{P}
. To show that
\mathcal{T}_{m}
is dense in
\mathbb{P}
, let
C=\{\tau\in q^{\ast}\mid|\tau|\geq m\}
. For any string
\sigma
,
C
is
k
-big above
\sigma
if and only if
k\leq q(|\sigma|)
. In particular,
C
is
k
-big above
\sigma
for all
k\leq q(|\sigma|-1)
.

Suppose
\langle\sigma,B\rangle\in\mathbb{P}
; let
k\leq q(|\sigma|-1)
be such that
B
is
k
-small above
\sigma
and
k
-closed. If
|\sigma|\geq m
, then we are done. Otherwise, let
\tau
be any string in
C\setminus B
extending
\sigma
. Because
B
is
k
-closed and
\tau\notin B
,
B
is
k
-small above
\tau
. Then
\langle\tau,B\rangle
is an extension of
\langle\sigma,B\rangle
in
\mathcal{T}_{m}
.
∎

Claim 2.

If
\mathcal{G}
is any filter on
\mathbb{P}
, then for all
\langle\sigma,B\rangle\in\mathcal{G}
,
X_{\mathcal{G}}\coloneq\bigcup\{\tau\in q^{\ast}\mid\exists C\subseteq q^{\ast}(\langle\tau,C\rangle\in\mathcal{G})\}
has no initial segment in
B
.

Proof.

Suppose otherwise, so that there is
\tau\in B
which is an initial segment of
X_{\mathcal{G}}
. By the definition of
X_{\mathcal{G}}
, there must be a
\langle\rho^{\prime},C^{\prime}\rangle\in\mathcal{G}
such that
\rho^{\prime}
extends
\tau
. Let
\langle\rho,C\rangle
be a common extension of
\langle\rho^{\prime},C^{\prime}\rangle
and
\langle\sigma,B\rangle
. Because
B
is upward-closed,
\rho\in B
. But
B\subseteq C
, so
\rho\in C
and hence
C
is
k
-big above
\rho
for every
k
, a contradiction.
∎

Claim 3.

For all
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{D}_{i}
is dense open in
\mathbb{P}
.

Proof.

\mathcal{D}_{i}
is clearly open in
\mathbb{P}
, so it must remains to show that
\mathcal{D}_{i}
is dense in
\mathbb{P}
.

Suppose
\langle\sigma,B\rangle\in\mathbb{P}
; let
k\leq q(|\sigma|-1)
be such that
B
is
k
-small above
\sigma
. By potentially extending to an element of
\mathcal{T}_{m}
for an appropriate
m
, we can assume that
|\sigma|>i
and that in the computation of
q(|\sigma|)
, Case 2 occurs. Let
x=|\sigma|
and define

A=\{\tau\in q^{\ast}\mid\Gamma_{i}^{\tau}(\theta_{1}(\sigma,i)){\downarrow}<p_{1}(\theta_{1}(\sigma,i))\}.

There are two cases:

Case I.

Suppose
A
is
p_{1}(\theta_{1}(\sigma,i))\cdot q(x-1)
-small above
\sigma
. Let
c=p_{1}(\theta_{1}(\sigma,i))\cdot q(x-1)+k-1
, so
A\cup B
is
c
-small above
\sigma
by
VI.1.4
. Let
C
be the
c
-closure of
A\cup B
(note that although this
c
-closure is taken in
\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
,
A\cup B
being
c
-big above
\tau
implies
\tau\in q^{\ast}
, so
C\subseteq q^{\ast}
). By definition,
C
is
c
-small above
\sigma
,
c
-closed, and upward closed.

Since
q(x)=p(\theta_{1}(\sigma,i))\cdot q(x-1)+q(x-1)+2>c
and
q
is nondecreasing,
\{\tau\in q^{x+1}\mid\sigma\subseteq\tau\}
is
c
-big above
\sigma
. Thus, there is a string
\tau
in
q^{x+1}\setminus C
extending
\sigma
. Because
C
is
c
-closed, it is
c
-small above
\tau
, and hence
\langle\tau,C\rangle\in\mathbb{P}
(here we are also using the fact that
C
is upward closed). Because
A\subseteq C
,
\langle\tau,C\rangle\in\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\psi}
by virtue of
\Gamma^{g}_{i}
being partial for any
g\in\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket_{q}\setminus\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{q}
. Because
B\subseteq C
and
\sigma\subseteq\tau
,
\langle\tau,C\rangle
extends
\langle\sigma,B\rangle
.

Case II.

If
A
is
p_{1}(\theta_{1}(\sigma,i))\cdot q(x-1)
-big above
\sigma
, then
VI.1.8
(c) implies
\{\tau\in q^{\ast}\mid\Gamma^{\tau}_{i}(\theta_{1}(\sigma,i)){\downarrow}=k\}
is
q(x-1)
-big above
\sigma
for some
k<p_{1}(\theta_{1}(\sigma,i))
. This implies
\varphi_{\theta_{1}(\sigma,i)}(\theta_{1}(\sigma,i))
is defined. Let
\tau
be an extension of
\sigma
in
q^{\ast}\setminus B
such that
\Gamma_{i}^{\tau}(\theta_{1}(\sigma,i))=\varphi_{\theta_{1}(\sigma,i)}(\theta_{1}(\sigma,i))
. Then
\langle\tau,B\rangle
is an element of
\mathcal{D}_{i}
extending
\langle\sigma,B\rangle
.

∎

Let
B_{\mathrm{A\kern-0.63779ptvoid}^{\psi_{1}}(q)}
be the set of all strings in
q^{\ast}
which cannot be extended to an element of
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{1}}(q)
. Let
\sigma_{0}
be a string of length
1
such that
\sigma_{0}(0)\nsimeq\psi_{1}(0)
, if
\psi_{1}(0){\downarrow}
.
B_{\mathrm{A\kern-0.63779ptvoid}^{\psi_{1}}(q)}
is
2
-small above
\sigma_{0}
, upward closed, and
2
-closed, so
\langle\sigma_{0},B_{\mathrm{A\kern-0.63779ptvoid}^{\psi_{1}}(q)}\rangle\in\mathbb{P}
. Let
\mathcal{G}
be a filter containing
\langle\sigma_{0},B_{\mathrm{A\kern-0.63779ptvoid}^{\psi_{1}}(q)}\rangle
which meets
\mathcal{T}_{m}
and
\mathcal{D}_{i}
for all
m,i\in\mathbb{N}
. Claim 1 shows that
X_{\mathcal{G}}\in\prod{q}
, Claim 2 shows that
X_{\mathcal{G}}\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{1}}(q)
, and Claim 3 shows that
X_{\mathcal{G}}
computes no element of
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p_{1})
. In other words,

\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p_{1})\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{1}}(q).

Step 4: Showing
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q\circ{u})\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{2}}(p_{2})
.

We define a sequence
\langle\sigma_{i},B_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
such that:

1.

\sigma_{i}\in p_{2}^{n_{i}}\setminus B_{i}
.

2.

B_{i}\subseteq p_{2}^{\ast}
is
p_{2}(n_{i})
-small above
\sigma_{i}
.

3.

For all
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\sigma_{i}\subseteq\sigma_{i+1}
and
B_{i}\subseteq B_{i+1}
.

Let
B_{1}=B_{\mathrm{A\kern-0.63779ptvoid}^{\psi_{2}}(p_{2})}
and
\sigma_{1}
an arbitrary element of
p_{2}^{n_{1}}\setminus B_{1}
. (Note that
p_{2}(1)\geq 2
and
B_{1}
is
2
-small above
\sigma_{1}
, so in particular
p_{2}(1)
-small above
\sigma_{1}
.) Suppose
\sigma_{i},B_{i}
have been constructed. Let
k
be as in Case 2 of the construction of
Q

and let
\rho
be an extension of
\sigma_{i}
in
p_{2}^{k}\setminus B_{i}
(
k\geq n_{i}
, so
p_{2}^{k}
is
p_{2}(n_{i})
-big above
\sigma_{i}
). For
j<(q\circ{u})(\theta_{2}(\rho,i))
, let

A_{j}=\{\tau\in p_{2}^{\ast}\mid\Gamma_{i-1}^{\tau}(\theta_{2}(\rho,i)){\downarrow}=j\}.

We have two cases, depending upon whether
A_{j}
is
p_{2}(n_{i})
-big above
\rho
for some
j
or not.

Case 1.

If
A_{j}
is
p_{2}(n_{i})
-big above
\rho
for some
j
, then
\varphi_{\theta_{2}(\rho,i)}(\theta_{2}(\rho,i))
is defined. In that case, let
j^{\prime}=\varphi_{\theta_{2}(\rho,i)}(\theta_{2}(\rho,i))
, so there is a
\tau\in A_{j^{\prime}}\setminus B_{i}
extending
\rho
such that

\Gamma_{i-1}^{\tau}(\theta_{2}(\rho,i))=j^{\prime}=\varphi_{\theta_{2}(\rho,i)}(\theta_{2}(\rho,i)).

Let
B_{i+1}=B_{i}
and let
\sigma_{i+1}
be any extension of
\tau
in
p_{2}^{n_{i+1}}\setminus B_{i+1}
.

Case 2.

If
A_{j}
is
p_{2}(n_{i})
-small above
\rho
for all
j
, then
\bigcup_{j}{A_{j}}
is
(p_{2}(n_{i})\cdot(q\circ{u})(\theta_{2}(\rho,i))-(q\circ{u})(\theta_{2}(\rho,i))+1)
-small above
\rho
. Let

c=p_{2}(n_{i})\cdot((q\circ{u})(\theta_{2}(\rho,i))+1)-(q\circ{u})(\theta_{2}(\rho,i))

Then
C=\bigcup_{j}{A_{j}}\cup B_{i}
is
c
-small above
\rho
.

We claim that
(q\circ{u})(n_{i})=(q\circ{u})(\theta_{2}(\rho,i))
. First we note the inequality
k=|\rho|\leq\theta_{2}(\rho,i)
by the definition of
\theta_{2}
, so that
N_{i}<u(k)=u(|\rho|)\leq\theta_{2}(\rho,i)
. By the definition of
N_{i+1}
, we additionally have
\theta_{2}(\rho,i)<N_{i+1}
. The definition of
q
then gives
q(N_{i})=q({u}(\theta_{2}(\rho,i)))
, or equivalently
(q\circ{u})(n_{i})=(q\circ{u})(\theta_{2}(\rho,i))
.

As a result,

\displaystyle p_{2}(k)

\displaystyle\geq p_{2}(n_{i})\cdot((q\circ{u})(n_{i})+1)

\displaystyle=p_{2}(n_{i})\cdot((q\circ{u})(\theta_{2}(\rho,i))+1)

\displaystyle\geq c,

so that
C
is
p_{2}(k)
-small above
\rho
. Because
k\leq n_{i+1}
,
C
is also
p_{2}(n_{i+1})
-small above
\rho
. Finally, let
B_{i+1}=C
and let
\sigma_{i+1}
be any extension of
\rho
in
p_{2}^{n_{i+1}}\setminus B_{i+1}
.

This completes the construction of the sequence
\langle\sigma_{i},B_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
. Let
Y\coloneq\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\sigma_{i}}
. By construction,
Y\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{2}}(p_{2})
. Observe that within the above construction, falling into Case 1 at stage
i
implies
\Gamma_{i}^{Y}
is not a member of
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q)
. In other words,

\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q\circ{u})\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{2}}(p_{2}).

This completes the proof.

∎

VI.3
Avoidance of Well-Behaved Families of Universal Partial Recursive Functions

Now we turn to families of universal partial recursive functions, with the prototypical example being the family of linearly universal partial recursive functions.

Definition VI.3.1

(translation)
.

If
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
are universal partial recursive functions, then a
translation
from
\psi_{1}
to
\psi_{2}
is a total recursive function
u
such that
\psi_{1}\circ u=\psi_{2}
.

Definition VI.3.2

(translationally bounded)
.

Fix a universal partial recursive function
\psi_{0}
. We say that a family
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
of universal partial recursive functions is
translationally bounded
if there exists an order function
U\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every
\psi\in\mathcal{C}
there is a translation from
\psi
to
\psi_{0}
which is dominated by
U
.

Remark VI.3.3
.

Being translationally bounded from above does not depend on the choice of universal partial recursive function
\psi_{0}
: Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is translationally bounded with respect to
\psi_{0}
, witnessed by
U
, and
\psi_{1}
is another universal partial recursive function. Let
v
be a translation from
\psi_{1}
to
\psi_{0}
, and let
\overline{v}
be the order function defined by
\overline{v}(x)\coloneq\max_{i\leq x}{v(i)}
. Then
U\circ\overline{v}
witnesses
\mathcal{C}
being translationally bounded with respect to
\psi_{1}
.

Example VI.3.4
.

The family
\mathcal{LU}
of linearly universal partial recursive functions is translationally bounded, witnessed by the order function
U=\lambda n.n^{2}
. The same function also shows the family of the diagonals of linear admissible enumerations of the partial recursive functions is also translationally bounded.

Example VI.3.5
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is a set of total recursive functions such that there is a recursive function
U
dominating every member of
\mathcal{F}
. Temporarily say that a universal partial recursive function
\psi
is
\mathcal{F}
-universal
if for every partial recursive function
\theta\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:{\subseteq}\!}\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
there exists a
u\in\mathcal{F}
such that
\psi\circ u=\theta
. Then the family
\mathcal{C}
of all
\mathcal{F}
-universal partial recursive functions is translationally bounded, witnessed by
U
.

Particular examples include the set of linear functions, the set of recursive linearly bounded functions, or the set of primitive recursive functions.

Lemma VI.3.6
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a nonempty family of universal partial recursive functions which is translationally bounded, witnessed by the order function
U\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
. Then for all order functions
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
and all universal partial recursive functions
\psi_{0}
,
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{0}}(p\circ U)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\mathcal{C}}(p)
.

Proof.

Let
X
be an element of
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\mathcal{C}}(p)
, so that there is a
\psi\in\mathcal{C}
such that
X\in\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
. By hypothesis, there exists a total recursive function
u\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\psi\circ u=\psi_{0}
and which is dominated by
U
. Then
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{0}}(p\circ U)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{0}}(p\circ u)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p)
, so
X
computes a member of
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{0}}(p\circ U)
.
∎

Theorem VI.3.7
.

Suppose
p_{1}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
and
p_{2}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
are order functions,
u\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is a strictly increasing order function,
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
are nonempty families of universal partial recursive functions which are each translationally bounded, and
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
are partial recursive functions. Then there exists an order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
q\circ u
is slow-growing and for which

\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(p_{1})\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{1}}(q)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\mathcal{C}_{2}}(q\circ u)\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{2}}(p_{2}).

Proof.

Let
\psi
be the diagonal of an acceptable enumeration of the partial recursive functions. By hypothesis, there are order functions
U_{1}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
and
U_{2}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
witnessing the fact that
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
are translationally bounded, respectively. Applying
VI.2.3
to
p_{1}\circ U_{1}
,
p_{2}
, and
u\circ U_{2}
yields an order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
q\circ u\circ U_{2}
is slow-growing (and hence
q\circ u
as well),
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p_{1}\circ U_{1})\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{1}}(q)
, and
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q\circ u\circ U_{2})\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{2}}(p_{2})
. By
VI.3.6
,
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p_{1}\circ U_{1})\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(p_{1})
and
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q\circ u\circ U_{2})\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\mathcal{C}_{2}}(q\circ u)
, so
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p_{1}\circ U_{1})\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{1}}(q)
implies
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(p_{1})\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{1}}(q)
and
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q\circ u\circ U_{2})\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{2}}(p_{2})
implies
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\mathcal{C}_{2}}(q\circ u)\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{2}}(p_{2})
.
∎

VI.2.1
is then an easy consequence:

Proof of
VI.2.1
.

Let
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
be order functions,
u\coloneq\id_{\mathbb{N}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{1}=\mathcal{C}_{2}\coloneq\mathcal{LU}
, and
\psi_{1}=\psi_{2}\coloneq\psi
any linearly universal partial recursive function. By
VI.3.7
, there is a slow-growing order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p_{1})\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q)
and
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p_{2})
. Since
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q)\subseteq\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
and
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(p_{2})\subseteq\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p_{2})
, we find that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p_{1})\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p_{2})
.
∎

VI.4
Implications for
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}

VI.2.1
allows us to make several deductions concerning
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}
. The first is that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}
is not of deep degree:

Theorem VI.4.1
.

\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}
is not of deep degree.

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of a contradiction that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}
is weakly equivalent to a deep r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class
P
. We may assume without loss of generality that
P\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
. Because
P
is deep, taking the inverse of a modulus of depth for
P
gives us an order function
r\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathbf{M}(P{\upharpoonright}\,n)\leq 2^{-r(n)}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular,
\mathbf{M}(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\leq 2^{-r(n)}
for every
X\in P
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, or equivalently
\apc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\geq r(n)
for every
X\in P
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, i.e., every
X\in P
is strongly
r
-random. Thus, every
X\in P
is
r
-random, so
P\subseteq\mathrm{COMPLEX}(r)
and hence
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(r)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}P
.
III.1.1
shows there exists a fast-growing order function
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(r)
. Applying
VI.2.1
yields a slow-growing order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
and
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
are weakly incomparable. But
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\subseteq\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}
and hence

\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(r)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}P\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)

giving a contradiction.
∎

Likewise, we can show that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}\mathrel{\nequiv_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
for every slow-growing order function
q
:

Theorem VI.4.2
.

There is no order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
.

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of a contradiction that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
. By
VI.2.1
, there exists a slow-growing order function
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
and
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
are weakly incomparable. But
p
being slow-growing implies
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\geq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
, a contradiction.
∎

Similarly, we can show that
\mathrm{SC}\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}
.

Theorem VI.4.3
.

\mathrm{SC}\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}
.

Proof.

For each rational
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\delta)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{SC}(\delta)
. Since
\sqrt{n}\leq\delta\cdot n
for almost all
n
, it follows that
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.\sqrt{n})\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{SC}(\delta)
for all rational
\delta\in(0,1)
, so
\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.\sqrt{n})\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{SC}
. By
III.1.4
there exists a fast-growing order function
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.\sqrt{n})
.
VI.2.1
implies there is a slow-growing order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
and
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
are weakly incomparable. Thus, if
\mathrm{SC}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}
, then we would have

\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{COMPLEX}(\lambda n.\sqrt{n})\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{SC}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q),

yielding a contradiction.
∎

Corollary VI.4.4
.

There exists a slow-growing order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{SC}\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
.

VI.5
Replacing Slow-Growing with Depth

The proof of
II.2.2
produced admissible enumerations
\varphi_{\bullet}
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{\bullet}
such that
\mathrm{DNR}^{(1)}_{\lambda n.2^{n}}\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{DNR}^{(2)}_{\lambda x.x}
, where
\mathrm{DNR}^{(1)}
is
\mathrm{DNR}
defined with respect to
\varphi_{\bullet}
and
\mathrm{DNR}^{(2)}
is
\mathrm{DNR}
defined with respect to
\tilde{\varphi}_{\bullet}
. For this reason, the implications of
q
being ‘slow-growing’ on the weak degree of
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q)
are dependent on the choice of
\psi
. In the case where
\psi
is linearly universal partial recursive,
q
is slow-growing if and only if
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q)
is a deep r.b.
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class. With that motivation in mind, we strengthen
VI.2.2
, with the depth of
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{2}}(q\circ u)
replacing slow-growing.

Theorem VI.5.1
.

Suppose
p_{1}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
and
p_{2}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
are order functions,
u\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is a strictly increasing order function,
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
are universal partial recursive functions, and
\psi_{3}
and
\psi_{4}
are partial recursive functions. Then there exists a order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{2}}(q\circ u)
is of deep degree and

\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{1}}(p_{1})\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{3}}(q)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{2}}(q\circ u)\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{4}}(p_{2}).

Requiring that
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{2}}(q\circ u)
is deep is a stronger condition than simply requiring that
q\circ u
be slow-growing.

Proposition VI.5.2
.

Suppose
\psi
is a universal partial recursive function and
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
is an order function. If
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q)
is of deep degree, then
q
is slow-growing.

A benefit of working with linearly universal partial recursive functions rather than the diagonals of linear admissible enumerations is that if
\psi
is linearly universal and
\tilde{\psi}
is univeral, then we may take the translation
u
from
\psi
to
\tilde{\psi}
to be strictly increasing. Moreover,
II.4.20
showed that composition with a linear map does not affect whether an order function is fast-growing or slow-growing.

Proof of
VI.5.2
.

Suppose
\psi_{0}
is a linearly universal partial recursive function and
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q)
is of deep degree. There are
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\psi_{0}(ax+b)\simeq\psi(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{N}
, so let
u\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
be defined by
u(x)\coloneq ax+b
for
x\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
\psi_{0}\circ u=\psi
. Because
\psi
is universal,
a
must be nonzero (otherwise,
\psi
would either be constant or undefined everywhere depending on whether
\psi_{0}(b)
converges or diverges, respectively).
u
is hence a strictly increasing order function.

We extend
q
and
u
to real-valued functions
\overline{q}\colon{[0,\infty)}\to(1,\infty)
and
\overline{u}\colon{[0,\infty)}\to{[0,\infty)}
, respectively, by letting
\overline{q}
be linear between
\langle x,q(x)\rangle
and
\langle x+1,q(x+1)\rangle
for all
x\in\mathbb{N}
and likewise with
\overline{u}
. To be exact, we define

\displaystyle\overline{q}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneq(q(\lfloor x\rfloor+1)-q(\lfloor x\rfloor))(x-\lfloor x\rfloor)+q(\lfloor x\rfloor),

\displaystyle\overline{u}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneq(u(\lfloor x\rfloor+1)-u(\lfloor x\rfloor))(x-\lfloor x\rfloor)+u(\lfloor x\rfloor).

\overline{u}
is strictly increasing, so its inverse
\overline{u}^{-1}\colon[b,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is defined. Finally, define
\tilde{q}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
by

\tilde{q}(x)\coloneq\lfloor\overline{q}(\overline{u}^{-1}(x))\rfloor

for all
x\in\mathbb{N}
.
\tilde{q}\circ u
is dominated by
q
, so
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{0}}(\tilde{q})
.

II.4.19
implies
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{0}}(\tilde{q})
is of deep degree, so
II.4.9
implies
\tilde{q}
must be slow-growing.
\overline{u}^{-1}
is a linear map, so
\tilde{q}
being slow-growing implies
\tilde{q}\circ\overline{u}
is also slow-growing by
II.4.20
.
q
is dominated by
\tilde{q}\circ\overline{u}+1
, so
q
is slow-growing as well.
∎

Proof of
VI.5.1
.

Let
\psi_{0}
be a linearly universal partial recursive function, let
\varphi_{\bullet}
be the admissible enumeration corresponding to
\psi_{0}
as in
Section

II.2.2
, and let
\psi
be the diagonal of
\varphi_{\bullet}
. Define
\mathrm{DNR}
with respect to
\varphi_{\bullet}
(i.e., so that
\mathrm{DNR}=\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}
). Let
v\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
be a translation from
\psi_{2}
to
\psi
and let
\tilde{v}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
be a strictly increasing order function dominating
v
(e.g.,
\tilde{v}(x)\coloneq\max-{i\leq x}{v(i)}+x
for
x\in\mathbb{N}
).

VI.2.2
shows there is an order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
q\circ(u\circ\tilde{v})
is slow-growing,
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{1}}(p_{1})\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{3}}(q)
, and
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q\circ(u\circ\tilde{v}))\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{4}}(p_{2})
. Because
q\circ(u\circ\tilde{v})
is slow-growing and
\psi
is linearly universal, it follows that
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q\circ(u\circ\tilde{v}))
is deep. Using
II.4.16
and observing that

\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q\circ(u\circ\tilde{v}))\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q\circ(u\circ v))=\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{2}\circ v}((q\circ u)\circ v)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{2}}(q\circ u)

shows
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{2}}(q\circ u)
is deep.
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi}(q\circ(u\circ\tilde{v}))\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{4}}(p_{2})
implies
\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{2}}(q\circ u)\mathrel{\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}}\mathrm{A\kern-0.80002ptvoid}^{\psi_{4}}(p_{2})
, so we have found the desired
q
.
∎

VI.6
Open Problems

Although
VI.2.1
significantly expands our understanding of the relationships between the fast and slow-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchies as well as the structure of the slow-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchy itself, there remain many open problems concerning these two subjects.

For example,
VI.2.1
shows that to each order function
p
there is a
q
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
and
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
are weakly incomparable, and hence we must have
p\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{dom}}}q
and
q\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{dom}}}p
, but what more can be said?

Question VI.6.1
.

Given an order function
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
, what can be said about how the growth rates of the slow-growing order functions
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
for which
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
and
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
are weakly incomparable compare to the growth rate of
p
? In particular, for the
q
defined in the proof of
VI.2.1
alternates between staying constant and making large jumps, but can we quantify the lengths of those constant periods or the size of those jumps?

Although
VI.4.2
shows that there is no slow-growing order function
q
for which
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
, it does not eliminate the possibility that there exist slow-growing order functions
q
for which
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q))
is minimal among the weak degrees of the slow-growing
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}
hierarchy.

Question VI.6.2
.

Given a slow-growing order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
, does there exist a slow-growing order function
q^{+}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q^{+})\mathrel{<_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
? If so, can we take
q^{+}
so that
q\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}q^{+}
, and if that is true, can we quantify how much faster-growing
q^{+}
must be than
q
for
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q^{+})\mathrel{<_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
to hold?

A related question which would address
VI.6.2
if answered affirmative is the following:

Question VI.6.3
.

Given slow-growing order functions
p_{1}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
and
p_{2}\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
, is there a slow-growing order function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p_{1})\cup\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p_{2})
?

An affirmative answer to
VI.6.3
would provide an affirmative answer to the first half of
VI.6.2
thanks to
VI.2.1
. In
VI.6.3
, we cannot add the requirement that
q
dominate both
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
, as there exist slow-growing order functions
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
such that
\max\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
is fast-growing.

A positive answer to
VI.6.3
for
p_{1}=\id_{\mathbb{N}}
:

Proposition VI.6.4
.

Suppose
p
is a slow-growing order function. Then There exists a slow-growing order function
q
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\cup\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(\lambda n.n)
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
q\coloneq\max\{p,\id_{\mathbb{N}}\}
is slow-growing. Define
A\coloneq\{n\in\mathbb{N}\mid p(n)\leq n\}
. If
A
is finite, then
\id_{\mathbb{N}}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{dom}}}p
, so
\max\{p(n),n\}=p(n)
for almost all
n
, hence
q
is slow-growing. So suppose
A
is infinite. Given
n\in A
, let
m
be maximal such that
2^{m}\leq n
, so that
p(2^{m})\leq p(n)\leq n\leq 2^{m+1}
. Thus,
2^{m}\cdot\frac{1}{p(2^{m})}\geq\frac{1}{2}
. It follows that
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}{2^{m}\cdot\frac{1}{\max\{p(2^{m}),2^{m}\}}}=\infty
. By the Cauchy Condensation Test, this implies
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{q(n)^{-1}}=\infty
.
∎

Corollary VI.6.5
.

There exists a slow-growing order function
q
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\mathrel{<_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(\lambda n.n)
.

Proof.

By either
VI.2.1
or combining
[
16
, Theorem 3.11]
and
II.3.16
, there exists a slow-growing order function
p
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)
is weakly incomparable with
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(\lambda n.n)
. By
VI.6.4
, there is a slow-growing
q
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\cup\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(\lambda n.n)
, hence
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)\mathrel{<_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(\id_{\mathbb{N}})
.
∎

VI.4.3
suggests the following question:

Question VI.6.6
.

Can we give a natural and specific slow-growing function
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{SC}\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
?

Chapter VII
Structure of the Deep Region of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}

Two results stated in
Section

II.4
that gave some idea of the structure of the collection of deep degrees in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
were
II.4.19
, which showed that the collection forms a filter in
\langle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}\rangle
, and
II.4.22
, which shows that no difference random computes a member of any representative of a deep degree. The goal of this chapter is to examine the structure of the filter of deep degrees further. Our main goal is to prove the following main theorem.

Theorem VII.0.1
.

Define

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}\mid\text{$\mathbf{p}$ a deep degree}\}.

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}\mid\text{$\mathbf{p}=\inf\mathcal{C}$ for some $\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}$}\}.

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}\mid\forall P\in\mathbf{p}\forall X\in\mathsf{MLR}\,(\exists Y\in P\,(Y\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}X)\to(0^{\prime}\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}0^{\prime}))\}.

Then
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}
is a principal filter while
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}
and
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}
are nonprincipal filters. Consequently,
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\subset$}\hss\cr\kern 0.86108pt\raise-2.15277pt\hbox{\scalebox{0.7}{$\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\hbox{\scalebox{1.4}{$-$}}\hss\cr\kern 0.86108pt\raise-0.43057pt\hbox{\rotatebox{45.0}{$-$}}\crcr}}}$}}\crcr}}}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\subset$}\hss\cr\kern 0.86108pt\raise-2.15277pt\hbox{\scalebox{0.7}{$\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\hbox{\scalebox{1.4}{$-$}}\hss\cr\kern 0.86108pt\raise-0.43057pt\hbox{\rotatebox{45.0}{$-$}}\crcr}}}$}}\crcr}}}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}
.

In
Section

VII.1
, we show that the infimum of the collection of deep degrees
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)
lies in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
but is not a deep degree itself, showing the filter of deep degrees is nonprincipal.

Proposition
VII.1.1
.

The union
L
of all deep
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes is
\Sigma^{0}_{3}
. Consequently,
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

Theorem
VII.1.2
.

\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)
is not a deep degree in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

In
Section

VII.2
, we define the collection of
pseudo
-deep degrees in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
and characterize it as the principal filter generated by
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)
.

Theorem
VII.2.4
.

\{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}\mid\text{$\mathbf{p}$ pseudo-deep}\}
is equal to the principal filter generated by
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)
in
\langle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}},\leq\rangle
.

In
Section

VII.3
, we show that the filters of deep degrees and pseudo-deep degrees cannot be characterized by the property that no difference random computes a member of any representative of those degrees.

Theorem
VII.3.1
.

There exists a
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class
P
which is not of pseudo-deep degree but for which no difference random computes an element of
P
.

VII.1
The infimum of all deep degrees

An important observation about the collection of deep degrees is that its infimum is in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

Proposition VII.1.1
.

The union
L
of all deep
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes is
\Sigma^{0}_{3}
. Consequently,
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{M}
be a fixed universal left r.e. continuous semimeasure on
\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
, and let the map
\langle s,\sigma\rangle\mapsto\mathbf{M}_{s}(\sigma)
realize the left recursive enumerability of
\mathbf{M}
, i.e., it is a recursive function
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{Q}
such that
\langle\mathbf{M}_{s}(\sigma)\rangle_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
converges monotonically to
\mathbf{M}(\sigma)
from below for each
\sigma\in\mathbb{N}
. Given
e,s\in\mathbb{N}
, let
P_{e,s}\coloneq\{X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\varphi_{e,s}^{X{\upharpoonright}\,s}(0){\uparrow}\}
;
\langle P_{e,s}\rangle_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of uniformly recursive subsets whose intersection is
P_{e}
.

Claim 1.

The predicate
\{\langle e,m,q\rangle\mid\mathbf{M}(P_{e}{\upharpoonright}\,m)\leq q\}
is a
\Pi^{0}_{2}
subset of
\mathbb{N}^{2}\times\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
.

Proof.

The predicate
\{\langle e,m,q,s\rangle\mid\mathbf{M}_{s}(P_{e,s}{\upharpoonright}\,m)\leq q\}
is recursive, so it suffices to show that

\mathbf{M}(P_{e}{\upharpoonright}\,m)\leq q\iff\forall t\exists s\,(t<s\wedge\mathbf{M}_{s}(P_{e,s}{\upharpoonright}\,m)\leq q).

This follows essentially from the observation that the sequence
\langle\mathbf{M}_{s}(P_{e,s}{\upharpoonright}\,m)\rangle_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
is eventually nondecreasing for all
e,m\in\mathbb{N}
.

In the forward direction, suppose
\mathbf{M}(P_{e}{\upharpoonright}\,m)\leq q
.
P_{e}{\upharpoonright}\,m
is a finite set equal to
\bigcap_{s\in\mathbb{N}}{P_{e,s}}
, so there exists an
t\in\mathbb{N}
such that
P_{e,s}{\upharpoonright}\,m=P_{e}{\upharpoonright}\,m
for all
s>t
. Thus, there are arbitrarily large
s
such that
\mathbf{M}_{s}(P_{e,s}{\upharpoonright}\,m)\leq\mathbf{M}_{s}(P_{e}{\upharpoonright}\,m)\leq q
.

In the opposite direction, assume
\forall t\exists s\,(t<s\wedge\mathbf{M}_{s}(P_{e,s}{\upharpoonright}\,m)\leq q)
and suppose for the sake of a contradiction that
\mathbf{M}(P_{e}{\upharpoonright}\,m)>q
. Let
t
be large enough so that
P_{e,s}{\upharpoonright}\,m=P_{e}{\upharpoonright}\,m
and
\mathbf{M}_{s}(P_{e}{\upharpoonright}\,m)>q
for all
s>t
. But then for all
s>t
we have
\mathbf{M}_{s}(P_{e,s}{\upharpoonright}\,m)>q
, contradicting our assumption. Thus,
\mathbf{M}(P_{e}{\upharpoonright}\,m)\leq q
is a
\Pi^{0}_{2}
subset of
\mathbb{N}^{2}\times\mathbb{Q}
.
∎

Claim 2.

The predicate
\{i\in\mathbb{N}\mid\text{$\varphi_{i}$ is total}\}
is a
\Pi^{0}_{2}
subset of
\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

\varphi_{i}
being total is equivalent to
\forall n\exists s\,\varphi_{i,s}(n){\downarrow}
.
∎

Claim 3.

The predicate
\{e\in\mathbb{N}\mid\text{$P_{e}$ is deep}\}
is a
\Sigma^{0}_{3}
subset of
\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

P_{e}
is deep if and only if
\exists i((\text{$\varphi_{i}$ is total})\wedge\forall n(\mathbf{M}(P_{e}{\upharpoonright}\,\varphi_{i}(n))\leq 2^{-n}))
. By Claim 1, this is
\Sigma^{0}_{3}
.
∎

Finally,

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\bigcup\{P_{e}\mid\text{$P_{e}$ is deep}\}

\displaystyle=\{X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\exists e((\text{$P_{e}$ is deep})\wedge X\in P_{e})\}

shows
L
is
\Sigma^{0}_{3}
. Since
L
contains a nonempty
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class, the
Proposition I.4.17

(Embedding Lemma)
.
implies
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.
∎

That
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}
(
VI.4.1
) is not of deep degree shows that
L
is not of deep degree, further clarifying the structure of the filter of deep degrees in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
:

Theorem VII.1.2
.

\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)
is not a deep degree in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of a contradiction that
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)
is a deep degree in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)

\displaystyle=\inf\{\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P)\mid\text{$P\subseteq\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}$ is nonempty, deep}\}

\displaystyle\leq\inf\{\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p))\mid\text{$p$ slow-growing order function}\}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}).

Because
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)
is a deep degree in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
,
II.4.19
shows that
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}})
is a deep degree in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
, contradicting
VI.4.1
.
∎

Corollary VII.1.3
.

The filter of deep degrees in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
is nonprincipal.

Corollary VII.1.4
.

For any deep degree
\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
, there exists a deep degree
\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
such that
\mathbf{q}<\mathbf{p}
.

VII.2
The Filter of Pseudo-Deep Degrees

Motivated by
L
,
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}
, and
\mathrm{SC}
, we define:

Definition VII.2.1

(pseudo-deep degree in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
)
.

A weak degree
\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
is a
pseudo-deep degree (in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
)
if
\mathbf{p}
is an infimum of deep degrees in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
, or equivalently that there is a collection
\mathcal{C}
of deep
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes such that
\mathbf{p}=\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\bigcup{\mathcal{C}})
.

P\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
is
of pseudo-deep degree
if
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P)
is a pseudo-deep degree in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

II.4.22
continues to hold for
P\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
of pseudo-deep degree.

Proposition VII.2.2
.

Suppose
P\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
is of pseudo-deep degree. If
X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is difference random, then
X
computes no member of
P
.

Proof.

Because
P
is of pseudo-deep degree, there is a collection
\mathcal{C}
of deep
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes such that
P\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}
. If
Y\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}X
for some
Y\in P
, then the fact that
P\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}
implies there is a
Q\in\mathcal{C}
and a
Z\in Q
such that
Z\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}Y\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}X
, contradicting
II.4.11
.
∎

Notation VII.2.3
.

Let

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}\mid\text{$\mathbf{p}$ is a deep degree}\},

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}\mid\text{$\mathbf{p}$ is a pseudo-deep degree}\}.

Just as
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}
is a filter,
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}
also forms a filter – in fact, it is the principal filter generated by
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)
.

Theorem VII.2.4
.

\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}
is equal to the principal filter generated by
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)
in
\langle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}},\leq\rangle
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbf{p}
is a pseudo-deep degree in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
. Let
\mathcal{C}
be a collection of deep
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes such that
\mathbf{p}=\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\bigcup{\mathcal{C}})
. Then
L\supseteq\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}
, so
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)\leq\mathbf{p}
. This shows that every pseudo-deep degree in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
lies in the filter generated by
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)
in
\langle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}},\leq\rangle
.

Conversely, suppose
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)\leq\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
. Let
P
be a
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class for which
\mathbf{p}=\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P)
.
(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{w}},\leq)
is completely distributive (
I.4.13
(f)), so

\displaystyle\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P)

\displaystyle=\sup\{\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P),\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)\}

\displaystyle=\sup\{\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P),\inf\{\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P_{e})\mid\text{$P_{e}$ is deep}\}\}

\displaystyle=\inf\{\sup\{\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P),\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P_{e})\}\mid\text{$P_{e}$ is deep}\}

\displaystyle=\inf\{\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P\times P_{e})\mid\text{$P_{e}$ is deep}\}.

Thus,
\mathbf{p}
is pseudo-deep.
∎

Corollary VII.2.5
.

There is no minimal element of
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\setminus\{\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)\}
..

Proof.

By
VII.2.4
, if
\mathbf{p}
is a pseudo-deep degree distinct from
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)
then
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)\mathrel{<_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathbf{p}
. The Density Theorem for
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}

[
4
, Theorem 2]
shows that there exists
\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
such that
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)\mathrel{<_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathbf{q}\mathrel{<_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathbf{p}
. A second application of
VII.2.4
shows
\mathbf{q}
is a pseudo-deep degree, and hence
\mathbf{p}
is not a minimal element of
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\setminus\{\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)\}
.
∎

VII.3
The Filter of Deep Degrees in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
and Difference Randoms

VII.2.2
shows that no difference random computes a member of any
P\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
of pseudo-deep degree. However, we can show that this does not characterize the pseudo-deep degrees.

Theorem VII.3.1
.

There exists a
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class
P
which is not of pseudo-deep degree but for which no difference random computes an element of
P
.

Notation VII.3.2
.

Let
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}
be the collection of all weak degrees
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P)
in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
such that no difference random computes a member of
P
.

Proposition VII.3.3
.

\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}
is a filter.

Proof.

Given
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
, let
P
and
Q
be
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes such that
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P)=\mathbf{p}
and
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(Q)=\mathbf{q}
.

Suppose
\mathbf{p}\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}
and
\mathbf{p}\leq\mathbf{q}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
. If
Y\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}X
for some
Y\in Q
, then
\mathbf{p}\leq\mathbf{q}
implies
Z\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}Y
for some
Z\in P
, from which we find
Z\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}X
, a contradiction. Thus,
\mathbf{q}\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}
.

Now suppose
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}
.
\inf\{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\}=\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P\cup Q)
. As no difference random computes any member of
P
or
Q
, no difference random computes any member of
P\cup Q
, i.e.,
\inf\{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\}\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}
.
∎

We can show that
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\subset$}\hss\cr\kern 0.86108pt\raise-2.15277pt\hbox{\scalebox{0.7}{$\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\hbox{\scalebox{1.4}{$-$}}\hss\cr\kern 0.86108pt\raise-0.43057pt\hbox{\rotatebox{45.0}{$-$}}\crcr}}}$}}\crcr}}}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}
by showing that
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}
is non-principal.

Theorem VII.3.4
.

\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}
is non-principal.

To prove
VII.3.4
we make use of the notion of
\pfc
-triviality.

Definition VII.3.5

(
\pfc
-trivial)
.

X\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is
\pfc
-trivial
if there exists
c\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\pfc(X{\upharpoonright}\,n)\leq\pfc(n)+c
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof of
VII.3.4
.

Suppose
\mathbf{q}\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}
, and let
Q
be a
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class such that
\mathbf{q}=\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(Q)
. By
[
3
, Lemma 2]
, there exist r.e. sets
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that
0\mathrel{<_{\mathrm{T}}}A,B\mathrel{<_{\mathrm{T}}}0^{\prime}
,
A\cap B=\emptyset
and
A\cup B=0^{\prime}
, and for which neither
A
nor
B
compute any member of
Q
. Note that
A\oplus B\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{T}}}0^{\prime}
.

II.4.23
implies
\{A\}
and
\{B\}
are
\Pi^{0}_{2}
, so that
Q\cup\{A\}
and
Q\cup\{B\}
are each
\Pi^{0}_{2}
. Both sets contain a nonempty
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class (namely,
Q
), so the
Proposition I.4.17

(Embedding Lemma)
.
implies there are
\Pi^{0}_{1}
classes
P_{A}
and
P_{B}
such that
P_{A}\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}Q\cup\{A\}
and
P_{B}\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}Q\cup\{B\}
. Because neither
A
nor
B
compute any member of
Q
, we have
P_{A}\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}Q\cup\{A\}\mathrel{<_{\mathrm{w}}}Q
and
P_{B}\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}Q\cup\{B\}\mathrel{<_{\mathrm{w}}}Q
.

By
[
6
, Theorem 11.6.2]
, if
A
and
B
are both
\pfc
-trivial, then
A\oplus B\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{T}}}0^{\prime}
is
\pfc
-trivial, which is a contradiction. Thus, at least one of
A
and
B
are not
\pfc
-trivial. Without loss of generality, say that
A
is not
\pfc
-trivial, and let
P=P_{A}
.

Suppose
X
is a difference random.
VII.2.2
shows that
X
computes no member of
Q
, and if
A\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{T}}}X
then
[
11
, Corollary 3.6]
implies
A
is
\pfc
-trivial, contrary to hypothesis. Thus,
X
computes no member of
Q\cup\{A\}
, and hence computes no member of
P
, showing
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(P)\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}
. As
\mathbf{q}
was an arbitrary member of
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}
, it follows that
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}
is non-principal.
∎

Proof of
VII.3.1
.

VII.3.3
and
VII.3.4
show that
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}
is a nonprincipal filter. By
VII.2.2
,
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}\subseteq\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}
, but
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}
being principal means this inclusion must be proper.
∎

All these facts add up to imply
VII.0.1
:

Proof of
VII.0.1
.

VII.1.3
and
VII.3.4
shows that the outer two are nonprincipal, while
VII.2.4
shows the middle is. This implies that the inclusions
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}\subseteq\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\subseteq\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{diff}}
must be proper.
∎

VII.4
Open questions about the filter of pseudo-deep degrees

VII.2.4
and
VII.2.5
give important structural information about the filter of pseudo-deep degrees. However, there remain open questions about that structure, especially
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\setminus\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}
.

Question VII.4.1
.

What is the cardinality of
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\setminus\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}
? I.e., how many pseudo-deep degrees are there which aren’t deep degrees?

VII.2.5
puts constraints on
|\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\setminus\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}|
.

Proposition VII.4.2
.

|\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\setminus\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}|\in\{1,\aleph_{0}\}
.

Proof.

Because
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\setminus\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}
, we know
1\leq|\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\setminus\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}|
.
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{w}}
is countable, so
|\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\setminus\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}|\leq\aleph_{0}
.

If
1<|\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\setminus\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}|<\aleph_{0}
, then
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\setminus(\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}\cup\{\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)\})
has a minimal element, and such a minimal element is a minimal element of
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\setminus\{\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)\}
since
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}
is upward-closed, contradicting
VII.2.5
.
∎

Currently, the only two pseudo-deep degrees known to not be deep are
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)
and
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}})
, though they are not known to be distinct.

Question VII.4.3
.

Are
L
and
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}
weakly equivalent?

Something slightly stronger than asking whether
L\mathrel{\equiv_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}
is the following.

Question VII.4.4
.

Given a deep
\Pi^{0}_{1}
class
P
, does there exist a slow-growing order function
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}P
?

Proposition VII.4.5
.

1.

An affirmative answer to
VII.4.4
gives an affirmative answer to
VII.4.3
.

2.

An affirmative answer to
VII.4.4
gives an affirmative answer to
VI.6.3
, i.e., for all slow-growing order functions
p\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
and
q\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
there exists a slow-growing order function
r\colon\mathbb{N}\to(1,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(r)\mathrel{\leq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(p)\cup\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}(q)
.

3.

An answer to
VII.4.1
of ‘
1
’ gives an affirmative answer to
VII.4.3
.

An answer to
VII.4.1
of ‘
\aleph_{0}
’ suggests further structural questions about antichains in
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\setminus\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}
and related properties.

Question VII.4.6
.

1.

Do there exist weakly incomparable elements of
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\setminus\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}
?

2.

Do there exist infinitely many pairwise weakly incomparable elements of
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\setminus\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}
?

3.

Is
\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)
meet-irreducible? I.e., are there no pseudo-deep degrees
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}
such that
\inf\{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\}=\mathop{\deg_{\mathrm{w}}}(L)
?

In contrast, if
|\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\setminus\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}|=\aleph_{0}
, then
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\setminus\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}
contains infinite chains.

One possible approach to answering
VII.4.1
would be by showing that
\mathrm{SC}
is not of deep degree, as it is of pseudo-deep degree and we know that
\mathrm{SC}\mathrel{\nleq_{\mathrm{w}}}\mathrm{LU\kern-0.50003ptA}_{\mathrm{slow}}
by
VI.4.3
.

We observe that the known lattice theoretic properties available are not enough to determine the structure of
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}\setminus\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}
.

Remark VII.4.7
.

Some lattices
\langle P,\leq\rangle
and filters
\mathscr{F}_{1}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\raise 1.29167pt\hbox{$\subset$}\hss\cr\kern 0.86108pt\raise-2.15277pt\hbox{\scalebox{0.7}{$\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hss\hbox{\scalebox{1.4}{$-$}}\hss\cr\kern 0.86108pt\raise-0.43057pt\hbox{\rotatebox{45.0}{$-$}}\crcr}}}$}}\crcr}}}\mathscr{F}_{2}\subseteq P
which give some idea of how the boundary between
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{pseudo}}
and
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{deep}}
might look include the following:

•

Consider the lattice
\langle P,\leq\rangle\coloneq\langle\{S\subseteq[0,1]\mid|S|\leq\aleph_{0}\}\cup\{[0,1]\},\supseteq\rangle
and the filters
\mathscr{F}_{1}\coloneq\{S\subseteq[0,1]\mid|S|<\aleph_{0}\}
and
\mathscr{F}_{2}=P
. In this case,
|\mathscr{F}_{2}\setminus\mathscr{F}_{1}|=\aleph_{0}
and the minimum of
\mathscr{F}_{2}
,
[0,1]
, is meet-irreducible.

•

Consider the lattice
\langle P,\leq\rangle\coloneq\langle\{S\subseteq\mathbb{N}\mid|S|<\aleph_{0}\vee|\mathbb{N}\setminus S|<\aleph_{0}\},\supseteq\rangle
and the filters
\mathscr{F}_{1}\coloneq\{S\subseteq\mathbb{N}\mid|S|<\aleph_{0}\}
and
\mathscr{F}_{2}=P
. In this case,
|\mathscr{F}_{2}\setminus\mathscr{F}_{1}|=\aleph_{0}
and the minimum of
\mathscr{F}_{2}
,
\mathbb{N}
, is not meet-irreducible.
